## XXIII A Charitable Institution / 二十三 一所慈善机构

===Original===
When Ralph got back to Miss Nancy Sawyer’s, Shocky was sitting up in bed talking to Miss Nancy and Miss Semantha. His cheeks were a little flushed with fever and the excitement of telling his story; theirs were wet with tears. “Ralph,” whispered Miss Nancy, as she drew him into the kitchen, “I want you to get a buggy or a sleigh, and go right over to the poorhouse and fetch that boy’s mother over here. It’ll do me more good than any sermon I ever heard to see that boy in his mother’s arms tomorrow. We can keep the old lady over Sunday.”

Ralph was delighted, so delighted that he came near kissing good Miss Nancy Sawyer, whose plain face was glorified by her generosity.

But he did not go to the poorhouse immediately. He waited until he saw Bill Jones, the Superintendent of the Poorhouse, and Pete Jones, the County Commissioner, who was still somewhat shuck up, ride up to the courthouse. Then he drove out of the village, and presently hitched his horse to the poorhouse fence, and took a survey of the outside. Forty hogs, nearly ready for slaughter, wallowed in a pen in front of the forlorn and dilapidated house; for though the commissioners allowed a claim for repairs at every meeting, the repairs were never made, and it would not do to scrutinize Mr. Jones’s bills too closely, unless you gave up all hope of renomination to office. One curious effect of political aspirations in Hoopole County, was to shut the eyes that they could not see, to close the ears that they could not hear, and to destroy the sense of smell. But Ralph, not being a politician, smelled the hog-pen without and the stench within, and saw everywhere the transparent fraud, and heard the echo of Jones’s cruelty.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫回到南希·索耶小姐家，肖基正靠坐在床上，跟南希小姐和塞曼莎小姐说话。他的两颊叫烧热和讲故事的兴奋熏得微红；她们两家的脸颊，都叫泪水打湿了。「拉尔夫，」南希小姐把他拉进厨房，悄悄说，「我要你去雇一辆单马马车，要不一副雪橇，这就上济贫院，把那孩子的娘接过来。明天见那孩子躺进他娘怀里，比我这辈子听过的哪篇布道都养人。老太太嘛，咱们留她过个礼拜天。」

拉尔夫高兴极了，高兴得差点亲了亲好心的南希·索耶小姐——她那张平平常常的脸，叫这番慷慨照得容光焕发。

可他没有立刻上济贫院去。他一直等到看见济贫院监正比尔·琼斯和县委员皮特·琼斯——后一位还有些惊魂未定——骑马往法院去了，才驾车出村，不多时把马拴在济贫院栅栏上，先把外头打量了一遍。四十口快到出栏宰杀的肥猪，在那座破败凄凉的房子前头的猪圈里打滚；因为虽说委员们每回开会都准一笔修缮的账，修缮却从来不见做，而琼斯先生的账单是不便细看的——除非你把再获提名的指望断得干干净净。在胡普尔县，政治抱负有一桩奇效：叫人闭上眼，好看不见；堵上耳，好听不见；末了把鼻子也废了。拉尔夫不是政客，便闻见了外头的猪圈和屋里的恶臭，看穿了处处明摆着的欺冒，也听见了琼斯残酷行径的回声。

===Original===
A weak-eyed girl admitted him, and as he did not wish to make his business known at once, he affected a sort of idle interest in the place, and asked to be allowed to look round. The weak-eyed girl watched him. He found that all the women with children, twenty persons in all, were obliged to sleep in one room, which, owing to the hill-slope, was partly under ground, and which had but half a window for light, and no ventilation, except the chance draft from the door. Jones had declared that the women with children must stay there—“he warn’t goin’ to have brats a-runnin’ over the whole house.” Here were vicious women and good women, with their children, crowded like chickens in a coop for market. And there were, as usual in such places, helpless, idiotic women with illegitimate children. Of course this room was the scene of perpetual quarreling and occasional fighting.

In the quarters devoted to the insane, people slightly demented and raving maniacs were in the same rooms, while there were also those utter wrecks which sat in heaps on the floor, mumbling and muttering unintelligible words, the whole current of their thoughts hopelessly muddled, turning around upon itself in eddies never ending.

===Chinese===
一个眼睛怯怯的姑娘放他进去。他不想一上来就亮明来意，便装出一副对这地方闲闲好奇的样子，求准他四处看看。怯眼姑娘盯着他。他发现，凡是带孩子的女人，拢共二十口，全挤在一间屋里睡。那屋子因为顺着山坡，半截在地下，采光只有半扇窗，通风也只剩门口偶尔的一线穿堂风。琼斯放出过话，带孩子的女人就得待在那儿——「俺可不能叫一窝崽子满屋乱窜」。这里的坏女人和好女人，各带着各的孩子，像待售的鸡挤在贩子的笼里。这里也有那种此类地方照例少不了的、无依无靠又痴又傻的女人，带着私生的孩子。这间屋子，自然成了没完没了的吵骂、间或一场厮打的戏台。

在收疯人的那几间里，微微失神的和狂呼乱喊的疯子同屋共处；还有那等彻底垮掉的人，成堆瘫坐在地上，嘟嘟囔囔念着谁也听不懂的话，一脑子思路搅成了理不清的乱麻，围着自身打旋，永无了局。

===Original===
“That air woman,” said the weak-eyed girl, “used to holler a heap when she was brought in here. But Pap knows how to subjue ’em. He slapped her in the mouth every time she hollered. She don’t make no furss now, but jist sets down that way all day, and keeps a-whisperin’.”

Ralph understood it. When she came in she was the victim of mania; but she had been beaten into hopeless idiocy. Indeed this state of incurable imbecility seemed the end toward which all traveled. Shut in these bare rooms, with no treatment, no exercise, no variety, and meager food, cases of slight derangement soon grew into chronic lunacy.

One young woman, called Phil, a sweet-faced person, apparently a farmer’s wife, came up to Ralph and looked at him kindly, playing with the buttons on his coat in a childlike simplicity. Her blue-drilling dress was sewed all over with patches of white, representing ornamental buttons. The womanly instinct toward adornment had in her taken this childish turn.

===Chinese===
「那个婆娘，」怯眼姑娘说，「刚弄进来那阵儿，嚎得可凶。可俺爹有法子治服她们。她一嚎，俺爹就照嘴抽。这会儿她不闹腾了，成天就那么坐着，光小声念叨。」

拉尔夫明白了。她进来时，是躁狂症的受害人；后来，是生生被打成了不可救药的白痴。实在说，这种无药可救的痴傻，像是所有住进来的人共同的归宿。关在这些四壁精光的屋里，没有医治，没有活动，没有半点变化，入口又是菲薄的一口，轻度的失神很快便养成慢性的癫狂。

有一个年轻女人，大家叫她菲尔，生一张甜甜的脸，看样子是个庄户人的媳妇。她走到拉尔夫跟前，和和气气地望着他，带着孩子气的天真，摆弄他外衣上的扣子。她那身蓝粗布衣裳上缝满了白布片，一片一片，都剪成装饰扣子的模样。女人爱打扮的天性，在她身上转了这么一条孩子气的岔道。

===Original===
“Don’t you think they ought to let me go home?” she said with a sweetness and a wistful, longing, homesick look, that touched Ralph to the heart. He looked at her, and then at the muttering crones, and he could see no hope of any better fate for her. She followed him round the barn-like rooms, returning every now and then to her question. “Don’t you think I might go home now?”

The weak-eyed girl had been called away for a moment, and Ralph stood looking into a cell, where there was a man with a gay red plume in his hat and a strip of red flannel about his waist. He strutted up and down like a drill-sergeant.

“I am General Andrew Jackson,” he began. “People don’t believe it, but I am. I had my head shot off at Bueny Visty, and the new one that growed on isn’t nigh so good as the old one; it’s tater on one side.23 That’s why they take advantage of me to shut me up. But I know some things. My head is tater on one side, but it’s all right on t’other. And when I know a thing in the left side of my head, I know it. Lean down here. Let me tell you something out of the left side. Not out of the tater side, mind ye. I wouldn’t a told you if he hadn’t locked me up fer nothing. *Bill Jones is a thief!* He sells the bodies of the dead paupers, and then sells the empty coffins back to the county agin. But that a’n’t all—”

Just then the weak-eyed girl came back, and, as Ralph moved away, General Jackson called out: “That a’n’t all. I’ll tell the rest another time. And that a’n’t out of the tater side, you can depend on that. That’s out of the left side. Sound as a nut on that side!”

But Ralph began to wonder where he should find Hannah’s mother.

===Chinese===
「你说，他们不是该放俺回家了么？」她说得那么甜，眼里一副渴盼的、想家的神色，看得拉尔夫心尖发酸。他望望她，又望望那些念念叨叨的老妪，看不出她还有什么更好的命等着。她跟着他在谷仓似的大屋一间间转，转一阵就回来问一遍：「你说，俺这会儿不是可以回家了么？」

怯眼姑娘一时被人叫走了，拉尔夫站在一间号房前往里瞧：里头有个人，帽子上插一撮鲜亮的红翎毛，腰里缠一条红法兰绒。他操练军士似的，来回踱着正步。

「俺是安德鲁·杰克逊将军。」他开了腔。「人家不信，俺可真是。俺这颗脑袋在布埃纳维斯塔叫人打掉了，新长出来的这颗，比旧的那颗差得远：有一边是土豆做的。23所以人家趁这个由头把俺关起来。可俺知道一些事。俺的脑袋一边是土豆，另一边好好的。俺左半边脑袋里认下的事，那就是认下了。俯下身来。俺从左边告诉你件事。可别是土豆那边，你记着。要不是他平白无故把俺关起来，俺才不告诉你呢。*比尔·琼斯是个贼！*他把死了的穷人的尸首卖钱，卖完再把空棺材倒手卖回给县里。可还不光是这一桩——」

正在这时怯眼姑娘回来了，拉尔夫走开时，杰克逊将军在身后喊：「不光是这一桩。剩下的俺改天再讲。刚才那句可不是从土豆那边出来的，这个你只管放心。那是从左边出来的。俺那半边，结实得跟榛子似的！」

可拉尔夫开始犯嘀咕：汉娜的娘，上哪儿去找呢。

===Original===
“Don’t go in there,” cried the weak-eyed girl, as Ralph was opening a door. “Ole Mowley’s in there, and she’ll cuss you.”

“Oh! well, if that’s all, her curses won’t hurt,” said Hartsook, pushing open the door. But the volley of blasphemy and vile language that he received made him stagger. The old hag paced the floor, abusing everybody that came in her way. And by the window, in the same room, feeling the light that struggled through the dusty glass upon her face, sat a sorrowful, intelligent Englishwoman. Ralph noticed at once that she was English, and in a few moments he discovered that her sight was defective. Could it be that Hannah’s mother was the roommate of this loathsome creature, whose profanity and obscenity did not intermit for a moment?

Happily the weak-eyed girl had not dared to brave the curses of Mowley. Ralph stepped forward to the woman by the window, and greeted her.

===Chinese===
「别上那间屋里去，」拉尔夫正推一扇门，怯眼姑娘喊道。「老莫利在里头，她要咒你。」

「哦！就这个呀，她咒咒不疼。」哈茨克说着，把门推开了。可劈头浇来的那一阵渎神咒骂、污言秽语，打得他一个趔趄。老妖婆在屋里来回踱，见谁骂谁。而在同一间屋的窗边，坐着一位满面愁容、眉目聪慧的英国妇人，正让从积尘的玻璃里挣扎进来的那点光，落在自己脸上。拉尔夫一眼看出她是英国人，不多时又发觉她眼神不济。难道汉娜的娘，竟同这个可憎的东西同住一室？那家伙的咒骂和脏话，是一刻也不肯停的。

幸而怯眼姑娘没敢去冒莫利的骂，不曾走近。拉尔夫走向窗边那位妇人，向她问了好。

===Original===
“Is this Mrs. Thomson?”

“That is my name, sir,” she said, turning her face toward Ralph, who could not but remark the contrast between the thorough refinement of her manner and her coarse, scant, unshaped pauper-frock of blue drilling.

“I saw your daughter yesterday.”

“Did you see my boy?”

===Chinese===
「这位是汤姆森太太么？」

「我是姓这个姓，先生。」她说着，把脸转向拉尔夫。拉尔夫不能不留意那鲜明的对照：她举止里透出的十足教养，和她身上那件粗劣、短小、不成样子的蓝粗布济贫院囚衣。

「我昨天见到了您的女儿。」

「你见到我的孩子了吗？」

===Original===
There was a tremulousness in her voice and an agitation in her manner which disclosed the emotion she strove in vain to conceal. For only the day before Bill Jones had informed her that Shocky would be bound out on Saturday, and that she would find that goin’ agin him warn’t a payin’ business, so much as some others he mout mention.

Ralph told her about Shocky’s safety. I shall not write down the conversation here. Critics would say that it was an overwrought scene. As if all the world were as cold as they! All I can tell is that this refined woman had all she could do to control herself in her eagerness to get out of her prison-house, away from the blasphemies of Mowley, away from the insults of Jones, away from the sights and sounds and smells of the place, and, above all, her eagerness to fly to the little shocky-head from whom she had been banished for two years. It seemed to her that she could gladly die now, if she could die with that flaxen head upon her bosom.

===Chinese===
她的声音发颤，举止失措，露出她怎么也压不住的心绪。原来就在头一天，比尔·琼斯才通知她，肖基礼拜六就要立契给人抱养，还捎带叫她明白：跟他过不去，可不是什么来钱的营生，比不得他兴许可以点出来的另外几桩。

拉尔夫把肖基平安的消息讲给她听。这场对话，我不照录在这里了。批评家们准会说它写得过火。仿佛全世界都跟他们一样冷血似的！我只知道，这位教养有素的妇人尽了全部力气才按捺住自己：她是那样急切地要逃出这座牢狱，躲开莫利的渎神咒骂，躲开琼斯的侮辱，躲开这地方的所见、所闻、所嗅；尤其急切的，是要飞到那个乱蓬蓬小脑袋跟前——她被人从那脑袋身边夺走，已经两年了。在她想来，此刻死也死得甘愿，只要能怀里枕着那个亚麻色的小脑袋咽气。

===Original===
And so, in spite of the opposition of Bill Jones’s son, who threatened her with every sort of evil if she left, Ralph wrapped Mrs. Thomson’s blue drilling in Nancy Sawyer’s shawl, and bore the feeble woman off to Lewisburg. And as they drove away, a sad, childlike voice cried from the gratings of the upper window, “Goodbye! goodbye!” Ralph turned and saw that it was Phil, poor Phil, for whom there was no deliverance.24 And all the way back Ralph pronounced mental maledictions on the Dorcas Society, not for sending garments to the Five Points or the South Sea Islands, whichever it was, but for being so blind to the sorrow and poverty within its reach. He did not know, for he had not read the reports of the Boards of State Charities, that nearly all alms-houses are very much like this, and that the State of New York is not better in this regard than Indiana. And he did not know that it is true in almost all other counties, as it was in his own, that “Christian” people do not think enough of Christ to look for him in these lazar-houses.

And while Ralph denounced the Dorcas Society, the eager, hungry heart of the mother ran, flew toward the little white-headed boy.

===Chinese===
于是，尽管比尔·琼斯的儿子百般拦阻，拿种种凶事吓唬她不许走，拉尔夫还是把南希·索耶的披肩裹在汤姆森太太那身蓝粗布外头，把这位虚弱的妇人接去了刘易斯堡。车赶出院门的时候，楼上一扇装着铁栅的窗户里，一个凄凄的、孩子似的声音喊道：「再见！再见！」拉尔夫回头，看清那是菲尔，可怜的菲尔——没有人来救她。24 一路上，拉尔夫在心里把多加慈善会咒了又咒——不是咒它往五点区还是南洋群岛送衣裳（管它是哪里），是咒它对自己眼皮底下的愁苦和穷困，瞎成了这副样子。他不知道——因为他没读过州慈善委员会的报告——几乎所有的济贫院都是这一副样子，纽约州在这方面也并不比印第安纳强。他也不知道，几乎别的所有县也都同他这个县一样：「信基督」的人们，对基督想得还不够多，多到肯来这些麻风院里找一找他的地步。

拉尔夫还在数落多加会的时候，做娘的那颗饥渴滚烫的心，早已撒开腿，朝着那个白头小子飞去了。

===Original===
No, I can not do it; I can not tell you about that meeting. I am sure that Miss Nancy Sawyer’s tea tasted exceedingly good to the pauper, who had known nothing but cold water for years, and that the bread and butter were delicious to a palate that had eaten poorhouse soup for dinner, and coarse poorhouse bread and vile molasses for supper, and that without change for three years. But I can not tell you how it seemed that evening to Miss Nancy Sawyer, as the poor English lady sat in speechless ecstacy, rocking in the old splint-bottomed rocking-chair in the firelight, while she pressed to her bosom with all the might of her enfeebled arms, the form of the little Shocky, who half-sobbed and half-sang, over and over again, “God ha’n’t forgot us, mother; God ha’n’t forgot us.”

===Chinese===
不，我做不到；那场相见，我没法讲给你听。我拿得准的是：南希·索耶小姐的茶，在那位多年来只认得凉水的贫民嘴里，滋味好到了头；黄油面包在那条舌头上也是美味——那条舌头，午饭喝的是济贫院的汤，晚饭啃的是济贫院的粗面包、就着劣质糖稀，三年如一日。可我没法告诉你，那天傍晚，在南希·索耶小姐眼里那是怎样一番光景：可怜的英国太太坐在炉火光里那把旧绷椅面的摇椅上，喜得说不出话，用她那两条虚弱胳膊的全部力气，把小肖基的身子搂在怀里；小肖基一半呜咽、一半唱，翻来覆去只有一句：「上帝没有忘了俺们，娘；上帝没有忘了俺们。」

## XVII A Council of War / 十七 军事会议

===Original===
Shocky, whose feet had flown as soon as he saw the final fall of Pete Jones, told the whole story to the wondering and admiring ears of Miss Hawkins, who unhappily could not remember anything at the East just like it; to the frightened ears of the rheumatic old lady who felt sure her ole man’s talk and stubbornness would be the ruin of him, and to the indignant ears of the old soldier who was hobbling up and down, sentinel-wise, in front of his cabin, standing guard over himself.

“No, I won’t leave,” he said to Ralph and Bud. “You see I jest won’t. What would Gin’ral Winfield Scott say ef he knew that one of them as fit at Lundy’s Lane backed out, retreated, run fer fear of a passel of thieves? No, sir; me and the old flintlock will live and die together. I’ll put a thunderin’ charge of buckshot into the first one of them scoundrels as comes up the holler. It’ll be another Lundy’s Lane. And you, Mr. Hartsook, may send Scott word that ole Pearson, as fit at Lundy’s Lane under him, died a-fightin’ thieves on Rocky Branch, in Hoopole Kyounty, State of Injeanny.”

And the old man hobbled faster and faster, taxing his wooden leg to the very utmost, as if his victory depended on the vehemence with which he walked his beat.

Mrs. Pearson sat wringing her hands and looking appealingly at Martha Hawkins, who stood in the door, in despair, looking appealingly at Bud. Bud was stupefied by the old man’s stubbornness and his own pain, and in his turn appealed mutely to the master, in whose resources he had boundless confidence. Ralph, seeing that all depended on him, was taxing his wits to think of some way to get round Pearson’s stubbornness. Shocky hung to the old man’s coat and pulled away at him with many entreating words, but the venerable, bareheaded sentinel strode up and down furiously, with his flintlock on his shoulder and his basket-knife in his belt.

===Chinese===
肖基一看见皮特·琼斯终于栽倒，脚底下早就飞了起来，把事情从头到尾讲给霍金斯小姐那又惊又羡的耳朵听——可惜小姐想不起东部有一件堪与此相比的事；讲给那位害风湿的老太太那吓得慌慌的耳朵听——她拿得准，她家老头子那张嘴和那股倔劲儿，迟早要了他的命；又讲给那老兵愤愤的耳朵听——他正一瘸一拐、哨兵似的在小屋前来回踱步，给自己站岗。

「不，俺不走，」他对拉尔夫和巴德说。「你瞧，俺就是不走。温菲尔德·斯科特将军（Winfield Scott）要是知道，当年在伦迪巷（Lundy’s Lane）打过仗的人，竟为一伙毛贼缩了阵、退了兵、撒腿跑了，会怎么说？不，先生；俺跟这支老燧发枪，活着在一起，死了也在一起。哪号狗东西头一个敢进沟，俺就先给他一枪膛管够的铅砂。这儿再来一回伦迪巷。你呢，哈茨克先生，可以给斯科特捎话：当年在伦迪巷跟着他打过仗的老皮尔逊，在印第安纳州胡普尔县石头溪（Rocky Branch）沟里，打着贼，战死了。」

老人越走越快，把那条木头腿使唤到十足十的地步，仿佛胜负全看他巡逻这一趟的火气够不够旺。

皮尔逊太太坐在那儿绞着手，哀告似的望着玛莎·霍金斯；霍金斯小姐立在门口，一筹莫展，又哀告似的望着巴德。巴德被老人的倔劲儿和自己的疼劲儿弄得呆了，轮到他，又默默地求救于老师——他对老师的能耐，怀着无边无际的信任。拉尔夫眼看全指着自个儿了，便绞尽脑汁，想个法子绕过皮尔逊的倔脾气。肖基吊着老人的衣裳角，一边拽一边说许多央告的话；可那位光着脑袋、令人起敬的哨兵，肩上挎着燧发枪，腰里别着编筐刀，只顾大步流星、怒冲冲地来回走。

===Original===
Just at this point somebody could be seen indistinctly through the bushes coming up the hollow.

“Halt!” cried the old hero. “Who goes there?”

“It’s me, Mr. Pearson. Don’t shoot me, please.”

It was the voice of Hannah Thomson. Hearing that the whole neighborhood was rising against the benefactor of Shocky and of her family, she had slipped away from the eyes of her mistress, and run with breathless haste to give warning in the cabin on Rocky Branch. Seeing Ralph, she blushed, and went into the cabin.

===Chinese===
正在这时，透过灌木丛，影影绰绰瞧得见一个人影，顺着沟往这边来了。

「站住！」老英雄喊道。「什么人？」

「是俺呀，皮尔逊先生。求您别开枪。」

是汉娜·汤姆森的声音。她听说左邻右舍全都起来对付肖基和她一家的恩人，便从女主人的眼皮底下溜出来，气都不换一口，一路跑来石头溪上的小屋报信。一看见拉尔夫，她红了脸，进了屋。

===Original===
“Well,” said Ralph, “the enemy is not coming yet. Let us hold a council of war.”

This thought came to Ralph like an inspiration. It pleased the old man’s whim, and he sat down on the doorstep.

“Now, I suppose,” said Ralph, “that General Winfield Scott always looked into things a little before he went into a fight. Didn’t he?”

“*To* be sure,” assented the old man.

“Well,” said Ralph. “What is the condition of the enemy? I suppose the whole neighborhood’s against us.”

“*To* be sure,” said the old man. The rest were silent, but all felt the statement to be about true.

===Chinese===
「这样吧，」拉尔夫说，「敌人眼下还没来。咱们开个军事会议。」

这主意来得像灵光一现。它正投了老人的怪脾气，他便在门槛上坐下了。

「我想，」拉尔夫说，「温菲尔德·斯科特将军临开仗之前，总要把各处情形先摸一摸底。是不是？」

「*可*不是。」老人附和道。

「那么，」拉尔夫说，「敌情如何？我看是全村邻里都跟咱们作对吧。」

「*可*不是。」老人说。其余的人都不做声，可都觉得这话差不离是实情。

===Original===
“Next,” said Ralph, “I suppose General Winfield Scott would always inquire into the condition of his own troops. Now let us see. Captain Pearson has Bud, who is the right wing, badly crippled by having his arm broken in the first battle.” (Miss Hawkins looked pale.)

“*To* be sure,” said the old man.

“And I am the left wing, pretty good at giving advice, but very slender in a fight.”

“*To* be sure,” said the old man.

“And Shocky and Miss Martha and Hannah good aids, but nothing in a battle.”

“*To* be sure,” said the basket-maker, a little doubtfully.

===Chinese===
「其次，」拉尔夫说，「我想温菲尔德·斯科特将军总要查问查问自家兵马的情形。来，咱们瞧瞧。皮尔逊上尉麾下有巴德，是右翼，头一仗里胳膊打折了，残得厉害。」（霍金斯小姐脸色发白。）

「*可*不是。」老人说。

「我呢，是左翼，出主意还算在行，真打起来可单薄得很。」

「*可*不是。」老人说。

「还有肖基、玛莎小姐和汉娜，都是好副官，上了阵可顶不了事。」

「*可*不是。」编筐匠说，有点将信将疑。

===Original===
“Now let’s look at the arms and accouterments, I think you call them. Well, this old musket has been loaded—”

“This ten year,” said the old lady.

“And the lock is so rusty that you could not cock it when you wanted to take aim at Hannah.”

The old man looked foolish, and muttered “*To* be sure.”

“And there isn’t another round of ammunition in the house.”

The old man was silent.

===Chinese===
「再来看看军械和装备——我记得你们是这么叫的。嗯，这支老火枪，装了药——」

「装了有十年啦。」老太太说。

「枪机锈成那样，那天您想瞄着汉娜，扳都扳不开。」

老人露出一副窘相，嘟囔道：「*可*不是。」

「家里呢，再一发弹药也没有了。」

老人不吭声了。

===Original===
“Now let us look at the incumbrances. Here’s the old lady and Shocky. If you fight, the enemy will be pleased. It will give them a chance to kill you. And then the old lady will die and they will do with Shocky as they please.”

“*To* be sure,” said the old man reflectively.

“Now,” said Ralph, “General Winfield Scott, under such circumstances, would retreat in good order. Then, when he could muster his forces rightly, he would drive the enemy from his ground.”

“To be sure,” said the old man. “What ort I to do?”

“Have you any friends?”

===Chinese===
「再看看累赘。这儿是老太太和肖基。您要打，敌人正中下怀——正好得了机会杀您。往后老太太活不成，肖基呢，任凭他们想咋摆布就咋摆布。」

「*可*不是。」老人若有所思地说。

「这么着，」拉尔夫说，「温菲尔德·斯科特将军处在这种情况下，会井然有序地撤退。等他把兵力整顿齐整了，再把敌人赶出地盘。」

「可不是，」老人说。「俺该咋办呢？」

「您有亲朋没有？」

===Original===
“Well, yes; ther’s my brother over in Jackson Kyounty. I mout go there.”

“Well,” said Bud, “do you just go down to Spring-in-rock and stay there. Them folks won’t be here tell midnight. I’ll come fer you at nine with my roan colt, and I’ll set you down over on the big road on Buckeye Run. Then you can git on the mail-wagon that passes there about five o’clock in the mornin’, and go over to Jackson County and keep shady till we want you to face the enemy and to swear agin some folks. And then we’ll send fer you.”

“To be sure,” said the old man in a broken voice. “I reckon General Winfield Scott wouldn’t disapprove of such a maneuver as that thar.”

Miss Martha beamed on Bud to his evident delight, for he carried his painful arm part of the way home with her. Ralph noticed that Hannah looked at *him* with a look full of contending emotions. He read admiration, gratitude, and doubt in the expression of her face, as she turned toward home.

“Well, goodbye, ole woman,” said Pearson, as he took up his little handkerchief full of things and started for his hiding-place; “goodbye. I didn’t never think I’d desart you, and ef the old flintlock hadn’t a been rusty, I’d a stayed and died right here by the ole cabin. But I reckon ’ta’n’t best to be brash.”21 And Shocky looked after him, as he hobbled away over the stones, more than ever convinced that God had forgotten all about things on Flat Creek. He gravely expressed his opinion to the master the next day.

===Chinese===
「嗯，有；俺兄弟在杰克逊县那边。俺兴许可以投他去。」

「这么着，」巴德说，「您就先下到石中泉（Spring-in-rock）去待着。那伙人到半夜也来不了。俺九点钟套菊花青小马来接您，把您放在七叶树溪（Buckeye Run）的大路口。那儿早上五点来钟过邮车，您就搭上，去杰克逊县避避风头，等俺们要您回来照面敌人、冲某些人发誓对证的时候，俺们再来接您。」

「可不是，」老人声音发哽地说。「俺寻思，温菲尔德·斯科特将军也不会不赞成这么一路调度。」

玛莎小姐朝巴德满脸放光，巴德那份得意就别提了——他吊着那条疼胳膊，陪她走了一截回家的路。拉尔夫注意到，汉娜望的是*他*，那一眼里翻腾着好些互相打架的情绪。她转身往家去时，他从她脸上读出了倾慕、感激，还有疑虑。

「那么，再会了，老婆子，」皮尔逊说着，拾起那一小手帕包的家当，动身上他的藏身处去；「再会了。俺从来没想过俺会撇下你；要不是那支老燧发枪锈住了，俺就守在这儿，死也死在这老屋旁边。可俺寻思，莽撞不得。」21 肖基望着他踩着石头一瘸一拐地走远，比先前更拿得准：弗拉特克里克这边的种种，上帝已经忘了个一干二净。第二天，他一本正经地把这个意见向老师说了。

## XIV A Crisis with Bud / 十四 与巴德的一场危机

===Original===
Ralph sat still at his desk. The school had gone. All at once he became conscious that Shocky sat yet in his accustomed place upon the hard, backless bench.

“Why, Shocky, haven’t you gone yet?”

“No—sir—I was waitin’ to see if you warn’t a-goin’, too—I—”

“Well?”

“I thought it would make me feel as if God warn’t quite so fur away to talk to you. It did the other day.”

===Chinese===
拉尔夫仍坐在讲桌前没动。学生们都走了。他忽然察觉，肖基还坐在他那老位子上——那条又硬又没靠背的长凳上。

「咦，肖基，你怎么还没走？」

「没——先生——俺在等着瞅瞅，您是不是也要走了——俺——」

「嗯？」

「俺觉着，跟您说说话，就好像上帝没那么老远似的。前儿个就是那样。」

===Original===
The master rose and put his hand on Shocky’s head. Was it the brotherhood in affliction that made Shocky’s words choke him so? Or, was it the weird thoughts that he expressed? Or, was it the recollection that Shocky was Hannah’s brother? Hannah so far, far away from him now! At any rate, Shocky, looking up for the smile on which he fed, saw the relaxing of the master’s face, that had been as hard as stone, and felt just one hot tear on his hand.

“P’r’aps God’s forgot you, too,” said Shocky in a sort of half soliloquy. “Better get away from Flat Creek. You see God forgets everybody down here. ’Cause ’most everybody forgets God, ’cept Mr. Bosaw, and I ’low God don’t no ways keer to be remembered by sich as him. Leastways I wouldn’t if I was God, you know. I wonder what becomes of folks when God forgets ’em?” And Shocky, seeing that the master had resumed his seat and was looking absently into the fire, moved slowly out the door.

“Shocky!” called the master.

The little poet came back and stood before him.

===Chinese===
老师站起身，把手搁在肖基头上。是同病相怜，叫肖基这几句话噎得他这么厉害？还是他道出的那些奇思异想？还是想起肖基是汉娜的弟弟——汉娜如今离他那么、那么远！反正，肖基仰起脸来找他赖以为生的那点笑意时，看见老师那张本已硬得像石头似的脸松开了，还觉出手背上恰恰一滴热泪。

「兴许上帝把您也忘了，」肖基半是自言自语地说。「您顶好离弗拉特克里克远远的。您瞧，这旮旯的人，上帝谁都忘。因为差不多谁也都把上帝忘了，除了博索先生；可俺寻思，像他那号人记着，上帝才不稀罕呢。反正俺要是上帝，俺就不稀罕，您知道吧。俺纳闷，人要是叫上帝忘了，往后会变成啥样？」肖基见老师又坐了回去，正呆呆望着炉火，便慢慢挪出了门。

「肖基！」老师喊道。

小诗人走回来，站到他跟前。

===Original===
“Shocky, you mustn’t think God has forgotten you. God brings things out right at last.” But Ralph’s own faith was weak, and his words sounded hollow and hypocritical to himself. Would God indeed bring things out right?

He sat musing a good while, trying to convince himself of the truth of what he had just been saying to Shocky—that God would indeed bring things out right at last. Would it all come out right if Bud married Hannah? Would it all come out right if he were driven from Flat Creek with a dark suspicion upon his character? Did God concern himself with these things? Was there any God? It was the same old struggle between Doubt and Faith. And when Ralph looked up, Shocky had departed.

In the next hour Ralph fought the old battle of Armageddon. I shall not describe it. You will fight it in your own way. No two alike. The important thing is the End. If you come out as he did, with the doubt gone and the trust in God victorious, it matters little just what shape the battle may take. Since Jacob became Israel there have never been two such struggles alike, save in that they all end either in victory or in defeat.

It was after twelve o’clock on that Christmas day when Ralph put his head out the door of the schoolhouse and called out: “Bud, I’d like to see you.”

===Chinese===
「肖基，你可别想着上帝把你忘了。上帝到头来总会把事情理顺的。」可拉尔夫自己的信心正软弱，这话在他自己听来空洞又虚伪。上帝果真会把事情理顺吗？

他坐着出了半天神，想叫自己信服他刚才对肖基说的那句话——上帝到头来果真会把事情理顺。要是巴德娶了汉娜，一切还会理顺吗？要是他背着洗不清的黑嫌疑被赶出弗拉特克里克，一切还会理顺吗？上帝管不管这些事？到底有没有上帝？还是疑心与信心那场老仗。等拉尔夫抬起头来，肖基已经走了。

接下来的一个钟头，拉尔夫打了他自己的那场哈米吉多顿老仗。我不去描写它。你会用你自己的法子去打，没有两场是一样的。要紧的是结局。你若像他那样收场——疑云散尽，对上帝的信靠得胜——那这场仗究竟摆出个什么阵势，都无关紧要了。自从雅各成了以色列，这样的较量就再没有两场相同，只有一点相同：不是得胜，就是败北。

那个圣诞节，过了十二点，拉尔夫把头探出校舍门，喊道：「巴德，我想跟你说句话。」

===Original===
Bud did not care to see the master, for he had inly resolved to “thrash him” and have done with him. But he couldn’t back out, certainly not in sight of the others who were passing along the road with him.

“I don’t want the rest of you,” said Ralph in a decided way, as he saw that Hank and one or two others were resolved to come also.

“Thought maybe you’d want somebody to see far play,” said Hank as he went off sheepishly.

“If I did, you would be the last one I should ask,” said Ralph. “There’s no unfair play in Bud, and there is in you.” And he shut the door.

===Chinese===
巴德并不想见老师，因为他心里已经打定主意，要「揍他一顿」、一了百了。可他不能缩回去，当着同他一路走在道上的人的面，万万不能。

「别的人我不找。」拉尔夫口气斩钉截铁，因为他看见汉克和另外一两个也铁了心要跟来。

「想着兴许您要个人瞧着，别打得不公道。」汉克讪讪地走开时说。

「真要找人，也轮不到你，」拉尔夫说。「巴德身上没有不公平，你身上有。」说罢他关上了门。

===Original===
“Now, looky here, Mr. Ralph Hartsook,” said Bud. “You don’t come no gum games over me with your saft sodder and all that. I’ve made up my mind. You’ve got to promise to leave these ’ere diggins, or I’ve got to thrash you.”

“You’ll have to thrash me, then,” said Ralph, turning a little pale, but remembering the bulldog. “But you’ll tell me what it’s all about, won’t you?”

“You know well enough. Folks says you know more ’bout the robbery at the Dutchman’s than you orter. But I don’t believe them. Fer them as says it is liars and thieves theirselves. ’Ta’n’t fer none of that. And I shan’t tell you what it *is* fer. So now, if you won’t travel, why, take off your coat and git ready fer a thrashing.”

The master took off his coat and showed his slender arms. Bud laid his off, and showed the physique of a prizefighter.

===Chinese===
「听好了，拉尔夫·哈茨克先生，」巴德说。「少拿你那套软面汤来糊弄俺。俺主意定了。你要么应下离开这地界，要么俺就得揍你。」

「那你就只好揍我了，」拉尔夫说，脸色微微发白，心里却记着那条斗牛犬。「可你总得告诉我这是为什么吧？」

「你心里透亮着呢。人家说，『荷兰人』家那桩劫案，你知道得过了头。可俺不信他们。因为说这话的，自个儿就是撒谎的贼。俺也不是为那个。俺为啥，俺也不告诉你。这么着，你要是不走，那就脱褂子，预备挨揍吧。」

老师脱了上衣，露出细瘦的胳膊。巴德也把上衣撂下，露出一副职业拳手的身板。

===Original===
“You a’n’t a-goin’ to fight *me*?” said Bud.

“Not unless you make me.”

“Why I could chaw you all up.”

“I know that.”

“Well, you’re the grittiest feller I ever did see, and ef you’d jest kep off of my ground I wouldn’t a touched you. But I a’n’t a-goin’ to be cut out by no feller a livin’ ’thout thrashin’ him in an inch of his life. You see I wanted to git out of this Flat Crick way. We’re a low-lived set here in Flat Crick. And I says to myself, I’ll try to be somethin’ more nor Pete Jones, and dad, and these other triflin’, good-fer-nothin’ ones ’bout here. And when you come I says, There’s one as’ll help me. And what do you do with yor book-larnin’ and town manners but start right out to git away the gal that I’d picked out, when I’d picked her out kase I thought, not bein’ Flat Crick born herself, she might help a feller to do better! Now I won’t let nobody cut me out without givin’ ’em the best thrashin’ it’s in these ’ere arms to give.”

===Chinese===
「你不会是要跟*俺*打吧？」巴德说。

「你逼我，才打。」

「咦，俺能把你整个儿嚼了。」

「这我知道。」

「哼，你真是俺这辈子见过的最有种的家伙。你要是光离俺的地界远点儿，俺一根汗毛也不碰你。可天底下随便哪个活人想横刀夺爱，俺不把他往死里揍个半死，就不算完。你瞧，俺早想跳出弗拉特克里克这道辙了。俺们弗拉特克里克这一带的，都是下等坯子。俺跟自个儿说，俺要争口气，活出个人样来，别学皮特·琼斯，别学俺爹，也别学这一带别的二流子、窝囊废。你一来，俺就说：来了一个能帮俺的。可你倒好，拿你的书本学问、城里做派干了啥？一上来就抢俺挑定的姑娘——俺挑她，就为着寻思她不是弗拉特克里克土生土长的，兴许能拉着一个爷们儿往上走！这下你明白了，谁想截俺的道，俺就凭这双胳膊，给他一顿顶顶结实的揍。」

===Original===
“But I haven’t tried to cut you out.”

“You can’t fool *me*.”

“Bud, listen to me, and then thrash me if you will. I went with that girl once. When I found you had some claims, I gave her up. Not because I was afraid of you, for I would rather have taken the worst thrashing you can give me than give her up. But I haven’t spoken to her since the night of the first spelling-school.”

“You lie!” said Bud, doubling his fists.

Ralph grew red.

===Chinese===
「可我没想截你的道。」

「你糊弄不了*俺*。」

「巴德，听我说完，说完你要揍便揍。我跟那位姑娘来往过一回。后来发觉你先有话在先，我就把她放下了。不是怕你——你把我往死里揍，也比叫她撒手强。可打头一场拼字学校那晚起，我再没跟她说过一句话。」

「你放屁！」巴德攥起拳头说。

拉尔夫涨红了脸。

===Original===
“You was a-waitin’ on her last Sunday right afore my eyes, and a-tryin’ to ketch my attention too. So when you’re ready say so.”

“Bud, there is some misunderstanding.” Hartsook spoke slowly and felt bewildered. “I tell you that I did not speak to Hannah last Sunday, and you know I didn’t.”

“Hanner!” Bud’s eyes grew large. “Hanner!” Here he gasped for breath, and looked around, “Hanner!” He couldn’t get any further than the name at first. “Why, plague take it, who said Hanner?”

“Mirandy said you were courting Hannah,” said Ralph, feeling round in a vague way to get his ideas together.

“Mirandy! Thunder! You believed Mirandy! Well! Now, looky here, Mr. Hartsook, ef you was to say that my sister lied, I’d lick you till yer hide wouldn’t hold shucks. But *I* say, a-twix you and me and the gatepost, don’t you never believe nothing that Mirandy Means says. Her and marm has set theirselves like fools to git you. Hanner! Well, she’s a mighty nice gal, but you’re welcome to *her*. I never tuck no shine that air way. But I was out of school last Thursday and Friday a-shucking corn to take to mill a-Saturday. And when I come past the Squire’s and seed you talking to a gal as is a gal, you know”—here Bud hesitated and looked foolish—“I felt hoppin’ mad.”

===Chinese===
「上礼拜天，你当着俺的面陪着她献殷勤，还想法子引俺的注意呢。你要好了，言语一声。」

「巴德，这里头有误会。」哈茨克说得慢，人也懵了。「我告诉你，上礼拜天我没跟汉娜说过一句话，你知道我没有。」

「汉娜！」巴德的眼睛瞪圆了。「汉娜！」他倒抽了一口气，四下张望，「汉娜！」一时半会儿，除了这个名字他蹦不出下文。「咦，见鬼了，谁说汉娜来着？」

「米兰迪说，你追的是汉娜。」拉尔夫说，一面恍恍惚惚地想把头绪拢起来。

「米兰迪！我的老天爷！你信了米兰迪！好嘛！你听好了，哈茨克先生，你要是敢说俺妹妹撒谎，俺就揍得你那张皮连糠都兜不住。可是*俺*说句天知地知、你知俺知的话——明斯家米兰迪说的话，你可一句也别当真。她跟俺娘犯着傻，铁了心要把你勾到手。汉娜！哼，她是个顶好顶好的姑娘，你要她，俺让给你。俺对她压根儿没起过那个念头。可上礼拜四礼拜五俺没上学，在家剥玉米，预备礼拜六上磨坊。俺打治安官家门口过，瞅见你跟一个姑娘说话——那可是个真正的姑娘，你懂」——说到这儿巴德迟疑了，一脸傻相——「俺当时火冒三丈。」

===Original===
Bud put on his coat.

Ralph put on his coat.

Then they shook hands and Bud went out. Ralph sat looking into the fire. There was no conscientious difficulty now in the way of his claiming Hannah. The dry forestick lying on the rude stone andirons burst into a blaze. The smoldering hope In the heart of Ralph Hartsook did the same. He could have Hannah if he could win her. But there came slowly back the recollection of his lost standing in Flat Creek. There was circumstantial evidence against him. It was evident that Hannah believed something of this. What other stones Small might have put in circulation he did not know. Would Small try to win Hannah’s love to throw it away again, as he had done with others? At least he would not spare any pains to turn the heart of the bound girl against Ralph.

The bright flame on the forestick, which Ralph had been watching, flickered and burned low.

===Chinese===
巴德穿上了上衣。

拉尔夫穿上了上衣。

于是两人握了握手，巴德出去了。拉尔夫坐着望炉火。如今再没有什么良心上的拦阻，拦着他去求汉娜了。搭在粗石柴架上的那根干柴，轰地蹿起火苗。拉尔夫·哈茨克心里那点闷着的希望，也这么轰了一下。他要是能赢得汉娜，汉娜就是他的。可是他又慢慢想起，自己在弗拉特克里克已经没了立足的地。旁证都朝着他来。汉娜显然也信了几分。斯莫尔还往外撒了些什么别的石头子儿，他不得而知。斯莫尔会不会去赢汉娜的心，赢了再随手丢掉，像他对付别人那样？至少，要叫这个帮工姑娘的心背离拉尔夫，他是不惜力气的。

拉尔夫一直望着的那根柴上的亮火苗，摇了摇，暗下去了。

## XXVI A Letter and Its Consequences / 二十六 一封信及其后果

===Original===
> “Squar Haukins
> “this is too Lett u no that u beter be Keerful hoo yoo an yore familly tacks cides with fer peepl wont Stan it too hev the Men wat’s sportin the wuns wat’s robin us, sported bi yor Fokes kepin kumpne with ’em, u been a ossifer ov the Lau, yor Ha wil bern as qick as to an yor Barn tu. so Tak kere. No mor ad pressent.”

===Chinese===
> 「霍金斯治安官：
> 「写这封信是告诉你，你跟你一家子人到底跟啥人站一头，顶好给我当心着点。那个跟抢俺们的人穿一条裤子的家伙，叫你家的人抬举着、厮混着，乡亲们可不答应就这么下去。你是当过执法官的人，你家干草烧起来跟麻絮一样快，连谷仓也跑不了。所以自个儿掂量。多余的话不写了。」

===Original===
This letter accomplished its purpose. The Squire’s spectacles slipped off several times while he read it. His wig had to be adjusted. If he had been threatened personally he would not have minded it so much. But the hay stacks were dearer to him than the apple of his glass eye. The barn was more precious than his wig. And those who hoped to touch Bud in a tender place through this letter knew the Squire’s weakness far better than they knew the spelling-book. To see his new red barn with its large “Mormon” hay-press inside, and the mounted Indian on the vane, consumed, was too much for the Hawkins heart to stand. Evidently the danger was on the side of his niece. But how should he influence Martha to give up Bud? Martha did not value the haystacks half so highly as she did her lover. Martha did not think the new red barn, with the great Mormon press inside and the galloping Indian on the vane, worth half so much as a moral principle or a kindhearted action. Martha, bless her! would have sacrificed anything rather than forsake the poor. But Squire Hawkins’s lips shut tight over his false teeth in a way that suggested astringent purse-strings, and Squire Hawkins could not sleep at night if the new red barn, with the galloping Indian on the vane, were in danger. Martha must be reached somehow.

===Chinese===
这封信达到了目的。治安官读信的时候，眼镜滑下来好几回，假发也只得扶了又扶。倘若威胁的是他本人，他倒不至于这么放在心上。可那些干草垛，在他眼里比那只玻璃假眼的眼珠还金贵；谷仓又比他的假发贵重得多。想借这封信往巴德的痛处戳的人，对治安官的软处可比对拼写课本熟得多。眼睁睁瞧着那座新红谷仓——里头架着大型「摩门」压草机，风向标上骑着个印第安人——付之一炬，霍金斯家的心可受不了。明摆着，危险出在他侄女这一头。可他怎么才能叫玛莎撒开巴德呢？在玛莎眼里，干草垛抵不上她心上人的一半分量；那座新红谷仓——连同仓里的大摩门压草机、风向标上飞奔的印第安人——也抵不上一条道义、一件善举的一半。玛莎，老天保佑这好姑娘！她什么都舍得牺牲，就是舍不得撇下穷人。可是霍金斯治安官的嘴唇在假牙上抿得紧紧的，那副样子活像勒紧了的荷包绳；只要新红谷仓——连同风向标上那个飞奔的印第安人——有个闪失，他夜里就睡不成觉。无论如何，总得想个法子说动玛莎。

===Original===
So, with many adjustings of that most adjustable wig, with many turnings of that reversible glass eye, the Squire managed to frighten Martha by the intimation that he had been threatened, and to make her understand, what it cost her much to understand, that she must turn the cold shoulder to chivalrous, awkward Bud, whom she loved most tenderly, partly, perhaps, because he did not remind her of anybody she had ever known at the East.

Tuesday evening was the fatal time. Spelling-school was the fatal occasion. Bud was the victim. Pete Jones had his revenge. For Bud had been all the evening trying to muster courage enough to offer himself as Martha’s escort. He was not encouraged by the fact that he had spelled even worse than usual, while Martha had distinguished herself by holding her ground against Jeems Phillips for half an hour. But he screwed his courage to the sticking place, not by quoting to himself the adage, “Faint heart never won fair lady,” which, indeed, he had never heard, but by reminding himself that “ef you don’t resk nothin’ you’ll never git nothin’.” So, when the spelling-school had adjourned, he sidled up to her, and, looking dreadfully solemn and a little foolish, he said:

“Kin I see you safe home?”

And she, with a feeling that her uncle’s life was in danger, and that his salvation depended upon her resolution—she, with a feeling that she was pronouncing sentence of death on her own great hope, answered huskily:

“No, I thank you.”

If she had only known that it was the red barn with the Indian on top that was in danger, she would probably have let the galloping brave take care of himself.

It seemed to Bud, as he walked home mortified, disgraced, disappointed, hopeless, that all the world had gone down in a whirlpool of despair.

“Might a knowed it,” he said to himself. “Of course, a smart gal like Martha a’n’t a-goin’ to take a big, blunderin’ fool that can’t spell in two syllables. What’s the use of tryin’? A Flat Cricker is a Flat Cricker. You can’t make nothin’ else of him, no more nor you can make a Chiny hog into a Berkshire.”

===Chinese===
于是，治安官把那顶顶好摆弄的假发摆弄了又摆弄，把那只两面都能转的玻璃眼转了又转，总算借着自己受了恐吓的口风吓住了玛莎，也叫她明白了——她明白这个可费了大劲——她必须给那位侠气而笨拙的巴德冷脸子。她爱巴德爱得极温柔，也许一部分缘故，正在于巴德不像她在东部认得的任何人。

星期二晚上是那个要命的时刻，拼字学校是那个要命的场合，巴德是受害的人，皮特·琼斯报了他的仇。原来巴德一整晚都在攒劲，想攒足勇气提出送玛莎回家。偏偏这一晚他拼得比平时还糟，玛莎却大出风头，跟吉姆斯·菲利普斯顶了半个钟头不落下阵——这可没法给他壮胆。可他还是把勇气拧到了螺丝拧紧的地步，不是靠默念那句格言「懦夫难赢美人心」——这话他实在压根没听过——而是靠提醒自己：「啥险都不敢冒，就啥也捞不着。」于是拼字学校散场后，他蹭到她跟前，神情庄重得吓人，又带着点傻气，说道：

「俺能送你平安到家不？」

而她呢，一心只觉得叔叔性命堪虞、他的安危全看她这会儿的决心——她，怀着一种正给自己心头大希望宣判死刑的感觉，嗓音发哑地答道：

「不，谢谢你。」

她要是早知道遭殃的不过是屋顶上立着印第安人的红谷仓，多半就由着那个飞奔的勇士自求多福了。

巴德一路回家，又臊又丢脸，又失望又绝望，只觉得整个世界都沉进了一座绝望的漩涡里。

「早该想到喽，」他自言自语，「像玛莎这么伶俐的姑娘，咋会要俺这么个大个子笨蛋，连两个音节的词都拼不来。还试个啥劲？弗拉特克里克人天生就是弗拉特克里克人，咋也变不成别的，就跟中国猪变不成巴克夏猪一个理。」

## XXVII A Loss and a Gain / 二十七 一失一得

===Original===
Dr. Small, silent, attentive, assiduous Dr. Small, set himself to work to bind up the wounded heart of Bud Means, even as he had bound up his broken arm. The flattery of his fine eyes, which looked at Bud’s muscles so admiringly, which gave attention to his lightest remark, was not lost on the young Flat Creek Hercules. Outwardly at least Pete Jones showed no inclination to revenge himself on Bud. Was it respect for muscle, or was it the influence of Small? At any rate, the concentrated extract of the resentment of Pete Jones and his clique was now ready to empty itself upon the head of Hartsook. And Ralph found himself in his dire extremity without even the support of Bud, whose good resolutions seemed to give way all at once. There have been many men of culture and more favorable surroundings who have thrown themselves away with less provocation. As it was, Bud quit school, avoided Ralph, and seemed more than ever under the influence of Dr. Small, besides becoming the intimate of Walter Johnson, Small’s student and Mrs. Matilda White’s son. They made a strange pair—Bud with his firm jaw and silent, cautious manner, and Walter Johnson with his weak chin, his nice neckties, and general dandy appearance.

To be thus deserted in his darkest hour by his only friend was the bitterest ingredient in Ralph’s cup. In vain he sought an interview. Bud always eluded him. While by all the faces about him Ralph learned that the storm was getting nearer and nearer to himself. It might delay. If it had been Pete Jones alone, it might blow over. But Ralph felt sure that the relentless hand of Dr. Small was present in all his troubles. And he had only to look into Small’s eye to know how inextinguishable was a malignity that burned so steadily and so quietly.

===Chinese===
斯莫尔医生——沉默、周到、勤勉的斯莫尔医生——着手医治巴德·明斯那颗受了伤的心，就像他先前接好那条断胳膊一样。他那一双漂亮眼睛里的谀意，艳羡地盯着巴德的筋肉骨，连巴德最不经意的一句话也郑重其事地听着——这份抬举，年轻的弗拉特克里克大力士并非无动于衷。至少在表面上，皮特·琼斯已无意对巴德寻仇。这是敬畏筋肉呢，还是斯莫尔的力量使然？无论如何，皮特·琼斯和他那一伙浓缩提炼过的怨毒，如今正备好了要一股脑儿泼到哈茨克头上。拉尔夫发觉自己陷于绝境，连巴德的支持也失掉了——巴德那些向好之心，似乎一下子全垮了。世上多少有教养、境遇优渥的人，受的挫还不如这重，也就此自暴自弃了。事实是，巴德退了学，躲着拉尔夫，似乎比以往更受斯莫尔医生的摆布，还跟沃尔特·约翰逊成了密友——约翰逊是斯莫尔的学生，玛蒂尔达·怀特太太的儿子。这两人凑成古怪的一对：巴德下巴坚毅，沉默而谨慎；沃尔特·约翰逊下巴绵软，系着讲究的领结，浑身上下一股纨绔气。

在自己最黑暗的时辰被唯一的朋友这样抛下，是拉尔夫那杯苦酒里最苦的一味。他屡次求见，全是白费——巴德总是躲着他。与此同时，从周围一张张脸上，拉尔夫看出风暴正一步步朝自己逼近。也许还会拖延些时日。倘若只有皮特·琼斯一个人，兴许也就烟消云散了。可拉尔夫确信，他的一切祸事背后都有斯莫尔医生那只冷酷的手。他只消望进斯莫尔的眼睛，便知道一种烧得这样平稳、这样安静的恶意，是浇不灭的。

===Original===
But there is no cup of unmixed bitterness. With an innocent man there is no night so dark that some star does not shine. Ralph had one strong sheet-anchor. On his return from Lewisburg on Monday Bud had handed him a note, written on common blue foolscap, in round, old-fashioned hand. It ran:

> “Dear Sir: Anybody who can do so good a thing as you did for our Shocky, can not be bad. I hope you will forgive me. All the appearances in the world, and all that anybody says, can not make me think you anything else but a good man. I hope God will reward you. You must not answer this, and you hadn’t better see me again, or think any more of what you spoke about the other night. I shall be a slave for three years more, and then I must work for my mother and Shocky; but I felt so bad to think that I had spoken so hard to you, that I could not help writing this. Respectfully,
> “Hannah Thomson.
> “To Mr. R. Hartsook, Esq.”

===Chinese===
但世上没有一杯纯而又纯的苦酒。对一个清白的人，再黑的夜也总有哪颗星在亮。拉尔夫还有一只牢靠的大锚。星期一从刘易斯堡回来时，巴德交给他一张字条，写在普通的蓝色大开张纸上，一手圆润的老式字。全文如下：

> 「亲爱的先生：凡能为我们肖基做下这等善事的人，绝不会是坏人。我盼望您能原谅我。世上一切表象，加上任凭谁说什么，都不能叫我信您是好人以外的什么人。愿上帝奖赏您。您千万不可回信，也顶好别再同我见面，那晚您提过的事也别再想了。我还得给人当三年使唤丫头，往后还得干活养我娘和肖基；可我一想到对您说话说得那么狠，心里实在难受，忍不住写下这几句。恭恭敬敬，
> 「汉娜·汤姆森。
> 「呈拉·哈茨克先生。」

===Original===
Ralph read it over and over. What else he did with it I shall not tell. You want to know whether he kissed it, and put it into his bosom. Many a man as intelligent and manly as Hartsook has done quite as foolish a thing as that. You have been a little silly perhaps—if it is silly—and you have acted in a sentimental sort of a way over such things. But it would never do for me to tell you what Ralph did. Whether he put the letter into his bosom or not, he put the words into his heart, and, metaphorically speaking, he shook that little blue billet, written on coarse foolscap paper—he shook that little letter full of confidence, in the face and eyes of all the calamities that haunted him. If Hannah believed in him, the whole world might distrust him. When Hannah was in one scale and the whole world in the other, of what account was the world? Justice may be blind, but all the pictures of blind cupids in the world can not make Love blind. And it was well that Ralph weighed things in this way. For the time was come in which he needed all the courage the blue billet could give him.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫把它读了一遍又一遍。他还对它做了些别的什么，我可不说。你想知道他有没有吻它，有没有把它揣进怀里。多少跟哈茨克一样聪明、一样有男子气概的人，干过的傻事不比这轻。说不定你也曾有点犯傻——如果那算傻的话——也曾在这类事情上做出一副痴情的样子。可拉尔夫究竟做了什么，我断然不能告诉你。不管他有没有把信揣进怀里，他是把那些话揣进了心里；而且打个比方说，他把那张写在粗糙大开张纸上的蓝色小笺——把那封写满信任的小信——抖在一切缠着他的祸事的脸上和眼前。只要汉娜信他，全世界尽可以疑他。汉娜在天平这一头，全世界在那一头，全世界又算得了什么？司法女神尽可以瞎，可世上一切蒙眼的丘比特画像，也没法叫爱情瞎了眼。拉尔夫这样掂量事物，掂得好。因为时候到了——那封蓝色小笺能给他多少勇气，他正需要多少勇气。

## VI A Night at Pete Jones’s / 六 在皮特·琼斯家过夜

===Original===
When Ralph got to Pete Jones’s he found that sinister-looking individual in the act of kicking one of his many dogs out of the house.

“Come in, stranger, come in. You’ll find this ’ere house full of brats, but I guess you kin kick your way around among ’em. Take a cheer. Here, git out! go to thunder with you!” And with these mild imperatives he boxed one of his boys over in one direction and one of his girls over in the other. “I believe in trainin’ up children to mind when they’re spoke to,” he said to Ralph apologetically. But it seemed to the teacher that he wanted them to mind just a little before they were spoken to.

“P’raps you’d like a bed. Well, jest climb up the ladder on the outside of the house. Takes up a thunderin’ sight of room to have a stairs inside, and we ha’n’t got no room to spare. You’ll find a bed in the furdest corner. My Pete’s already got half of it, and you can take t’other half. Ef Pete goes to takin’ his half in the middle, and tryin’ to make you take yourn on both sides, jest kick him.”

===Chinese===
拉尔夫到皮特·琼斯家的时候，正撞见那位面相不善的人物在往屋外踢狗——他家狗多，这只是其中的一只。

「进来，外乡人，进来。你这就要瞧见了，这屋里满地崽子，不过俺瞅你在他们当中踢打出一条道来不难。坐椅子去。嘿，滚出去！滚你的！」伴随着这些客客气气的祈使句，他一巴掌把他一个儿子扇到东边，又一巴掌把一个闺女扇到西边。「俺信一条理儿：娃娃得练成个样子，一喊就应。」他向拉尔夫告罪似的说。可在老师看来，他要的是喊都不消喊，他们先就得乖起来。

「兴许你想睡了。行，你就从房子外头那架梯子爬上去。屋里打楼梯，太占地方，俺们也没那闲地方。顶里头的犄角有张床。俺家小皮特已经占了一半，你就睡另外一半。皮特要是把他那一半睡到正当中，叫你两头顶着边儿睡，你就踹他。」

===Original===
In this comfortless bed “in the furdest corner,” Ralph found sleep out of the question. Pete took three-fourths of the bed, and Hannah took all of his thoughts. So he lay, and looked out through the cracks in the “clapboards” (as they call rough shingles in the old West) at the stars. For the clouds had now broken away. And he lay thus recounting to himself, as a miser counts the pieces that compose his hoard, every step of that road from the time he had overtaken Hannah in the hollow to the fence. Then he imagined again the pleasure of helping her over, and then he retraced the ground to the box-elder tree at the spring, and repeated to himself the conversation until he came to the part in which she said that only time and God could help her. What did she mean? What was the hidden part of her life? What was the connection between her and Shocky?

Hours wore on, and still the mind of Ralph Hartsook went back and traveled the same road, over the fence, past the box-elder, up to the inexplicable part of the conversation, and stood bewildered with the same puzzling questions about the bound girl’s life.

===Chinese===
在那张「顶里头犄角」的、毫不舒服的床上，拉尔夫觉得睡着是别指望了。皮特占了床的四分之三，汉娜占了他心思的全部。他就那么躺着，透过「护墙板」——老西部的人管粗木瓦这么叫——的板缝望星星。云这时候已经散尽了。他躺在那儿，像守财奴一枚一枚数他窖里的钱那样，把那段路上的一步一程数给自己听：从他在沟里追上汉娜那一刻起，一直到那道栅栏。跟着他又在想象里重温了一遍扶她翻栅栏的快活，再顺着原路走回泉边那棵羽叶槭下，把那场对话在心里过了一遍又一遍，直到她说只有时间和上帝能救她那一段。她是什么意思？她日子背后藏着的那一半是什么？她跟肖基，是什么干系？

时辰一点点捱过去，拉尔夫·哈茨克的心思还是往回跑，还是走那条老路：翻过栅栏，走过羽叶槭，走到那场叫人摸不着头脑的对话跟前，对着这个帮工姑娘身世的那些谜，站在那儿发懵。

===Original===
At last he got up, drew on his clothes, and sat down on the top of the ladder, looking down over the bluegrass pasture which lay on the border between the land of Jones and the land of Means. The earth was white with moonlight. He could not sleep. Why not walk? It might enable him to sleep. And once determined on walking, he did not hesitate a moment as to the direction in which he should walk. The bluegrass pasture (was it not like unto the garden of Eden?) lay right before him. That box-elder stood just in sight. To spring over the fence and take the path down the hill and over the brook was as quickly done as decided upon. To stand again under the box-elder, to climb again over the farther fence, and to walk down the road toward the schoolhouse was so easy and so delightful that it was done without thought. For Ralph was an eager man—when he saw no wrong in anything that proposed itself, he was wont to follow his impulse without deliberation. And this keeping company with the stars, and the memory of a delightful walk, were so much better than the commonplace Flat Creek life that he threw himself into his night excursion with enthusiasm.

At last he stood in the little hollow where he had joined Hannah. It was the very spot at which Shocky, too, had met him a few mornings before. He leaned against the fence and tried again to solve the puzzle of Hannah’s troubles. For that she had troubles he did not doubt. Neither did he doubt that he could help her if he could discover what they were. But he had no clue. In the midst of his meditations he heard the thud of horses’ hoofs coming down the road. Until that moment he had not felt his own loneliness. He shrank back into the fence-corner. The horsemen were galloping. There were three of them, and there was one figure that seemed familiar to Ralph. But he could not tell who it was. Neither could he remember having seen the horse, which was a sorrel with a white left forefoot and a white nose. The men noticed him and reined up a little. Why he should have been startled by the presence of these men he could not tell, but an indefinable dread seized him. They galloped on, and he stood still shivering with a nervous fear. The cold seemed to have got into his bones. He remembered that the region lying on Flat Creek and Clifty Creek had the reputation of being infested with thieves, who practiced horse-stealing and housebreaking. Forever since the day when Murrell’s confederate bands were paralyzed by the death of their leader, there have still existed gangs of desperadoes in parts of Southern Indiana and Illinois, and in Iowa, Missouri, Kentucky, and the Southwest. It is out of these materials that border ruffianism has grown, and the nine members of the Reno band who were hanged two or three years ago by lynch law,16 were remains of the bad blood that came into the West in the days of Daniel Boone. Shall I not say that these bands of desperadoes still found among the “poor whitey, dirt-eater” class are the outcroppings of the bad blood sent from England in convict-ships? Ought an old country to sow the fertile soil of a colony with such noxious seed?

===Chinese===
他终于爬起来，穿上衣裳，在梯子顶上坐下，俯望琼斯家和明斯家地界之间那片蓝草牧场。地上铺着一层月光，白花花的。他睡不着。何不出去走走？走一走，兴许就困了。而一旦定了要走，往哪个方向走，他一刻也不曾犹豫。蓝草牧场（这岂不正像伊甸园么？）就在眼前；那棵羽叶槭，也正望得见。翻过栅栏、顺小路下山、蹚过小溪，说做就做，念头一转人已经到了。再站到羽叶槭底下，再翻过远处那道栅栏，顺着大路朝校舍走去——这件事又轻巧又快活，不假思索就做成了。拉尔夫原是个性急的人：凡是他看不出有什么不对的事，他向来不盘算就由着性子去做。何况这样同星星做伴，又有那一段快活散步的回忆垫底，比弗拉特克里克平平常常的日子强出太多，他便满腔热忱，一头扎进了这场夜游。

他终于站到了那条小沟里——他同汉娜结伴同行的地方。就在这块地方，几天前的一个早晨，肖基也碰到过他。他倚着栅栏，想再解一解汉娜苦处的谜。她有苦处，这他不疑；他也不疑，只要探明苦处是什么，他就帮得上她。可是他没有头绪。正想得出神，他听见大路上传来一阵马蹄的橐橐声。直到那一刻，他才觉出自己的孤单来。他缩回栅栏角上。来的是飞跑的三骑，其中一个身影拉尔夫觉得眼熟，却认不出是谁；那匹马他也想不起见过——一匹栗色马，左前脚一只白蹄，鼻子也是白的。那些人看见了他，略略勒了勒马。他自己也说不清，这几个人在场为什么叫他心惊，可一阵说不清道不明的惧意攫住了他。他们纵马跑远了，他还站在原地，一阵阵神经质地发抖。寒气像是钻进了骨头缝。他想起来，弗拉特克里克和克利夫蒂溪一带，向来背着贼患的名声，干的就是偷马、破门两类营生。自从默雷尔（Murrell）那伙联帮匪众因头目之死陷于瘫痪的那天起，南印第安纳和伊利诺伊的部分地区，还有艾奥瓦、密苏里、肯塔基和西南各地，就一直有亡命徒的匪帮存在。边境的凶悍之风，正是从这些材料里长出来的；两三年前被私刑吊死的雷诺帮那九个成员，16正是丹尼尔·布恩时代流进西部的那股坏血的余孽。我何妨不说：至今仍在「穷白人、吃泥巴」阶层里找得到的这些匪帮，就是当年英国用苦役犯船运出来的坏血，在地皮上露的头？一个老牌国家，该拿这样的毒种，去撒殖民地的沃土么？

===Original===
Before Ralph was able to move, he heard the hoofs of another horse striking upon the hard ground in an easy pace. The rider was Dr. Small. He checked his horse in a cool way, and stood still a few seconds while he scrutinized Ralph. Then he rode on, keeping the same easy gait as before, Ralph had a superstitious horror of Henry Small. And, shuddering with cold, he crept like a thief over the fence, past the tree, through the pasture, back to Pete Jones’s, never once thinking of the eyes that looked out of the window at Means’s. Climbing the ladder, he got into bed, and shook as with the ague. He tried to reason himself out of the foolish terror that possessed him, but he could not.

Half an hour later he heard a latch raised. Were the robbers breaking into the house below? He heard a soft tread upon the floor. Should he rise and give the alarm? Something restrained him. He reflected that a robber would be sure to stumble over some of the “brats.” So he lay still and finally slumbered, only awakening when the place in which he slept was full of the smoke of frying grease from the room below.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫还没来得及挪步，又听见一匹马的蹄子踏在硬地上，步子从容。骑马的是斯莫尔医生。他不动声色地勒住马，站了几秒钟，端详拉尔夫。随后照旧用方才那副从容的步子走远了。拉尔夫对亨利·斯莫尔怀着一种近乎迷信的恐惧。他冷得直打战，贼似的翻过栅栏，绕过那棵树，穿过牧场，回到皮特·琼斯家，压根儿没想到明斯家那扇窗户后头，正有一双眼睛望着他。爬上梯子钻进被窝，他抖得像打摆子。他想同自己讲道理，把攫住他的那股傻恐怖赶出去，可是赶不出去。

半个钟头以后，他听见门闩抬了一下。是贼在撬楼下的门么？他听见地板上一阵软软的脚步。要不要起来喊人？有什么东西拦住了他。他一想，贼准会叫那些「崽子」绊个跟头。于是他躺着没动，终于睡着，一觉醒来，只觉他睡的这一间已经灌满了楼下屋里煎油的烟。

===Original===
At breakfast Pete Jones scowled. He was evidently angry about something. He treated Ralph with a rudeness not to be overlooked, as if he intended to bring on a quarrel. Hartsook kept cool, and wished he could drive from his mind all memory of the past night. Why should men on horseback have any significance to him? He was trying to regard things in this way, and from a general desire to keep on good terms with his host he went to the stable to offer his services in helping to feed the stock.

“Don’t want no saft-handed help!” was all he got in return for his well-meant offer. But just as he turned to leave the stable he saw what made him tremble again. There was the same sorrel horse with a white left forefoot and a white nose.

===Chinese===
早饭桌上，皮特·琼斯沉着脸。他显然在为什么事动气，待拉尔夫那份粗鲁没法视而不见，像是有意要挑起一场口角。哈茨克沉住气，只巴不得把昨夜的种种从脑子里赶尽杀绝。骑马的人，跟他有什么相干？他正这么劝着自己，又出于同东道主和睦相处的一般心愿，走到马棚去，想尽一份力，帮着喂喂牲口。

「俺不用软手软脚的帮工！」——他这份好意换来的，就这一句。可就在他转身要走出马棚的时候，他又看见了叫他又是一抖的东西：还是那匹栗色马，左前脚一只白蹄，鼻子也是白的。

===Original===
To shake off his nervousness, Ralph started to school before the time. But, plague upon plagues! Mirandy Means, who had seen him leave Pete Jones’s, started just in time to join him where he came into the big road. Ralph was not in a good humor after his wakeful night, and to be thus dogged by Mirandy did not help the matter. So he found himself speaking crabbedly to the daughter of the leading trustee, in spite of himself.

“Hanner’s got a bad cold this mornin’ from bein’ out last night, and she can’t come to spellin’-school tonight,” began Mirandy, in her most simpering voice.

===Chinese===
为了抖掉这一身神经质，拉尔夫提前动身上学去了。可是祸不单行！米兰迪·明斯瞧见他出了皮特·琼斯家的门，掐着时辰出发，正好在他上大路的地方赶上他。一夜没合眼的拉尔夫本就没好气，叫米兰迪这么盯梢，气更是不打一处来。于是他发觉自己话里带刺，冲着这位首席学董的千金说话，想拦都拦不住。

「汉娜昨儿夜里出去了，今儿早上得了重感冒，晚上来不了拼字学校了。」米兰迪拿她那最嗲的腔调开了头。

===Original===
Ralph had forgotten that there was to be another spelling-school. It seemed to him an age since the orthographical conflict of the past night. This remark of Mirandy’s fell upon his ear like an echo from the distant past. He had lived a lifetime since, and was not sure that he was the same man who was spelling for dear life against Jim Phillips twelve hours before. But he was sorry to hear that Hannah had a cold. It seemed to him, in his depressed state, that he was to blame for it. In fact, it seemed to him that he was to blame for a good many things. He seemed to have been committing sins in spite of himself. Broken nerves and sleepless nights often result in a morbid conscience. And what business had he to wander over this very road at two o’clock in the morning, and to see three galloping horsemen, one of them on a horse with a white left forefoot and a white nose? What business had he watching Dr. Small as he went home from the bedside of a dying patient near daylight in the morning? And because he felt guilty he felt cross with Mirandy, and to her remark about Hannah he only replied that “Hannah was a smart girl.”

“Yes,” said Mirandy, “Bud thinks so.”

“Does he?” said Ralph.

“I should say so. What’s him and her been a-courtin’ fer for a year ef he didn’t think she was smart? Marm don’t like it; but ef Bud and her does, and they seem to, I don’t see as it’s marm’s lookout.”

===Chinese===
拉尔夫忘了今晚还有一场拼字学校。昨夜那场拼字的鏖战，在他恍如隔世。米兰迪这句话落进他耳朵，像极远极远的过去传来的回声。打那以后他又活了一辈子，都拿不准自己还是不是十二个钟头前拼死拼活跟吉姆·菲利普斯对拼的那一个了。可听说汉娜着了凉，他心里不好受。在他那消沉的境地，他觉着这都得怪他。其实他觉着好些事都得怪他。他像是身不由己，一直在造下罪孽。崩坏的神经、不眠的长夜，常常养出一颗病态的良心。再说，他凭什么半夜两点在这条道上晃荡，看见三骑飞驰，其中一骑的马左前脚一只白蹄、鼻子也是白的？他又凭什么天蒙蒙亮还瞅着斯莫尔医生从一个垂死病人的床边回家？他心里发虚，于是对米兰迪更存着气；听到她提汉娜，他只回了一句：「汉娜是个伶俐的姑娘。」

「可不，」米兰迪说，「巴德就这么想。」

「是么？」拉尔夫说。

「俺瞅可不是。他要是不觉着她伶俐，跟她处了一年的对象，图个啥？俺娘不乐意；可巴德跟她乐意——他俩瞅着是真乐意——俺就瞅不出这归俺娘哪门子管。」

===Original===
When one is wretched, there is a pleasure in being entirely wretched. Ralph felt that he must have committed some unknown crime, and that some Nemesis was following him. Was Hannah deceitful? At least, if she were not, he felt sure that he could supplant Bud. But what right had he to supplant Bud?

“Did you hear the news?” cried Shocky, running out to meet him. “The Dutchman’s house was robbed last night.”

Ralph thought of the three men on horseback, and to save his life he could not help associating Dr. Small with them. And then he remembered the sorrel horse with the left forefoot and muzzle white, and he recalled the sound he had heard as of the lifting of a latch. And it really seemed to him that in knowing what he did he was in some sense guilty of the robbery.

===Chinese===
人到了倒霉的时候，索性倒霉个透，里头也有一种快活。拉尔夫觉着，自己准是造下了什么不知道的罪孽，有什么报应在身后紧追。汉娜虚诈么？至少，她若不虚诈，他拿得准自己能把巴德挤掉。可他有什么权利挤掉巴德？

「听着信儿没有？」肖基跑出来迎他，喊道。「『荷兰人』家昨儿夜里叫人抢啦。」

拉尔夫想起那三骑，拼了命也没法不把斯莫尔医生同他们连到一块儿。跟着他又想起那匹左前脚和口鼻皆白的栗色马，又想起他听见的那一声，像门闩被抬了起来。他当真觉得，自己既然知道这些，在这桩劫案里，也就多少有了份儿罪。

## I A Private Lesson from a Bulldog / 一 斗牛犬的私人一课

===Original===
“Want to be a schoolmaster, do you? You? Well, what would *you* do in Flat Crick deestrick, *I’d* like to know? Why, the boys have driv off the last two, and licked the one afore them like blazes. You might teach a summer school, when nothin’ but children come. But I ’low it takes a right smart *man* to be schoolmaster in Flat Crick in the winter. They’d pitch you out of doors, sonny, neck and heels, afore Christmas.”

The young man, who had walked ten miles to get the school in this district, and who had been mentally reviewing his learning at every step he took, trembling lest the committee should find that he did not know enough, was not a little taken aback at this greeting from “old Jack Means,” who was the first trustee that he lighted on. The impression made by these ominous remarks was emphasized by the glances which he received from Jack Means’s two sons. The older one eyed him from the top of his brawny shoulders with that amiable look which a big dog turns on a little one before shaking him. Ralph Hartsook had never thought of being measured by the standard of muscle. This notion of beating education into young savages in spite of themselves dashed his ardor.

He had walked right to where Jack Means was at work shaving shingles in his own front yard. While Mr. Means was making the speech which we have set down above, and punctuating it with expectorations, a large brindle bulldog had been sniffing at Ralph’s heels, and a girl in a new linsey-woolsey dress, standing by the door, had nearly giggled her head off at the delightful prospect of seeing a new schoolteacher eaten up by the ferocious brute.

The disheartening words of the old man, the immense muscles of the young man who was to be his rebellious pupil, the jaws of the ugly bulldog, and the heartless giggle of the girl, gave Ralph a delightful sense of having precipitated himself into a den of wild beasts. Faint with weariness and discouragement, and shivering with fear, he sat down on a wheelbarrow.

===Chinese===
「想当教书先生，是吧？就凭你？嘿，俺倒要问问，*你*能在弗拉特克里克学区干出个啥名堂？前头那两个，都叫小子们撵跑了，再前头那个，挨了顿火烧火燎的狠揍。夏天学堂你兴许教得了，来的光是小娃娃。可俺寻思，要在弗拉特克里克当冬天学堂的老师，得是个响当当的*男子汉*。不等过圣诞，他们就得揪着你脖领子脚脖子，把你倒提着扔出门去，小子。」

年轻人是走了十英里路来谋这个学区的教职的，一路走一路在心里把满肚子的学问过了一遍又一遍，直打哆嗦，生怕委员会查出他懂得不够。这厢一见面，「老杰克·明斯」——他撞见的第一位学董——就迎头来了这么一句欢迎辞，叫他好不措手不及。这番不祥之言留下的印象，又叫杰克·明斯家两个儿子的目光加重了几分。大的那个，从他那一副虎背熊腰的高处睨着他，带着大狗抖小狗之前惯有的那副和善眼色。拉尔夫·哈茨克从没想过，自己有朝一日要拿筋肉当尺子量。要把学问硬打进一群年少野人的脑袋里、还由不得他们——这个念头，叫他的一腔热望凉了半截。

他是一路直走到杰克·明斯干活的跟前 的——明斯正在自家前院刮木瓦。明斯先生发表我们上文记下的那篇演说、又拿吐痰当标点的当儿，一条虎斑大斗牛犬一直在嗅拉尔夫的脚后跟；门口还站着一个穿新麻毛混纺裙子的姑娘，一想到眼看要瞧见一位新老师葬身恶犬之口的美妙前景，险些把脑袋笑掉下来。

老人那番泄气话，那个日后要当他顽劣学生的小伙子的一身蛮肉，那条丑斗牛犬的上下牙，加上姑娘没心没肺的嗤笑，给了拉尔夫一种妙不可言的感觉：自己一头扎进了兽笼。他又累又丧，头昏眼花，又怕得发抖，便在一只独轮车上坐了下来。

===Original===
“You, Bull!” said the old man to the dog, which was showing more and more a disposition to make a meal of the incipient pedagogue, “you, Bull! git aout,1 you pup!” The dog walked sullenly off, but not until he had given Ralph a look full of promise of what he meant to do when he got a good chance. Ralph wished himself back in the village of Lewisburg, whence he had come.

“You see,” continued Mr. Means, spitting in a meditative sort of a way, “you see, we a’n’t none of your saft sort in these diggin’s. It takes a *man* to boss this deestrick. Howsumdever, ef you think you kin trust your hide in Flat Crick schoolhouse I ha’n’t got no ’bjection. But ef you git licked, don’t come on us. Flat Crick don’t pay no ’nsurance, you bet! Any other trustees? Wal, yes. But as I pay the most taxes, t’others jist let me run the thing. You can begin right off a Monday. They a’n’t been no other applications. You see, it takes grit to apply for this school. The last master had a black eye for a month. But, as I wuz sayin’, you can jist roll up and wade in. I ’low you’ve got spunk, maybe, and that goes for a heap sight more’n sinnoo with boys. Walk in, and stay over Sunday with me. You’ll hev’ to board roun’, and I guess you better begin here.”

===Chinese===
「你，布尔！」老头子对狗喝道——那狗拿这位初出茅庐的教书匠当一顿饭的心思，越来越露骨了——「你，布尔！滚出去1，你个小崽子！」狗悻悻地走开了，可临走还丢给拉尔夫一眼，满眼都是「等俺逮着好机会，有你好瞧」的许诺。拉尔夫直巴望自己还待在他来的刘易斯堡村子里。

「你瞧，」明斯先生接着说，若有所思地吐了一口，「你瞧，俺们这地界的人，没一个是你那路软骨头。要镇住这个学区，得是个*男子汉*。话又说回来，你要是信得过你那张皮，敢进弗拉特克里克的校舍，俺也没啥不乐意的。可你要是挨了揍，别来寻俺们。弗拉特克里克可不出保险费，这个你放心！还有别的学董没？呃，有。可俺缴的税最多，旁人就都由着俺当家。你礼拜一直接开工就得。旁人一个来应这差事的都没有。你瞧，应这个学堂的差，得有点胆水。上回那位老师，乌眼青挂了一个月。不过，俺方才说了，你只管挽起袖子蹚进来。俺瞅你兴许有点血性，对付小子们，这个可比筋骨值钱得多啦。进来吧，上俺家过个礼拜天。你往后得轮流在各家搭伙，俺瞅你就打俺家起头。」

===Original===
Ralph did not go in, but sat out on the wheelbarrow, watching the old man shave shingles, while the boys split the blocks and chopped wood. Bull smelled of the newcomer again in an ugly way, and got a good kick from the older son for his pains. But out of one of his red eyes the dog warned the young schoolmaster that *he* should yet suffer for all kicks received on his account.

“Ef Bull once takes a holt, heaven and yarth can’t make him let go,” said the older son to Ralph, by way of comfort.

It was well for Ralph that he began to “board roun’” by stopping at Mr. Means’s. Ralph felt that Flat Creek was what he needed. He had lived a bookish life; but here was his lesson in the art of managing people, for he who can manage the untamed and strapping youths of a winter school in Hoopole County has gone far toward learning one of the hardest of lessons. And in Ralph’s time, things were worse than they are now. The older son of Mr. Means was called Bud Means. What his real name was, Ralph could not find out, for in many of these families the nickname of “Bud” given to the oldest boy, and that of “Sis,” which is the birthright of the oldest girl, completely bury the proper Christian name. Ralph saw his first strategic point, which was to capture Bud Means.

After supper, the boys began to get ready for something. Bull stuck up his ears in a dignified way, and the three or four yellow curs who were Bull’s satellites yelped delightedly and discordantly.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫没有进去，只在外头独轮车上坐着，看老头子刮木瓦，看两个小子劈木墩、砍柴。布尔又恶狠狠地过来嗅这位新客，为此挨了大小子结结实实一脚，算白辛苦。可它从一只红眼睛里警告这位年轻教师：为它挨的这些脚，往后都记在*他*的账上，一笔一笔要还。

「布尔一旦咬住了，老天爷和大地7也掰不开它的嘴。」大小子对拉尔夫说，算是安慰。

拉尔夫这「轮流搭伙」的头一站落在明斯先生家，倒是件好事。拉尔夫觉得，弗拉特克里克正是他缺的那一课。他过的向来是书本子里的日子；可这儿摆着的是一门驭人的功课——谁能驾驭胡普尔县冬天学堂里那帮没笼头的壮小子，谁就等于修完了天底下最难功课的一大半。何况在拉尔夫那时候，世道比现在还要糟些。明斯先生家的大小子，大家叫他巴德·明斯。他真名叫什么，拉尔夫打听不出来，因为这一带许多人家，大儿子的诨名「巴德」，还有大闺女生来就该的诨名「妹子」，把正经的教名埋得严严实实。拉尔夫看准了他的头一个战略要点：拿下巴德·明斯。

吃过晚饭，两个小子开始张罗起什么来。布尔庄重地竖起了耳朵，三四个围着它转的黄毛野狗，欢天喜地、南腔北调地吠成一片。

===Original===
“Bill,” said Bud Means to his brother, “ax the master ef he’d like to hunt coons. I’d like to take the starch out uv the stuck-up feller.”

“’Nough said,”2 was Bill’s reply.

“You durn’t3 do it,” said Bud.

“I don’t take no sech a dare,”4 returned Bill, and walked down to the gate, by which Ralph stood watching the stars come out, and half wishing he had never seen Flat Creek.

“I say, mister,” began Bill, “mister, they’s a coon what’s been a eatin’ our chickens lately, and we’re goin’ to try to ketch5 the varmint. You wouldn’t like to take a coon hunt nor nothin’, would you?”

“Why, yes,” said Ralph, “there’s nothing I should like better, if I could only be sure Bull wouldn’t mistake me for the coon.”

===Chinese===
「比尔，」巴德·明斯对弟弟说，「去问问老师想不想打浣熊。俺想煞煞那神气家伙的威风。」

「就这么着。2」比尔应道。

「你不敢。3」巴德说。

「这种激将，俺不吃。4」比尔答道，说着走向院门。拉尔夫正站在门边，看星星一颗一颗出来，一半巴望着这辈子没见过什么弗拉特克里克。

「俺说，先生，」比尔开了腔，「先生，有个浣熊，这些天老来吃俺们的鸡，俺们打算去逮5那家伙。你不想一块儿去打趟浣熊啥的，是不是？」

「咦，想啊，」拉尔夫说，「再没有比这更合我心意的了——只要拿得准布尔别把我错当成浣熊。」

===Original===
And so, as a matter of policy, Ralph dragged his tired legs eight or ten miles, on hill and in hollow, after Bud, and Bill, and Bull, and the coon. But the raccoon6 climbed a tree. The boys got into a quarrel about whose business it was to have brought the axe, and who was to blame that the tree could not be felled. Now, if there was anything Ralph’s muscles were good for, it was climbing. So, asking Bud to give him a start, he soon reached the limb above the one on which the raccoon was. Ralph did not know how ugly a customer a raccoon can be, and so got credit for more courage than he had. With much peril to his legs from the raccoon’s teeth, he succeeded in shaking the poor creature off among the yelping brutes and yelling boys. Ralph could not help sympathizing with the hunted animal, which sold its life as dearly as possible, giving the dogs many a scratch and bite. It seemed to him that he was like the raccoon, precipitated into the midst of a party of dogs who would rejoice in worrying *his* life out, as Bull and his crowd were destroying the poor raccoon. When Bull at last seized the raccoon and put an end to it, Ralph could not but admire the decided way in which he did it, calling to mind Bud’s comment, “Ef Bull once takes a holt, heaven and yarth7 can’t make him let go.”

But as they walked home, Bud carrying the raccoon by the tail, Ralph felt that his hunt had not been in vain. He fancied that even red-eyed Bull, walking uncomfortably close to his heels, respected him more since he had climbed that tree.

“Purty peart kind of a master,” remarked the old man to Bud, after Ralph had gone to bed. “Guess you better be a little easy on him. Hey?”

But Bud deigned no reply. Perhaps because he knew that Ralph heard the conversation through the thin partition.

===Chinese===
于是，出于方略，拉尔夫拖着他那两条累腿，翻山上沟，跟在巴德、比尔、布尔和浣熊后头，跑了八九到十英里。可浣熊6上了树。孩子们吵起架来，争的是带斧子该算谁的差、树砍不倒又怪谁。要说拉尔夫的筋肉天生顶得了什么用，那便是爬树。他请巴德托了他一把，不多时就够到了浣熊蹲的那根枝子上头的一根。拉尔夫不晓得浣熊是多大一号难缠的主顾，于是得了个比他实有更大的胆名。他冒着两条腿喂浣熊牙的危险，终于把那可怜东西抖了下去，正落在狂吠的狗群和嚎叫的孩子中间。拉尔夫忍不住心疼那被围猎的畜生：它把命卖得顶贵，给狗留了好些抓痕和咬口。他觉得自己就像那只浣熊，一个趔趄栽进了一群狗中间——那群狗巴望着把*他的*性命磨掉，正如布尔和它那一伙，眼下正在结果那只可怜的浣熊。等布尔终于咬住浣熊、送它归了西，拉尔夫不能不佩服它那副干脆利落，心里想起巴德的考语：「布尔一旦咬住了，老天爷和大地7也掰不开它的嘴。」

回家路上，巴德倒提着浣熊尾巴，拉尔夫觉得这一趟打猎没有白去。他恍惚觉得，连那条红眼睛的布尔——它贴着他的脚后跟走，走得人心里发毛——自打他爬了那棵树，也多敬了他三分。

「这老师，伶俐得很呐。」拉尔夫睡下之后，老头子对巴德评论道。「俺瞅你顶好对他手下留点情。嗯？」

可巴德不屑搭理。兴许是因为他知道，隔着一层薄板墙，这番话拉尔夫一字不落全听见了。

===Original===
Ralph woke delighted to find it raining. He did not want to hunt or fish on Sunday, and this steady rain would enable him to make friends with Bud. I do not know how he got started, but after breakfast he began to tell stories. Out of all the books he had ever read he told story after story. And “old man Means,” and “old *Miss* Means,” and Bud Means, and Bill Means, and Sis Means listened with great eyes while he told of Sinbad’s adventures, of the Old Man of the Sea, of Robinson Crusoe, of Captain Gulliver’s experiences in Liliput, and of Baron Munchausen’s exploits.

Ralph had caught his fish. The hungry minds of these backwoods people were refreshed with the new life that came to their imaginations in these stories. For there was but one book in the Means library, and that, a well-thumbed copy of *Captain Riley’s Narrative*, had long since lost all freshness.

“I’ll be dog-on’d,”8 said Bill, emphatically, “ef I hadn’t ’ruther hear the master tell them whoppin’ yarns than to go to a circus the best day I ever seed!” Bill could pay no higher compliment.

What Ralph wanted was to make a friend of Bud. It’s a nice thing to have the seventy-four-gun ship on your own side, and the more Hartsook admired the knotted muscles of Bud Means the more he desired to attach him to himself. So, whenever he struck out a peculiarly brilliant passage, he anxiously watched Bud’s eye. But the young Philistine kept his own counsel. He listened, but said nothing, and the eyes under his shaggy brows gave no sign. Ralph could not tell whether those eyes were deep and inscrutable or only stolid. Perhaps a little of both. When Monday morning came, Ralph was nervous. He walked to school with Bud.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫一觉醒来，见下着雨，喜出望外。他本就不想在礼拜天打猎钓鱼，这场稳稳当当的雨，正好成全他同巴德交朋友。他怎么起的头，我不知道，反正早饭后他就开讲起来。他把读过的书翻了个底朝天，一个故事接一个故事地讲。「明斯老头子」、「明斯*老*太太」、巴德·明斯、比尔·明斯、「妹子」明斯，全都瞪圆了大眼睛听：听辛巴达的奇遇，听海上老人的故事，听鲁滨逊·克鲁索，听格列佛船长在小人国的经历，听明希豪森男爵的壮举。

拉尔夫钓着他的鱼了。这些林莽人家饥渴的心智，被故事灌进想象里的一股新鲜活水，浇了个透。要知道明斯家的藏书统共一本，还是那本翻烂了的《赖利船长航海记》，新鲜劲儿早没了。

「叫俺天打雷劈8，」比尔加重了语气说，「俺这辈子看马戏看过的顶好的日子，也比不上听老师讲这些个牛皮故事过瘾！」比尔能捧出的最高的好话，也就到这一句了。

拉尔夫要的，是同巴德交成朋友。七十四炮的大战船肯站在你这一边，总是件美事；哈茨克越看巴德·明斯那一疙瘩一疙瘩的筋肉入眼，越想把他拢到自己身边来。于是每逢讲到一段格外出彩的地方，他就眼巴巴地瞟巴德的眼色。可这位年轻的非利士人不露一点口风。他听，可不说，乱眉毛底下那双眼睛不透一丝消息。拉尔夫说不清那双眼是深沉莫测呢，还是不过木然。兴许两样都占一点。礼拜一早晨到了，拉尔夫心头发慌。他同巴德一道走路上学。

===Original===
“I guess you’re a little skeered by what the old man said, a’n’t you?”

Ralph was about to deny it, but on reflection concluded that it was best to speak the truth. He said that Mr. Means’s description of the school had made him feel a little downhearted.

“What will you do with the tough boys? You a’n’t no match for ’em.” And Ralph felt Bud’s eyes not only measuring his muscles, but scrutinizing his countenance. He only answered:

“I don’t know.”

“What would you do with me, for instance?” and Bud stretched himself up as if to shake out the reserve power coiled up in his great muscles.

“I shan’t have any trouble with you.”

===Chinese===
「俺瞅，老头子那些话把你吓着了点，是不是？」

拉尔夫正要否认，转念一想，觉得还是照实说的好。他说，明斯先生对这学堂的那番描画，叫他心里有点发蔫。

「那些愣小子你咋办？你可不是他们的对手。」拉尔夫觉出巴德那双眼不但在量他的筋骨，还在端详他的脸色。他只答道：

「不知道。」

「比方说，拿俺咋办？」巴德说着，挺直了身子，像要把盘在那身大筋肉里的后劲抖擞出来。

「你我之间，不会有麻烦。」

===Original===
“Why, I’m the wust chap of all. I thrashed the last master, myself.”

And again the eyes of Bud Means looked out sharply from his shadowing brows to see the effect of this speech on the slender young man.

“You won’t thrash me, though,” said Ralph.

“Pshaw! I ’low I could whip you in an inch of your life with my left hand, and never half try,” said young Means, with a threatening sneer.

“I know that as well as you do.”

“Well, a’n’t you afraid of me, then?” and again he looked sidewise at Ralph.

===Chinese===
「嘿，俺就是里头顶坏的那号。上回那个老师，就是俺亲手揍的。」

巴德·明斯那双眼又从浓眉毛底下尖锐地探出来，看这句话在这位瘦削的年轻人身上起了什么作用。

「可你不会揍我。」拉尔夫说。

「啐！俺寻思俺单用左手，半点力气不费，就能把你揍个半死。」小明斯说，带着威吓的冷笑。

「这个，我跟你一样明白。」

「那么，你不怕俺？」他又斜眼瞅拉尔夫。

===Original===
“Not a bit,” said Ralph, wondering at his own courage.

They walked on in silence a minute. Bud was turning the matter over.

“Why a’n’t you afraid of me?” he said presently.

“Because you and I are going to be friends.”

“And what about t’others?”

“I am not afraid of all the other boys put together.”

“You a’n’t! The mischief! How’s that?”

===Chinese===
「一点也不怕。」拉尔夫说，心里纳罕自己哪来的胆子。

两人闷头走了一分钟。巴德在心里翻来覆去掂量这事。

「你咋就不怕俺？」过了一会儿他说。

「因为你我要做朋友。」

「那旁人呢？」

「其余的孩子全加在一起，我也不怕。」

「不怕！见鬼！这是咋说的？」

===Original===
“Well, I’m not afraid of them because you and I are going to be friends, and you can whip all of them together. You’ll do the fighting and I’ll do the teaching.”

The diplomatic Bud only chuckled a little at this; whether he assented to the alliance or not Ralph could not tell.

When Ralph looked round on the faces of the scholars—the little faces full of mischief and curiosity, the big faces full of an expression which was not further removed than second-cousin from contempt—when when young Hartsook looked into these faces, his heart palpitated with stage-fright. There is no audience so hard to face as one of schoolchildren, as many a man has found to his cost. Perhaps it is that no conventional restraint can keep down their laughter when you do or say anything ridiculous.

Hartsook’s first day was hurried and unsatisfactory. He was not of himself, and consequently not master of anybody else. When evening came, there were symptoms of insubordination through the whole school. Poor Ralph was sick at heart. He felt that if there had ever been the shadow of an alliance between himself and Bud, it was all “off” now. It seemed to Hartsook that even Bull had lost his respect for the teacher. Half that night the young man lay awake. At last comfort came to him. A reminiscence of the death of the raccoon flashed on him like a vision. He remembered that quiet and annihilating bite which Bull gave. He remembered Bud’s certificate, that “Ef Bull once takes a holt, heaven and yarth can’t make him let go.” He thought that what Flat Creek needed was a bulldog. He would be a bulldog, quiet, but invincible. He would take hold in such a way that nothing should make him let go. And then he went to sleep.

In the morning Ralph got out of bed slowly. He put his clothes on slowly. He pulled on his boots in a bulldog mood. He tried to move as he thought Bull would move if he were a man. He ate with deliberation, and looked everybody in the eyes with a manner that made Bud watch him curiously. He found himself continually comparing himself with Bull. He found Bull possessing a strange fascination for him. He walked to school alone, the rest having gone on before. He entered the schoolroom preserving a cool and dogged manner. He saw in the eyes of the boys that there was mischief brewing. He did not dare sit down in his chair for fear of a pin. Everybody looked solemn. Ralph lifted the lid of his desk. “Bow-wow! wow-wow!” It was the voice of an imprisoned puppy, and the school giggled and then roared. Then everything was quiet.

===Chinese===
「这个嘛，我不怕他们，是因为你我要做朋友，而他们全绑在一块儿也打不过你。动手归你，教书归我。」

老练的巴德只嘿嘿笑了一小声；这同盟他应没应下，拉尔夫看不出。

拉尔夫环视学生们的脸——小脸蛋满是调皮和好奇，大脸盘子挂满一种离轻蔑顶多隔着个隔山二表哥的表情——年轻的哈茨克望进这一张张脸，心里怦怦直跳，害起怯场来。天底下最难面对的看客，莫过于一屋子学童，多少人吃了亏才明白这一点。兴许是因为，你一举一动、一言一语，只要可笑，任什么礼法拘束也按不住他们的哄笑。

哈茨克头一天过得仓皇而不如意。他不在状态，因而不但做不了自己的主，更做不了任何人的主。到了傍晚，全校都露出犯上的苗头。可怜的拉尔夫心里发苦。他觉得，自己同巴德之间那点同盟的影子，就算有过，如今也全「黄」了。在哈茨克看来，连布尔都对这位老师失了敬意。那晚年轻人半宿没合眼。后来，安慰终于来了。浣熊之死的回忆，像幻象一样闪过他眼前。他想起布尔那一口——不出声、却干净彻底的一口。他想起巴德的那纸鉴定：「布尔一旦咬住了，老天爷和大地也掰不开它的嘴。」他想，弗拉特克里克缺的，是一条斗牛犬。他就来做这条斗牛犬：不出声，可打不垮。他要咬得那样牢，任什么都别想掰开。想着想着，他睡着了。

早晨，拉尔夫慢吞吞地下了床，慢吞吞地穿衣裳，憋着一股斗牛犬的劲头蹬上靴子。他试着照布尔的样子动作——要是布尔是个人，想必就是这么动的。他不慌不忙地吃饭，直直地看着每个人的眼睛，那副做派引得巴德好奇地打量他。他发觉自己不住地拿自己同布尔比，还发觉布尔对他有一股说不出的魔力。别人先走了，他独自一人上学去。他走进教室，端着一副冷静而执拗的做派。他从孩子们的眼里看出，有一场淘气正在酝酿。他不敢往椅子上坐，怕椅子上有图钉。人人一脸庄重。拉尔夫掀开讲桌的盖子。「汪汪！汪汪！」是一只被囚的小狗崽的嗓音，全校先嗤嗤，后哄堂。随后，一切归于安静。

===Original===
The scholars expected an outburst of wrath from the teacher. For they had come to regard the whole world as divided into two classes, the teacher on the one side representing lawful authority, and the pupils on the other in a state of chronic rebellion. To play a trick on the master was an evidence of spirit; to “lick” the master was to be the crowned hero of Flat Creek district. Such a hero was Bud Means; and Bill, who had less muscle, saw a chance to distinguish himself on a teacher of slender frame. Hence the puppy in the desk.

Ralph Hartsook grew red in the face when he saw the puppy. But the cool, repressed, bulldog mood in which he had kept himself saved him. He lifted the dog into his arms and stroked him until the laughter subsided. Then, in a solemn and set way, he began:

“I am sorry,” and he looked round the room with a steady, hard eye—everybody felt that there was a conflict coming—“I am sorry that any scholar in this school could be so mean”—the word was uttered with a sharp emphasis, and all the big boys felt sure that there would be a fight with Bill Means, and perhaps with Bud—“could be so *mean*—as to—shut up his *brother* in such a place as that!”

There was a long, derisive laugh. The wit was indifferent, but by one stroke Ralph had carried the whole school to his side. By the significant glances of the boys, Hartsook detected the perpetrator of the joke, and with the hard and dogged look in his eyes, with just such a look as Bull would give a puppy, but with the utmost suavity in his voice, he said:

“William Means, will you be so good as to put this dog out of doors?”

===Chinese===
学生们等着老师大发雷霆。因为他们早把全世界看成两等人：老师算一等人，代表官家的威权；学生算另一等，处于常年造反的状态。捉弄老师，是有血性的凭证；「揍」了老师，就是弗拉特克里克学区戴冠的英雄。这样的英雄，就是巴德·明斯；筋骨差些的比尔，则看准了机会，要在一位瘦条老师身上显一显身手。讲桌里那只小狗崽，就是这么来的。

一看见小狗崽，拉尔夫·哈茨克脸涨红了。可他一路给自己端着的那副冷静、压抑的斗牛犬心绪救了他。他把狗抱进怀里，抚它，一直抚到笑声平息。然后，他拿出一副郑重其事的腔调，开了口：

「我很难过，」他拿一双又稳又硬的眼睛扫视全屋——人人都觉出一场冲突要来了——「我很难过，这学堂里竟有学生能这么下作」——这个词咬得极重，大男孩们个个拿准，这一架跟比尔·明斯是打定了，兴许还得搭上巴德——「能这么*下作*——以至于——把自己的*亲弟弟*关在这么个地方！」

起了一长串哄笑。这俏皮话本不高明，可就这一手，拉尔夫把全校拉到了自己这边。从孩子们彼此使的眼色里，哈茨克认出了这场玩笑的主犯，便拿眼里那副又硬又拗的神情——正是布尔看小狗崽的那种神情——嘴里却用十二分的和气，说道：

「威廉·明斯，劳你的驾，把这条狗放到门外去，好不好？」

## II A Spell Coming / 二 一场风波将起

===Original===
There was a moment of utter stillness; but the magnetism of Ralph’s eye was too much for Bill Means. The request was so polite, the master’s look was so innocent and yet so determined. Bill often wondered afterward that he had not “fit” rather than obeyed the request. But somehow he put the dog out. He was partly surprised, partly inveigled, partly awed into doing just what he had not intended to do. In the week that followed, Bill had to fight half a dozen boys for calling him “Puppy Means.” Bill said he wished he’d licked the master on the spot. ’Twould ’a’ saved five fights out of the six.

And all that day and the next, the bulldog in the master’s eye was a terror to evildoers. At the close of school on the second day Bud was heard to give it as his opinion that “the master wouldn’t be much in a tussle, but he had a heap of thunder and lightning in him.”

Did he inflict corporal punishment? inquired some philanthropic friend. Would you inflict corporal punishment if you were tiger-trainer in Van Amburgh’s happy family? But poor Ralph could never satisfy his constituency in this regard.

===Chinese===
有那么一瞬全场死寂；可拉尔夫眼里的磁力，叫比尔·明斯招架不住。请求那么客气，老师的神情那么无辜，却又那么坚决。比尔后来常常纳闷，自己怎么没当场「干一架」，反倒听了话。反正不知怎么的，他把狗弄出去了。他一半是猝不及防，一半是被哄住了，一半是被镇住了，做的偏偏是他原本不打算做的事。接下来那个礼拜，比尔为「狗崽子明斯」这个外号，跟六个男孩子打了架。比尔说，早知如此，不如当场就把老师狠揍一顿——那样六场架能省下五场。

那一整天加第二天，老师眼里那条斗牛犬，一直叫存心捣蛋的胆战心惊。第二天放学的时候，有人听见巴德发表他的高见：「老师真交起手来怕是不咋的，可他肚里的雷火多着咧。」

「他动不动就体罚？」哪位慈悲为怀的朋友要问了。可你要是去当范·安伯格（Van Amburgh）「快乐一家子」里的驯虎人，你会不会动用体罚呢？可怜的拉尔夫在这一条上，永远没法叫他选区的选民称心。

===Original===
“Don’t believe he’ll do,” was Mr. Pete Jones’s comment to Mr. Means. “Don’t thrash enough. Boys won’t l’arn ’less you thrash ’em, says I. Leastways, mine won’t. Lay it on good is what I says to a master. Lay it on good. Don’t do no harm. Lickin’ and l’arnin’ goes together. No lickin’, no l’arnin’, says I. Lickin’ and l’arnin’, lickin’ and larnin’, is the good ole way.”

And Mr. Jones, like some wiser people, was the more pleased with his formula that it had an alliterative sound. Nevertheless, Ralph was master from this time until the spelling-school came. If only it had not been for that spelling-school! Many and many a time after the night of the fatal spelling-school Ralph used to say, “If only it had not been for that spelling-school!”

===Chinese===
「瞅他不中。」皮特·琼斯先生对明斯先生这么评论。「揍得不够。不打不成器，俺说的。反正俺家那几个，不打不成器。俺跟老师就交代一句：下手要狠。下手狠狠的。不碍事。挨揍跟开窍是一路货。不挨揍，不开窍，俺说的。挨揍开窍，挨揍开窍，老规矩顶用。」

琼斯先生跟某些更有学问的人一样，正因为这口诀念着押韵合辙，越发自鸣得意。尽管如此，从这时起直到拼字学校那晚来临之前，拉尔夫稳稳当当是这学校的主人。要不是那场拼字学校就好了！在那要命的拼字学校之夜以后，拉尔夫不知多少回念叨：「要不是那场拼字学校就好了！」

===Original===
There had to be a spelling-school. Not only for the sake of my story, which would not have been worth the telling if the spelling-school had not taken place, but because Flat Creek district had to have a spelling-school. It is the only public literary exercise known in Hoopole County. It takes the place of lyceum lecture and debating club. Sis Means, or, as she wished now to be called, Mirandy Means, expressed herself most positively in favor of it. She said that she ’lowed the folks in that district couldn’t in no wise do without it. But it was rather to its social than to its intellectual benefits that she referred. For all the spelling-schools ever seen could not enable her to stand anywhere but at the foot of the class. There is one branch diligently taught in a backwoods school. The public mind seems impressed with the difficulties of English orthography, and there is a solemn conviction that the chief end of man is to learn to spell. “‘Know *Webster’s Elementary*’ came down from Heaven,” would be the backwoods version of the Greek saying but that, unfortunately for the Greeks, their fame has not reached so far. It often happens that the pupil does not know the meaning of a single word in the lesson. This is of no consequence. What do you want to know the meaning of a word for? Words were made to be spelled, and men were probably created that they might spell them. Hence the necessity for sending a pupil through the spelling-book five times before you allow him to begin to read, or indeed to do anything else. Hence the necessity for those long spelling-classes at the close of each forenoon and afternoon session of the school, to stand at the head of which is the cherished ambition of every scholar. Hence, too, the necessity for devoting the whole of the afternoon session of each Friday to a “spelling-match.” In fact, spelling is the “national game” in Hoopole County. Baseball and croquet matches are as unknown as Olympian chariot-races. Spelling and shucking9 are the only public competitions.

So the fatal spelling-school had to be appointed for the Wednesday of the second week of the session, just when Ralph felt himself master of the situation. Not that he was without his annoyances. One of Ralph’s troubles in the week before the spelling-school was that he was loved. The other that he was hated.
