And while the time between the appointing of the spelling tournament and the actual occurrence of that remarkable event is engaged in elapsing, let me narrate two incidents that made it for Ralph a trying time.

===Chinese===
这场拼字学校是免不了的。不光是为了我这故事——没有这场拼字学校，故事就不值一讲——更因为弗拉特克里克学区非有拼字学校不可。这是胡普尔县所知道的唯一公共文学活动，讲演厅的讲座啦、辩论会啦，一概由它兼任。明斯家那位「妹子」——如今她愿意人家叫她米兰迪·明斯了——对此表态最是斩钉截铁。她说她「寻思」这学区的乡亲们断断少它不得。不过她指的多半是它的交际好处，而非益智好处。因为开天辟地以来所有拼字学校加在一起，也没能叫她站到班级末尾以外的任何位置。边远林区的学校里，有一门功课是勤勤恳恳教的。公众的心思似乎被英文拼写的难处镇住了，人人都庄严地相信：人生在世，头等大事就是学会拼字。希腊人的名言若按林区的说法，就得改成「认识《韦伯斯特基础拼写课本》乃是上天所赐」——只可惜希腊人的名气没传到那么远，委屈了他们。学生不认得课文中任何一个词的意思，是常有的事。这毫无关系。你要知道一个词的意思干什么？词造出来就是给人拼的，人被造出来，多半也就是为了拼它们。所以，非得叫一个学生把拼写课本过足五遍，才许他开始读书，或者当真去做别的什么事，这是必要的。所以，每天上午、下午放学之前的那些长长的拼字班是必要的——站上班级的头名，是每个学童珍藏于心的宏愿。所以，每个星期五把整个下午都拿来搞一场「拼字比赛」，也是必要的。事实上，拼写就是胡普尔县的「国民运动」。棒球和槌球比赛在这里闻所未闻，跟奥林匹亚战车赛一样稀罕。拼字和剥玉米9是仅有的两项公开竞技。

于是，这场要命的拼字学校就定在开学第二周的星期三，正是拉尔夫自觉大局在握的时候。倒不是说他没有烦心事。拼字学校前的那一个礼拜，拉尔夫的一桩烦恼是有人爱他，另一桩是有人恨他。趁这场拼字大赛从定下日子到那桩大事真正发生之间的时光正自流逝，且容我讲两件小事——正是它们，叫拉尔夫的日子不好过起来。

## XIII A Struggle for the Mastery / 十三 争夺主导权

===Original===
The school had closed on Monday evening as usual. The boys had been talking in knots all day. Nothing but the bulldog in the slender, resolute young master had kept down the rising storm. A teacher who has lost moral support at home, can not long govern a school. Ralph had effectually lost his popularity in the district, and the worst of it was that he could not divine from just what quarter the ill wind came, except that he felt sure of Small’s agency in it somewhere. Even Hannah had slighted him, when he called at Means’s on Monday morning to draw the pittance of pay that was due him.

He had expected a petition for a holiday on Christmas day. Such holidays are deducted from the teacher’s time, and it is customary for the boys to “turn out” the teacher who refuses to grant them, by barring him out of the schoolhouse on Christmas and New Year’s morning. Ralph had intended to grant a holiday if it should be asked, but it was not asked. Hank Banta was the ringleader in the disaffection, and he had managed to draw the surly Bud, who was present this morning, into it. It is but fair to say that Bud was in favor of making a request before resorting to extreme measures, but he was overruled. He gave it as his solemn opinion that the master was mighty peart, and they would be beat anyhow some way, but he would lick the master fer two cents ef he warn’t so slim that he’d feel like he was fighting a baby.

And all that day things looked black. Ralph’s countenance was cold and hard as stone, and Shocky trembled where he sat. Betsey Short tittered rather more than usual. A riot or a murder would have seemed amusing to her.

School was dismissed, and Ralph, instead of returning to the Squire’s, set out for the village of Clifty, a few miles away. No one knew what he went for, and some suggested that he had “sloped.”

===Chinese===
学校星期一傍晚照常放了学。男孩子们成堆成堆地嘀咕了一整天。全亏那位瘦削而果决的年轻老师眼里养着一条斗牛犬，才压住了那场越涌越高的风浪。一个在家里失了道义撑腰的老师，是管不长久一所学校的。拉尔夫在学区里的人缘算是丢尽了，糟糕的是他掐不准这股阴风打哪一面来——只有一条拿得稳：里头有斯莫尔的手脚。连汉娜也怠慢了他——星期一早上，他去明斯家领那点儿该他的束脩的时候。

他原等着他们来求圣诞节的假。这种假要从老师的工时里扣，而孩子们照老规矩，谁不肯放假，圣诞节和元旦一大早就把他「关在校门外」，撵他走。拉尔夫原打算只要开口就放假，可没人开口。汉克·班塔是这回哗变的主谋，他还设法把闷声不响的巴德——他今早也在场——拉了进去。凭良心说，巴德主张先礼后兵，先走个请求的过场再使非常手段，可被大伙儿压了下去。他郑重其事地发表意见说：老师精得很，横竖他们总有法子输；可只要老师别瘦成那副样子——瘦得叫他觉得像在打一个小娃娃——给两个铜子儿他也肯揍老师一顿。

那一整天，天色都是黑的。拉尔夫的脸又冷又硬，像石头，肖基坐在位子上直打哆嗦。贝特西·肖特比平时嗤嗤笑得更勤了。就是闹一场暴动、出一桩人命，在她看来也不过是个乐子。

放了学，拉尔夫没回治安官家，却动身往几英里外的克利夫蒂村子去了。没人知道他去干什么，有人说他「溜之大吉」了。

===Original===
But Bud said “he warn’t that air kind. He was one of them air sort as died in their tracks, was Mr. Hartsook. They’d find him on the ground nex’ morning, and he lowed the master war made of that air sort of stuff as would burn the dog-on’d ole schoolhouse to ashes, or blow it into splinters, but what he’d beat. Howsumdever he’d said he was a-goin’ to help, and help he would; but all the sinno in Golier wouldn’t be no account again the cute they was in the head of the master.”

But Bud, discouraged as he was with the fear of Ralph’s “cute,” went like a martyr to the stake and took his place with the rest in the schoolhouse at nine o’clock at night. It may have been Ralph’s intention to preoccupy the schoolhouse, for at ten o’clock Hank Banta was set shaking from head to foot at seeing a face that looked like the master’s at the window. He waked up Bud and told him about it.

“Well, what are you a-tremblin’ about, you coward?” growled Bud. “He won’t shoot you; but he’ll beat you at this game, I’ll bet a hoss, and me, too, and make us both as ’shamed of ourselves as dogs with tin-kittles to their tails. You don’t know the master, though he did duck you. But he’ll larn you a good lesson this time, and me too, like as not.” And Bud soon snored again, but Hank shook with fear every time he looked at the blackness outside the windows. He was sure he heard footfalls. He would have given anything to have been at home.

===Chinese===
可巴德说，「他不是那号人。哈茨克先生是那种死也死在道上、寸步不退的主儿。明儿一早，他们准能在原地找见他。俺寻思，老师是那种材料做的：不把他赢下来，他宁可把这该死的老校屋烧成灰、炸成碎片儿。话虽这么说，俺既然说过要帮，就得帮到底。可把歌利亚浑身的腱子肉都算上，也抵不住老师脑壳里的那点子机灵。」

可巴德虽然叫拉尔夫的「机灵」吓泄了气，还是像殉道者上刑场似的，晚上九点同大伙儿一起在校舍里各就各位。没准拉尔夫的本意就是先占住校舍，因为十点钟的时候，汉克·班塔看见窗户外头一张像老师的脸，吓得从头抖到脚。他推醒巴德，说给他听。

「嚎什么嚎，你抖个啥，胆小鬼？」巴德咕哝道。「他不会开枪打你；可这一局他要赢你——俺拿一匹马打赌，外搭上俺自个儿——还要叫咱俩臊得像尾巴上拴了铁皮桶的狗。你不了解老师，虽说他把你往水里按过。可这回他要好好给你上一课，八成还捎带上俺。」说罢巴德很快又打起了呼噜，汉克却每回望一眼窗外的黑影就抖一回。他拿得准自己听见了脚步声。给他什么他都愿意换，只要能待在家里。

===Original===
When morning came, the pupils began to gather early. A few boys who were likely to prove of service in the coming siege were admitted through the window, and then everything was made fast, and a “snack” was eaten.

“How do you ’low he’ll get in?” said Hank, trying to hide his fear.

“How do I ’low?” said Bud. “I don’t ’low nothin’ about it. You might as well ax me where I ’low the nex’ shootin’ star is a-goin’ to drap. Mr. Hartsook’s mighty onsartin. But he’ll git in, though, and tan your hide fer you, you see ef he don’t. *Ef* he don’t blow up the schoolhouse with gunpowder!” This last was thrown in by way of alleviating the fears of the cowardly Hank, for whom Bud had a great contempt.

The time for school had almost come. The boys inside were demoralized by waiting. They began to hope that the master had “sloped.” They dreaded to see him coming.

“I don’t believe he’ll come,” said Hank, with a cold shiver. “It’s past school-time.”

“Yes, he will come, too,” said Bud. “And he ’lows to come in here mighty quick. I don’t know how. But he’ll be a-standin’ at that air desk when it’s nine o’clock. I’ll bet a thousand dollars on that. *Ef* he don’t take it into his head to blow us up!” Hank was now white.

===Chinese===
天亮了，学生们早早聚了来。几个看来能在接下来的围守里派上用场的男孩，从窗户放了进去，然后门户一齐拴牢，吃了顿「点心」。

「你估摸他咋进来？」汉克说，想掩住心里的怕。

「俺估摸？」巴德说。「俺啥也不估摸。你不如问问俺，下颗流星要往哪块儿掉。哈茨克先生深不可测。可他进是进得来的，还要替你把皮剥下来紧一紧，不信你瞧。*除非*他拿火药把校屋崩了！」这末一句，是巴德特意添上，好宽宽胆小鬼汉克的心的——对汉克，他满心瞧不起。

上课的时候快到了。里头的男孩们等得军心涣散，开始指望老师真「溜了」。他们怕看见他来。

「我看他不来了，」汉克说，打了个冷颤。「过上课点儿了。」

「来，他准来，」巴德说。「而且他打谱儿马上就进这屋。咋进，俺不知道。可一到九点，他就站在那张讲桌后头。这话俺拿一千块钱打赌。*除非*他动念头，把咱们崩上天！」汉克这下脸都白了。

===Original===
Some of the parents came along, accidentally of course, and stopped to see the fun, sure that Bud would thrash the master if he tried to break in. Small, on the way to see a patient perhaps, reined up in front of the door. Still no Ralph. It was just five minutes before nine. A rumor now gained currency that he had been seen going to Clifty the evening before, and that he had not come back, though in fact Ralph had come back, and had slept at Squire Hawkins’s.

“There’s the master,” cried Betsey Short, who stood out in the road shivering and giggling alternately. For Ralph at that moment emerged from the sugar-camp by the schoolhouse, carrying a board.

“Ho! ho!” laughed Hank, “he thinks he’ll smoke us out. I guess he’ll find us ready.” The boys had let the fire burn down, and there was now nothing but hot hickory coals on the hearth.

“I tell you he’ll come in. He didn’t go to Clifty fer nothing,” said Bud, who sat still on one of the benches which leaned against the door. “I don’t know how, but they’s lots of ways of killing a cat besides chokin’ her with butter. He’ll come in—*ef* he don’t blow us all sky-high!”

Ralph’s voice was now heard, demanding that the door be opened.

“Let’s open her,” said Hank, turning livid with fear at the firm, confident tone of the master.

===Chinese===
有些家长也「碰巧」打这儿过，停下来看热闹，拿得准老师要是硬闯，巴德准揍他。斯莫尔呢，兴许是出诊去，在门前勒住了马。还是没有拉尔夫。差五分钟九点。这时有话传开了，说头天傍晚有人瞧见他去了克利夫蒂，一直没回来——其实拉尔夫回来了，在霍金斯治安官家过的夜。

「老师来啦！」贝特西·肖特喊道——她站在外头道上，一会儿发抖，一会儿傻笑。只见拉尔夫这时从校舍旁的熬糖棚里钻出来，手里拿着一块木板。

「嗬！嗬！」汉克笑道，「他想着把俺们熏出去呢。俺看他得发现俺们早有防备。」男孩们早把火压下去了，炉膛里如今只剩山核桃的热炭。

「俺跟你说，他进得来。他去克利夫蒂可不是白去的，」巴德说，他还坐在一条斜顶着门的长凳上。「咋进俺不知道，可杀猫的法子多着呢，不光拿黄油噎死它这一条。他进得来——*除非*他把咱们都崩上天！」

这时听得见拉尔夫的声音了，喝令把门打开。

「开门得了，」汉克说，老师那又稳又笃的口气，吓得他脸色铁青。

===Original===
Bud straightened himself up. “Hank, you’re a coward. I’ve got a mind to kick you. You got me into this blamed mess, and now you want to crawfish. You jest tech one of these ’ere fastenings, and I’ll lay you out flat of your back afore you can say Jack Robinson.”

The teacher was climbing to the roof with the board in hand.

“That air won’t win,” laughed Pete Jones outside. He saw that there was no smoke. Even Bud began to hope that Ralph would fail for once. The master was now on the ridgepole of the schoolhouse. He took a paper from his pocket, and deliberately poured the contents down the chimney.

Mr. Pete Jones shouted “Gunpowder!” and set off down the road to be out of the way of the explosion. Dr. Small remembered, probably, that his patient might die while he sat here, and started on.

===Chinese===
巴德直起了身子。「汉克，你是个胆小鬼。俺真想踹你。是你把俺拉进这摊烂事，如今你又想缩头。你敢碰一碰这些个门闩，俺就叫你仰面朝天躺个平，快得你都来不及喊一声『我的妈』。」

老师手里拿着那块木板，正往房顶上爬。

「那招不顶用，」外头的皮特·琼斯笑道。他看明白了：没有烟。连巴德也开始指望拉尔夫这一回栽个跟头。老师这时已经骑在校舍的屋脊上。他从兜里掏出一个纸包，不慌不忙，把里头的东西顺着烟囱倒了下去。

皮特·琼斯先生喊了一声「火药！」，撒腿顺大路跑开，躲爆炸去了。斯莫尔医生呢，大概是想起了他的病人没准要在他干坐着的当口咽气，也上马走了。

===Original===
But Ralph emptied the paper, and laid the board over the chimney. What a row there was inside! The benches that were braced against the door were thrown down, and Hank Banta rushed out, rubbing his eyes, coughing frantically, and sure that he had been blown up. All the rest followed, Bud bringing up the rear sulkily, but coughing and sneezing for dear life. Such a smell of sulphur as came from that schoolhouse!

Betsey had to lean against the fence to giggle.

As soon as all were out, Ralph threw the board off the chimney, leaped to the ground, entered the schoolhouse, and opened the windows. The school soon followed him, and all was still.

“Would he thrash?” This was the important question in Hank Banta’s mind. And the rest looked for a battle with Bud.

“It is just nine o’clock,” said Ralph, consulting his watch, “and I’m glad to see you all here promptly. I should have given you a holiday if you had asked me like gentlemen yesterday. On the whole, I think I shall give you a holiday, anyhow. The school is dismissed.”

And Hank felt foolish.

And Bud secretly resolved to thrash Hank or the master, he didn’t care which.

And Mirandy looked the love she could not utter.

And Betsey giggled.

===Chinese===
可拉尔夫把纸包倒净，拿木板往烟囱口上一盖。屋里那阵好乱！顶着门的条凳全被掀翻，汉克·班塔头一个冲出来，揉着眼、没命地咳嗽，一口咬定自己已经被炸上了天。其余的跟着一涌而出，巴德闷闷地殿后，却也拼了命地咳嗽打喷嚏。那校屋里冒出来的，好一股硫磺味儿！

贝特西只得倚着栅栏，才笑得下去。

人一出来齐，拉尔夫掀掉烟囱上的木板，跳下地，走进校舍，打开了窗。学生们很快跟了进去，一切归于安静。

「他打不打人？」这是汉克·班塔心里的头号大问题。其余的人都等着看跟巴德的一场恶战。

「正好九点，」拉尔夫看了看表说，「很高兴见大家到得这么齐。昨天你们要是像个上等人那样开口，我本来就会给你们放假的。想来想去，我看假我还是要放。放学。」

汉克自觉没趣。

巴德暗自打定主意，要揍汉克或者老师一顿，揍哪个都行。

米兰迪把她说不出口的爱，都望在了眼睛里。

贝特西嗤嗤地笑。

## XXXII After the Battle / 三十二 战后

===Original===
Nothing can be more demoralizing in the long run than lynch law. And yet lynch law often originates in a burst of generous indignation which is not willing to suffer a bold oppressor to escape by means of corrupt and cowardly courts. It is oftener born of fear. Both motives powerfully agitated the people of the region round about Clifty as night drew on after Ralph’s acquittal. They were justly indignant that Ralph had been made the victim of such a conspiracy, and they were frightened at the unseen danger to the community from such a band as that of Small’s. It was certain that they did not know the full extent of the danger as yet. And what Small might do with a jury, or what Pete Jones might do with a sheriff, was a question. I must not detain the reader to tell how the mob rose. Nobody knows how such things come about. Their origin is as inexplicable as that of an earthquake. But, at any rate, a rope was twice put round Small’s neck during that night, and both times Small was saved only by the nerve and address of Ralph, who had learned how unjust mob law may be. As for Small, he neither trembled when they were ready to hang him, nor looked relieved when he was saved, nor showed the slightest flush of penitence or gratitude. He bore himself in a quiet, gentlemanly way throughout, like the admirable villain that he was.

He waived a preliminary examination the next day; his father went his bail, and he forfeited bail and disappeared from the county and from the horizon of my story. Two reports concerning Small have been in circulation—one that he was running a faro-bank in San Francisco, the other that he was curing consumption in New York by some quack process. If this latter were true, it would leave it an open question whether Ralph did well to save him from the gallows. Pete Jones and Bill, as usually happens to the rougher villains, went to prison, and when their terms had expired moved to Pike County, Missouri.

===Chinese===
长而久之，没有什么比私刑更能败坏人心。然而私刑往往发端于一阵慷慨的义愤——不肯叫横行的恶人靠腐败而怯懦的法庭逃之夭夭。但它更多的时候生于恐惧。拉尔夫无罪开释之后，夜幕渐渐合拢，这两种动机一齐激动着克利夫蒂一带的百姓。他们义愤填膺，因为拉尔夫竟成了这样一场阴谋的牺牲品；他们也惊惶不安，因为斯莫尔那样一伙人对地方暗藏着危险。可以肯定的是，这危险的全部分量，他们还不曾掂出。而斯莫尔对付一个陪审团能干出什么，皮特·琼斯对付一个县治安官又能干出什么，都还是个问题。至于暴民是怎么聚起来的，我就不耽搁读者细讲了。谁也说不上这类事情是怎么闹起来的。它的起因跟地震一样没法解释。反正，那天夜里，绳子两次套上斯莫尔的脖子，两次都全亏拉尔夫的胆气与手腕才救下他来——拉尔夫可是领教过私刑会多么不公。至于斯莫尔，人家预备吊他时他不发抖，得救时也不见宽慰，悔意或感恩的最淡淡一阵红晕，更是一丝也没有。他自始至终举止安详，一派绅士风度，真不愧是位令人叹服的反派。

第二天他放弃了预审；他父亲替他具了保，他随即弃保潜逃，从此在本县消失，也从我这故事的地平线上消失。关于斯莫尔，后来流传着两种说法：一说他在旧金山开法罗赌场，一说他在纽约拿什么江湖方子治痨病。倘若后一说属实，那么拉尔夫当初救他不上绞架究竟对不对，倒成了一桩悬案。皮特·琼斯和比尔这对粗坯恶棍，照例的结局是进了监狱，刑满之后迁往密苏里州派克县。

===Original===
But it is about Hannah that you wish to hear, and that I wish to tell. She went straight from the court room to Flat Creek, climbed to her chamber, packed in a handkerchief all her earthly goods, consisting chiefly of a few family relics, and turned her back on the house of Means forever. At the gate she met the old woman, who shook her fist in the girl’s face and gave her a parting benediction in the words: “You mis’able, ongrateful critter you, go ’long. I’m glad to be shed of you!” At the barn she met Bud, and he told her goodbye with a little huskiness in his voice, while a tear glistened in her eyes. Bud had been a friend in need, and such a friend one does not leave without a pang.

“Where are you going? Can I—”

“No, no!” And with that she hastened on, afraid that Bud would offer to hitch up the roan colt. And she did not want to add to his domestic unhappiness by compromising him in that way.

===Chinese===
可你想听的是汉娜，我想讲的也是汉娜。她出了法庭就径直回弗拉特克里克，爬上自己的小间，把全部家当打进一只手帕里——无非几件传家的旧物——就永远背过身去，不再理明斯家的门。在大门口她遇上那老婆子，老婆子对着姑娘的脸挥拳头，临别祝祷道：「你这个贱骨头、没良心的东西，滚吧。甩了你，俺才清净！」在谷仓边她遇见巴德，巴德同她道别时嗓音有点发哑，她眼里也闪着泪光。巴德是患难里的朋友，这样的朋友，离开时总免不了心头一痛。

「你要上哪儿去？俺能不能——」

「不用，不用！」她说着赶紧往前赶，生怕巴德提出去套那匹菊花青小马。她也不愿那样连累他，再添上他家里那本难念的经。

===Original===
It was dusk and was raining when she left. The hours were long, the road was lonely, and after the revelations of that day it did not seem wholly safe. But from the moment that she found herself free, her heart had been ready to break with an impatient homesickness. What though there might be robbers in the woods? What though there were ten rough miles to travel? What though the rain was in her face? What though she had not tasted food since the morning of that exciting day? Flat Creek and bondage were behind; freedom, mother, Shocky, and home were before her, and her feet grew lighter with the thought. And if she needed any other joy, it was to know that the master was clear. And he would come? And so she traversed the weary distance, and so she inquired and found the house, the beautiful, homely old house of beautiful, homely old Nancy Sawyer, and knocked, and was admitted, and fell down, faint and weary, at her blind mother’s feet, and laid her tired head in her mother’s lap and wept and wept like a child, and said, “O mother! I’m free! I’m free!” while the mother’s tears baptized her face, and the mother’s trembling fingers combed out her tresses. And Shocky stood by her and cried: “I knowed God wouldn’t forget you, Hanner!”

===Chinese===
她动身的时候，天已擦黑，又下着雨。钟点漫长，道路冷清，而且经过白天那番揭发，这一路也不见得全然安稳。可从她发觉自己自由了的那一刻起，她的心早被一股按捺不住的乡愁涨得要破了。树林里兴许藏着劫匪，那又怎样？还有十英里难走的路，那又怎样？雨点打着脸，那又怎样？从那个叫人心头发热的早晨起粒米未进，那又怎样？弗拉特克里克和苦役在身后；自由、母亲、肖基、家在前头——想到这里，她的脚步越来越轻。倘若她还缺别的欢喜，那就是知道老师洗清了冤屈。而他也会来的，不是吗？就这样，她走完了那段累人的长路；就这样，她一路打听，找到了那座房子——美丽而家常的老南希·索耶那座美丽而家常的老房子——敲了门，被让了进去，晕晕乎乎、筋疲力尽地跌在她瞎眼母亲的脚边，把疲倦的头枕上母亲的膝头，孩子似的哭了又哭，说：「噢，妈！我自由了！我自由了！」母亲的眼泪洒在她脸上，母亲颤抖的手指替她梳开长发。肖基站在她身旁喊：「俺就知道上帝不会忘了你，汉娜！」

===Original===
Hannah was ready now to do anything by which she could support her mother and Shocky. She was strong, and inured to toil. She was willing and cheerful, and she would gladly have gone to service if by that means she could have supported the family. And, for that matter her mother was already able nearly to support herself by her knitting. But Hannah had been carefully educated when young, and at that moment the old public schools were being organized into a graded school, and the good minister, who shall be nameless, because he is, perhaps, still living in Indiana, and who in Methodist parlance was called “the preacher-in-charge of Lewisburg Station”—this good minister and Miss Nancy Sawyer got Hannah a place as teacher in the primary department. And then a little house with four rooms was rented, and a little, a very little furniture was put into it, and the old sweet home was established again. The father was gone, never to come back again. But the rest were here. And somehow Hannah kept waiting for somebody else to come.

===Chinese===
如今汉娜什么都肯干，只要能养活母亲和肖基。她身子骨结实，苦活惯了的；她勤快又爽利，只要能撑起这个家，去帮佣也甘心。说到这个，她母亲靠织毛线，也已经差不多能自给了。但汉娜幼年受过正经的教育，又恰逢那时旧的公立学堂正改组分等学校，那位好心牧师——姑隐其名，因为他兴许还活在印第安纳；按美以美会的行话，他叫做「刘易斯堡堂会的驻任牧师」——这位好牧师同南希·索耶小姐一道，给汉娜在初级部谋了个教席。随后租下一座四间房的小屋，置办了一点点、真真一点点家具，那个甜甜蜜蜜的老家又立起来了。父亲去了，再也不会回来。其余的人都在。而不知怎么，汉娜总在等着另一个人来。

## XXX “Brother Sodom” / 三十 「所多玛弟兄」

===Original===
In order to explain Walter Johnson’s testimony and his state of mind, I must carry the reader back nearly a week. The scene was Dr. Small’s office. Bud and Walter Johnson had been having some confidential conversation that evening, and Bud had got more out of his companion than that exquisite but weak young man had intended. He looked round in a frightened way.

“You see,” said Walter, “if Small knew I had told you that, I’d get a bullet some night from somebody. But when you’re initiated it’ll be all right. Sometimes I wish I was out of it. But, you know, Small’s this kind of a man. He sees through you. He can look through a door”—and there he shivered, and his voice broke down into a whisper. But Bud was perfectly cool, and doubtless it was the strong coolness of Bud that made Walter, who shuddered at a shadow, come to him for sympathy and unbosom himself of one of his guilty secrets.

“Let’s go and hear Brother Sodom preach tonight,” said Bud.

“No, I don’t like to.”

“He don’t scare you?” There was just a touch of ridicule in Bud’s voice. He knew Walter, and he had not counted amiss when he used this little goad to prick a skin so sensitive. “Brother Sodom” was the nickname given by scoffers to the preacher—Mr. Soden—whose manner of preaching had so aroused Bud’s combativeness, and whose saddle-stirrups Bud had helped to amputate. For reasons of his own, Bud thought best to subject young Johnson to the heat of Mr. Soden’s furnace.

===Chinese===
要解释沃尔特·约翰逊的证词和他的心境，我得把读者往回带将近一个礼拜。地点是斯莫尔医生的诊所。那天晚上，巴德和沃尔特·约翰逊正谈着体己话，从同伴嘴里套出来的东西，比那位精巧而软弱的年轻人原本打算吐露的还多。他心惊胆战地四下张望。

「你瞧，」沃尔特说，「要是斯莫尔知道我把这个告诉了你，哪天夜里就会有人赏我一颗枪子儿。可等你入了会，就没事了。有时候我真想退出来。不过你也知道，斯莫尔是这么一号人：他能看穿你，能隔着门看人。」说到这里他打了个寒噤，声音低成了耳语。巴德却镇定得很；想来正是巴德这份沉着的镇定，才叫见个影子就哆嗦的沃尔特跑来向他讨同情，把一桩亏心事的秘密倒了出来。

「今晚俺们去听『所多玛弟兄』讲道吧。」巴德说。

「不，我不想去。」

「他吓不着你？」巴德的嗓音里正带着一丝讥诮。他了解沃尔特，拿这根小刺去扎这么嫩的一张皮，算得一点不差。「所多玛弟兄」是讥诮的人给那位传道人起的绰号——索登先生——他讲道的路数曾大大激起巴德的好斗心，他的马鞍镫还是巴德帮着砍掉的。出于自家的心思，巴德觉得最好让约翰逊这小伙子去受一受索登先生炉火的烤。

===Original===
Peter Cartwright boasts that, on a certain occasion, he “shook his brimstone wallet” over the people. Mr. Soden could never preach without his brimstone wallet. There are those of refinement so attenuated that they will not admit that fear can have any place in religion. But a religion without fear could never have evangelized or civilized the West, which at one time bade fair to become a perdition as bad as any that Brother Sodom ever depicted. And against these on the one side, and the Brother Sodoms on the other, I shall interrupt my story to put this chapter under shelter of that wise remark of the great Dr. Adam Clark, who says “The fear of God is the beginning of wisdom, the terror of God confounds the soul;” and that other saying of his: “With the *fear* of God the love of God is ever consistent; but where the *terror* of the Lord reigns, there can neither be *fear, faith*, nor *love*; nay, nor *hope* either.” And yet I am not sure that even the Brother Sodoms were made in vain.

On this evening Mr. Soden was as terrible as usual. Bud heard him without flinching. Small, who sat farther forward, listened with pious approval. Mr. Soden, out of distorted figures pieced together from different passages of Scripture, built a hell, not quite Miltonic, nor yet Dantean, but as Miltonic and Dantean as his unrefined imagination could make it. As he rose toward his climax of hideous description, Walter Johnson trembled from head to foot and sat close to Bud. Then, as burly Mr. Soden, with great gusto, depicted materialistic tortures that startled the nerves of everybody except Bud, Walter wanted to leave, but Bud would not let him. For some reason he wished to keep his companion in the crucible as long as possible.

===Chinese===
彼得·卡特赖特（Peter Cartwright）自夸说，有一回他曾朝众人「抖开他的硫磺钱袋」。索登先生呢，讲道从来离不了他的硫磺钱袋。也有那种雅致得淡薄了的人，死也不肯承认恐惧在宗教里能有一席之地。可是当年，一种没有恐惧的宗教，是绝无可能叫西部归教开化的——那阵子的西部，大有指望变成一座同所多玛弟兄描画过的任何一个一样糟的地狱。为抵御这一头的这些人、那一头的所多玛弟兄们，我且打断故事，把这一章置于伟大的亚当·克拉克博士那句名言的庇荫之下，他说：「敬畏上帝是智慧的开端，惊骇上帝却搅乱人的灵魂。」他还有一句话：「上帝的*敬畏*与上帝的*爱*永远相合；可哪里盛行的是对主的*惊骇*，那里就既没有*敬畏*，没有*信*，也没有*爱*；不，连*望*也没有。」然而我也不敢说，连这些所多玛弟兄们都是白造的。

这天晚上，索登先生一如既往地可怕。巴德听着，面不改色。坐在更前排的斯莫尔，怀着敬虔的赞许听着。索登先生拿《圣经》各处段落拼凑起来的歪曲形象，造出一座地狱——不太弥尔顿，也不太但丁，可已经是他那未受教化的想象力所能造出的最弥尔顿、最但丁的样子了。随着他朝那骇人描画的高潮步步攀升，沃尔特·约翰逊从头到脚打颤，紧紧贴着巴德坐。随后，魁伟的索登先生兴致勃勃地描画起种种肉刑，把在场每一个人的神经都吓了一跳——只有巴德除外——沃尔特想走，巴德不放。出于某种缘故，他要把同伴尽量久地扣在这只坩埚里。

===Original===
“Young man!” cried Mr. Soden, and the explosive voice seemed to come from the hell that he had created—“young man! you who have followed the counsel of evil companions”—here he paused and looked about, as if trying to find the man he wanted, while Walter crept up close to Bud and shaded his face—“I mean you who have chosen evil pursuits and who can not get free from bad habits and associations that are dragging you down to hell! You are standing on the very crumbling brink of hell tonight. The smell of the brimstone is on your garments; the hot breath of hell is in your face! The devils are waiting for you! Delay and you are damned! You may die before daylight! You may never get out that door! The awful angel of death is just ready to strike you down!” Here some shrieked with terror, others sobbed, and Brother Sodom looked with approval on the storm he had awakened. The very harshness of his tone, his lofty egotism of manner, that which had roused all Bud’s combativeness, shook poor Walter as a wind would shake a reed. In the midst of the general excitement he seized his hat and hastened out the door. Bud followed, while Soden shot his lightnings after them, declaring that “young men who ran away from the truth would dwell in torments forever.”

===Chinese===
「年轻人！」索登先生喊道，那炸雷似的嗓音仿佛就发自他造出的那座地狱——「年轻人！你这个听了恶友教唆的！」——说到这里他顿住，眼睛四下里扫，像要找出他要的那个人，沃尔特则往巴德身边缩，捂住了脸——「我说的是你，你这个拣了恶行、又甩不掉那把你往地狱里拖的坏习气坏交际的！今夜你就站在地狱那正塌陷的边上！硫磺的气味已经上了你的衣裳；地狱的热气正喷在你脸上！众魔鬼正等着你！再耽搁，你就灭亡了！不到天亮你就可能死！你可能出不了那扇门！那可怕的死亡天使正扬起手来，就要把你击倒！」这时有人吓得尖叫，有人低声啜泣，所多玛弟兄满意地望着自己唤起的这场风暴。他那嗓门的粗暴、那副高高在上的自大——正是这些东西激起了巴德全部的好斗心——此刻摇撼着可怜的沃尔特，像风摇撼一枝芦苇。在一片鼎沸的骚动里，他抓起帽子，夺门而出。巴德跟了出去，索登的雷电在他们背后追着劈下来，宣告说「从真理面前逃跑的年轻人，必永远住在痛苦里」。

===Original===
Bud had not counted amiss when he thought that Mr. Soden’s preaching would be likely to arouse so mean-spirited a fellow as Walter. So vivid was the impression that Johnson begged Bud to return to the office with him. He felt sick, and was afraid that he should die before morning. He insisted that Bud should stay with him all night. To this Means readily consented, and by morning he had heard all that the frightened Walter had to tell.

And now let us return to the trial, where Ralph sits waiting the testimony of Walter Johnson, which is to prove his statement false.

===Chinese===
巴德料定索登先生的讲道准能挑动沃尔特这么个胆小鬼，果然一点没算错。印象深到这般地步，约翰逊竟恳求巴德陪他回诊所去。他觉得难受，生怕自己活不到天亮。他非要巴德陪他一整夜不可。这个嘛，明斯满口答应，到早晨，吓破了胆的沃尔特该说的，他已经全听完了。

现在让我们回到法庭上去吧——拉尔夫正坐在那里，等着沃尔特·约翰逊的证词，等着它来证明他的话是假话。

## XXV Bud Wooing / 二十五 巴德求亲

===Original===
The Sunday that Ralph spent in Lewisburg, the Sunday that Shocky spent in an earthly paradise, the Sunday that Mrs. Thomson spent with Shocky instead of old Mowley, the Sunday that Miss Nancy thought was “just like heaven,” was also an eventful Sunday with Bud Means. He had long adored Miss Martha in his secret heart, but, like many other giants, while brave enough to face and fight dragons, he was a coward in the presence of the woman that he loved. Let us honor him for it. The man who loves a woman truly, reverences her profoundly and feels abashed in her presence. The man who is never abashed in the presence of womanhood, the man who tells his love without a tremor, is a shallow egotist. Bud’s nature was not fine. But it was deep, true, and manly. To him Martha Hawkins was the chief of women. What was he that he should aspire to possess her? And yet on that Sunday, with his crippled arm carefully bound up, with his cleanest shirt, and with his heavy boots freshly oiled with the fat of the raccoon, he started hopefully through fields white with snow to the house of Squire Hawkins. When he started his spirits were high, but they descended exactly in proportion to his proximity to the object of his love. He thought himself not dressed well enough He wished his shoulders were not so square, and his arms not so stout. He wished that he had book-larnin’ enough to court in nice, big words. And so, by recounting his own deficiencies, he succeeded in making himself feel weak, and awkward, and generally good-for-nothing, by the time he walked up between the rows of dead hollyhocks to the Squire’s front door, to tap at which took all his remaining strength.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫在刘易斯堡度过的那个星期天，肖基在人间的乐园里度过的那个星期天，汤姆森太太没有去陪老莫利、倒陪了肖基的那个星期天，南希小姐觉得「简直像天堂」的那个星期天，对巴德·明斯来说，也是个多事的星期天。他心里暗暗倾慕玛莎小姐已经很久了，可是同许多别的大个子一样，当面斗恶龙他有的是胆量，到了心爱的女人跟前，却成了个懦夫。为这一点，我们倒该敬他。真爱一个女人的男人，必深深敬重她，在她面前自惭形秽。在女人面前从不害臊的男人、表白爱情时毫不动心的男人，是个浅薄的自我主义者。巴德的天性算不得精致，却深厚、真挚、有男子气。在他眼里，玛莎·霍金斯是女子中的头一份。他算老几，配痴心妄想得到她？然而就在那个星期天，他吊着那条仔细包扎好的伤胳膊，穿着身上最干净的一件衬衫，笨重的靴子新抹了浣熊油，满怀希望地踏着一片雪白的田野，朝霍金斯治安官家出发了。动身时他兴致很高，可那兴致偏偏按着他离爱情对象的远近，成比例地往下降。他嫌自己穿得不够体面。他恨不得肩膀别这么方，胳膊别这么粗。他恨自己没有足够的书本学问，好拿漂亮的大字眼儿谈恋爱。就这样，他把自个儿的短处一件一件数下来，成功地数到走上夹在两排枯蜀葵之间的过道、来到治安官家大门跟前时，已经觉得自己虚弱、笨拙、百无一用——敲一敲那扇门，用尽了他剩下的全部力气。

===Original===
Miss Martha received her perspiring lover most graciously, but this only convinced Bud more than ever that she was a superior being. If she had slighted him a bit, so as to awaken his combativeness, his bashfulness might have disappeared.

It was in vain that Martha inquired about his arm and complimented his courage. Bud could only think of his big feet, his clumsy hands, and his slow tongue. He answered in monosyllables, using his red silk handkerchief diligently.

===Chinese===
玛莎小姐满脸和气地接待她这位冒汗的追求者，这反倒叫巴德越发认定她高不可攀。她要是稍稍怠慢他一点，把他的好斗心激起来，他的腼腆倒兴许就没了。

玛莎问他胳膊、夸他勇敢，全是白费。巴德满脑子只有自己的大脚、笨手、慢舌头。他一个字一个字地往外蹦，红绸手绢儿倒是用得勤快。

===Original===
“Is your arm improving?” asked Miss Hawkins.

“Yes, I think it is,” said Bud, hastily crossing his right leg over his left, and trying to get his fists out of sight.

“Have you heard from Mr. Pearson?”

“No, I ha’n’t,” answered Bud, removing his right foot to the floor again, because it looked so big, and trying to push his left hand into his pocket.

“Beautiful sunshine, isn’t it?” said Martha.

“Yes, ’tis,” answered Bud, sticking his right foot up on the rung of the chair and putting his right hand behind him.

“This snow looks like the snow we have at the East,” said Martha. “It snowed that way the time I was to Bosting.”

“Did it?” said Bud, not thinking of the snow at all nor of Boston, but thinking how much better he would have appeared had he left his arms and legs at home.

“I suppose Mr. Hartsook rode your horse to Lewisburg?”

“Yes, he did;” and Bud hung both hands at his side.

“You were very kind.”

===Chinese===
「你的胳膊见好吗？」霍金斯小姐问。

「嗯，俺瞅着是见好。」巴德说，慌忙把右腿翘到左腿上，想法子叫两只拳头躲开人的眼。

「皮尔逊先生有信儿没有？」

「没，没信儿。」巴德答道，又把右脚放回地板上，因为它瞧着实在太大了，一面想法把左手塞进兜里去。

「太阳多好，是不是？」玛莎说。

「嗯，是好。」巴德答道，把右脚架上椅子的横档，右手背到身后。

「这雪跟我们东边的雪一个样，」玛莎说。「我上『波士顿』那回，就是这么个下法。」

「是吗？」巴德说，心里压根儿没想雪，也没想波士顿，光想着自己要是把胳膊腿儿留在家里没带来，该有多体面。

「哈茨克先生是骑你的马去的刘易斯堡吧？」

「嗯，是。」巴德说着，把两只手垂在身子两边。

「你真够意思。」

===Original===
This set Bud’s heart a-going so that he could not say anything, but he looked eloquently at Miss Hawkins, drew both feet under the chair, and rammed his hands into his pockets. Then, suddenly remembering how awkward he must look, he immediately pulled his hands out again, and crossed his legs. There was a silence of a few minutes, during which Bud made up his mind to do the most desperate thing he could think of—to declare his love and take the consequences.

“You see, Miss Hawkins,” he began, forgetting boots and fists in his agony, “I thought as how I’d come over here today, and”—but here his heart failed him utterly—“and—see—you.”

“I’m glad to see you, Mr. Means.”

“And I thought I’d tell you”—Martha was sure it was coming now, for Bud was in dead earnest—“and I thought I’d just like to tell you, ef I only know’d jest how to tell it right”—here Bud got frightened, and did not dare close the sentence as he had intended—“I thought as how you might like to know—or ruther I wanted to tell you—that—the—that I—that we—all of us—think—that—I—that we are going to have a spellin’-school a Chewsday night.”

“I’m real glad to hear it,” said the bland but disappointed Martha. “We used to have spelling-schools at the East.” But Miss Martha could not remember that they had them “to Bosting.”

Hard as it is for a bashful man to talk, it is still more difficult for him to close the conversation. Most men like to leave a favorable impression, and a bashful man is always waiting with the forlorn hope that some favorable turn in the talk may let him out without absolute discomfiture. And so Bud stayed a long time, and how he ever did get away he never could tell.

===Chinese===
这一句叫巴德的心怦怦直跳，什么话也说不出来，只朝霍金斯小姐脉脉地望着，把两只脚收到椅子底下，两只手狠狠捅进兜里。接着猛然想起这副模样一定蠢透了，又立刻把手拔出来，翘起二郎腿。沉默了几分钟，就在这几分钟里，巴德横下心来，要干一件他所能想到的最豁得出去的事——把爱说出来，后果自负。

「你瞧，霍金斯小姐，」他开了口，苦楚里忘了靴子也忘了拳头，「俺寻思俺今儿个该过来一趟，还有」——可说到这儿，他的心彻底泄了气——「还有——见——你。」

「见到你我很高兴，明斯先生。」

「俺还想告诉你」——玛莎拿准这回要来了，因为巴德一片死心眼儿的认真——「俺就是想告诉你，要是俺知道咋把话说囫囵了就好」——到这儿巴德又怯了场，不敢照原来的意思把这句话收尾——「俺寻思你兴许乐意知道——或者说，俺想告诉你——那个——那个俺——那个俺们——俺们大伙儿——觉着——那个——俺——俺们礼拜二晚上要办一场拼字学校。」

「听了这个我真高兴，」和气却失望了的玛莎说。「我们东边从前也办拼字学校。」只是玛莎小姐怎么也想不起来，在「波士顿」也办过这个。

腼腆的人开口难，收场更难。多数人都想留个好印象，腼腆的人则永远怀着一线渺茫的希望等着，盼着谈话能有个好转弯，叫他体体面面脱身，不至于一败涂地。就这样，巴德又坐了老半天；至于他到底是怎么走脱的，他自己一辈子也说不清。

===Original===
> As a pebble cast upon a great cairn, this edition is inscribed to the memory of James Russell Lowell, whose cordial encouragement to my early studies of American dialect is gratefully remembered.
> The Author.

===Chinese===
> 谨将本版题献给詹姆斯·拉塞尔·洛威尔（James Russell Lowell）的在天之灵，如同向巍峨的石冢奉上一枚卵石；他对笔者早年研究美国方言时那份恳切的鼓励，至今感念不忘。
> 作者谨识。

## Endnotes / 尾注

===Original===
1. “Aout” is not the common form of “out,” as it is in certain rustic New England regions. The vowel is here drawn in this way for imperative emphasis, and it occurs as a consequence of drawling speech. ↩︎

2. “ ’Nough said” is more than enough said for the French translator, who takes it apparently for a sort of barbarous negative and renders it, “I don’t like to speak to him.” I need hardly explain to any American reader that “enough said” implies the ending of all discussion by the acceptance of the proposition or challenge. ↩︎

3. “Durn’t,” “daren’t,” “dasent,” “dursent,” and “don’t dast” are forms of this variable negative heard in the folk-speech of various parts of the country. The tenses of this verb seem to have got hopelessly mixed long ago, even in literary use, and the speech of the people reflects the historic confusion. ↩︎

4. “To take a dare” is an expression used in senses diametrically opposed. Its common sense is that of the text. The man who refuses to accept a challenge is said to take a dare, and there is some implication of cowardice in the imputation. On the other hand, one who accepts a challenge is said also to take the dare. ↩︎

===Chinese===
1. 「Aout」并不像新英格兰某些乡土地区那样，是「out」的通行拼法。这里把元音拖成这样，是为了加重祈使的语气，是说话拖腔带出来的结果。 ↩︎

2. 「’Nough said」（说定了）对那位法文译者来说，实在是「说得太多了」：他显然把它当成了某种野蛮的否定词，译成了「我不愿意同他说话」。任何美国读者都无须我多解释：「enough said」的意思是接受提议或应下挑战，从此一切讨论告终。 ↩︎

3. 「Durn’t」「daren’t」「dasent」「dursent」「don’t dast」，是全国各地民间口语里听得到的这个多变否定词的种种形态。这个动词的时态，似乎早在文学用法里就已搅成了一团理不清的乱麻，百姓的口语，正是这份历史性混乱的留影。 ↩︎

4. 「To take a dare」（吃激将）是一个可以用于截然相反两义的短语。通行的一义，即正文中的用法。拒绝应战的人，被说成 take a dare，这顶帽子里多少藏着懦夫的暗示。可另一方面，应下挑战的人，也说成 take the dare。 ↩︎

===Original===
5. Most bad English was once good English. “Ketch” was used by writers of the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries for “catch.” A New Hampshire magistrate in the seventeenth century spells it “caitch,” and probably pronounced it in that way. “Ketch,” a boat, was sometimes spelled “catch” by the first American colonists, and the far-fetched derivation of the word from the Turkish may be one of the fancies of etymologists. ↩︎

6. The derivation of “raccoon” from the French *raton*, to which Mr. Skeat gives currency, still holds its place in some of our standard dictionaries. If American lexicographers would only read the literature of American settlement they would know that Mr. Skeat’s citation of a translation of Buffon is nearly two centuries too late. As early as 1612 Captain John Smith gives *aroughcune* as the aboriginal Virginia word, and more than one New England writer used “rackoon” a few years later. ↩︎

7. This prefixed *y* is a mark of a very illiterate or antique form of the dialect. I have known “piece yarthen” used for “a piece of earthen” [ware], the preposition getting lost in the sound of the *y*. I leave it to etymologists to determine its relation to that ancient prefix that differentiates “earn” in one sense from “yearn.” But the article before a vowel may account for it if we consider it a corruption. “The earth” pronounced in a drawling way will produce “the yearth.” In the New York Documents is a letter from one Barnard Hodges, a settler in Delaware in the days of Governor Andros, whose spelling indicates a free use of the parasitic *y*. He writes “yunless,” “yeunder” (under), “yunderstanding,” “yeundertake,” and “yeouffeis” (office). ↩︎

===Chinese===
5. 大多数坏的英语，从前都是好的英语。十六、十七世纪的作家就拿「ketch」当「catch」用。新罕布什尔一位十七世纪的治安法官把它拼作「caitch」，八成也就是那么念的。「Ketch」（双桅小帆船）呢，头一批美洲殖民者有时反倒拼作「catch」；把这个词硬从土耳其语里认远亲的那路溯源，兴许只是词源学家的一厢情愿。 ↩︎

6. 把「raccoon」（浣熊）的词源算到法文 *raton* 头上——斯基特先生使这个说法流行开来——至今仍盘踞在咱们几部标准词典里。美国的词典学家只要肯读一读美洲殖民的文献，就会明白斯基特先生所引布封译文的例证，差不多晚了两个世纪。早在 1612 年，约翰·史密斯船长就记下了弗吉尼亚土语的 *aroughcune*，没过几年，新英格兰也不止一位作家用过「rackoon」。 ↩︎

7. 这个缀在头里的 *y*，是这方言极不识字或极古旧形态的记号。我见过有人拿「piece yarthen」当「a piece of earthen」〔器皿〕用，介词就淹死在 *y* 的音里了。它同那个古老前缀——就是叫「earn」在一个意义上区别于「yearn」的那个——是什么关系，我留给词源学家去断。不过若把它看作讹变，元音前的冠词倒可以解释它：把「the earth」拖长了念，就会念出「the yearth」来。《纽约文献》里有一封巴纳德·霍奇斯（Barnard Hodges）的信，此人是安德罗斯总督时代特拉华的移民，他的拼法表明他对这个寄生 *y* 用得毫不见外：他写「yunless」「yeunder」（under）、「yunderstanding」「yeundertake」，还有「yeouffeis」（office）。 ↩︎

===Original===
8. Like many of the earmarks of this dialect, the verb “dog-on” came from Scotland, presumably by the way of the north of Ireland. A correspondent of *The Nation* calls attention to the use of “dagon” as Scotch dialect in Barrie’s *Little Minister*. On examining that story, I find that the word has precisely the sense of our Hoosier “dog-on,” which is to be pronounced broadly as a Hoosier pronounces dog—“daug-on.” If Mr. Barrie gives his *a* the broad sound, his “dagon” is nearly identical with “dog-on.” Here are some detached sentences from *The Little Minister*:

 “Beattie spoke for more than himself when he said: ‘Dagon that Manse! I never gie a swear but there it is glowering at me.’ ”

 “ ‘Dagon religion,’ Rob retorted fiercely; ‘ ’t spoils a’ thing.’ ”

 “There was some angry muttering from the crowd, and young Charles Yuill exclaimed, ‘Dagon you, would you lord it ower us on weekdays as well as on Sabbaths?’ ”

 “ ‘Have you on your Sabbath shoon or have you no on your Sabbath shoon?’ ‘Guid care you took I should ha’e the dagont things on!’ retorted the farmer.”

 It will be seen that “dagont,” as used above, is the Scotch form of “dog-oned.” But Mr. Barrie uses the same form apparently for “dog-on it” in the following passage:

===Chinese===
8. 同这方言的许多印记一样，动词「dog-on」（天杀的）是从苏格兰来的，多半取道北爱尔兰。*The Nation*（《民族》周刊）的一位通信者提请注意：「dagon」作为苏格兰方言，见于巴里的《小牧师》。查考那部小说，我发现这个词同我们胡希尔人的「dog-on」意义恰好一样——胡希尔人念 dog 要念成宽音，于是「dog-on」也就念成「daug-on」。巴里先生若把他的 *a* 念成宽音，他的「dagon」便同「dog-on」几乎是一个词。《小牧师》里挑出几句如下：

 「比蒂说这话时，代表的不止他一个：『Dagon that Manse! I never gie a swear but there it is glowering at me.』（天杀那牧师宅！俺每回起誓，它总在那儿瞪着俺。）」

 「『Dagon religion，』罗布狠狠回嘴；『't spoils a' thing.』（『天杀的宗教，』『它把什么都败坏了。』）」

 「There was some angry muttering from the crowd, and young Charles Yuill exclaimed, 『Dagon you, would you lord it ower us on weekdays as well as on Sabbaths?』（人群里起了一阵怒骂，小查尔斯·尤尔嚷道：天杀的你，难道安息日之外，连平日你也要骑在俺们头上？）」

 「『Have you on your Sabbath shoon or have you no on your Sabbath shoon?』『Guid care you took I should ha'e the dagont things on!』 retorted the farmer.（『你安息日穿鞋没穿鞋？』『亏你操心，叫俺偏偏穿上了这双天杀的东西！』庄户人回嘴道。）」

 可见上文用的「dagont」，就是「dog-oned」的苏格兰形。可在下面这一段里，巴里先生显然又拿同一形态当「dog-on it」用：

===Original===
 “Ay, there was Ruth when she was na wanted, but Ezra, dagont it looked as if Ezra had jumped clean out o’ the Bible!”

 Strangely enough, this word as a verb is not to be found in Jamieson’s dictionary of the Scottish dialect, but Jamieson gives “dugon” as a noun. It is given in the supplement to Jamieson, however, as “dogon,” but still as a noun, with an ancient plural “dogonis.” It is explained as “a term of contempt.” The example cited by Jamieson is Hogg’s *Winter Tales*, I 292, and is as follows:

 “What wad my father say if I were to marry a man that loot himsel’ be thrashed by Tommy Potts, a great supple wi’ a back nae stiffer than a willy brand?  … When one comes to close quarters wi’ him he’s but a dugon.”

 Halliwell and Wright give “dogon” as a noun, and mark it Anglo-Norman, but they apparently know it only from Jamieson and the supplement to Jamieson, where “dogguin” is cited from Cotgrave as meaning “a filthie old curre,” and “doguin” from Roquefort, defined by “brutal, currish” [hargneux]. A word with the same orthography, “doguin,” is still used in French for puppy. It is of course a question whether the noun “dogon” and its French antecedents are connected with the American verb “dog-on.” It is easy to conceive that such an epithet as “dogon” might get itself mixed up with the word dog, and so become an imprecation. For instance, a servant in the family of a friend of mine in Indiana, wishing to resign her place before the return of some daughters of the house whom she had never seen, announced that she was going to leave “before them dog-on girls got home.” Here the word might have been the old epithet, or an abbreviated participle. “Dogged” is apparently a corruption of dog-on in the phrase “I’ll be dogged.” I prefer “dog-on” to “dogone,” because in the dialect the sense of setting a dog on is frequently present to the speaker, though far enough away from the primitive sense of the word, perhaps. ↩︎

===Chinese===
 「Ay, there was Ruth when she was na wanted, but Ezra, dagont it looked as if Ezra had jumped clean out o' the Bible!」（是啊，不该来的露丝倒来了；可伊斯拉——天杀的，瞧那样子，伊斯拉简直是从《圣经》里整个儿跳出来的！）」

 说来奇怪，这个词作动词，在贾米森的苏格兰方言词典里竟寻不见，不过贾米森收了「dugon」作名词。在贾米森词典的补编里，它作「dogon」出现，仍是名词，还有个古复数「dogonis」，释作「一个蔑称」。贾米森引的例证出自霍格的《冬日故事集》卷一第 292 页，如下：

 「What wad my father say if I were to marry a man that loot himsel' be thrashed by Tommy Potts, a great supple wi' a back nae stiffer than a willy brand? … When one comes to close quarters wi' him he's but a dugon.」（俺要是嫁了个任凭汤米·波茨揍、腰杆比柳条还软的大个子，俺爹会怎么说？……真跟他交起手来，他不过是个天杀货。）」

 哈利韦尔和赖特也收「dogon」作名词，标为盎格鲁-诺曼语源，不过他们显然只是从贾米森和贾米森补编里知道这个词——补编里从科特格雷夫引了「dogguin」，释为「a filthie old curre」（一条脏兮兮的老恶狗），又从罗克福尔引「doguin」，界说作「brutal, currish」〔凶横、狗性〕。同拼法的「doguin」，法文至今还当「小狗」用。名词「dogon」连同它的法文前身，同美语动词「dog-on」有没有渊源，当然是另一回事。不难设想：「dogon」这样一个骂名，满可以同 dog 这个词搅到一处，就此变成一句咒骂。譬如，印第安纳我一位朋友家里有个女仆，想在从未谋面的几位宅里小姐回来之前辞工，便宣称她要走，「before them dog-on girls got home」（趁那伙天杀的丫头还没回家）。这里的词，兴许还是那个老骂名，兴许是个缩略的分词。「Dogged」在「I'll be dogged」一语里，显然是 dog-on 的讹变。我取「dog-on」不取「dogone」，因为在这方言里，放狗咬人的那层意思，说话人心里常是有的——尽管离这个词的原始词义，或许已经很远了。 ↩︎

===Original===
9. In naming the several parts of the Indian corn and the dishes made from it, the English language was put to many shifts. Such words as “tassel” and “silk” were poetically applied to the blossoms; “stalk,” “blade,” and “ear” were borrowed from other sorts of corn, and the Indian tongues were forced to pay tribute to name the dishes borrowed from the savages. From them we have “hominy,” “pone,” “supawn,” and “succotash.” For other nouns words were borrowed from English provincial dialects. “Shuck” is one of these. On the northern belt, shucks are the outer covering of nuts; in the middle and southern regions the word is applied to what in New England is called the husks of the corn. “Shuck,” however, is much more widely used than “husk” in colloquial speech—the farmers in more than half of the United States are hardly acquainted with the word “husk” as applied to the envelope of the ear. “Husk,” in the Middle States, and in some parts of the South and West, means the bran of the cornmeal, as notably in Davy Crockett’s verse:

 “She sifted the meal, she gimme the hus’;

 She baked the bread, she gimme the crus’;

 She b’iled the meat, she gimme the bone;

 She gimme a kick and sent me home.”

 In parts of Virginia, before the war, the word “husk” or “hus’ ” meant the cob or spike of the corn. “I smack you over wid a cawn-hus’ ” is a threat I have often heard one negro boy make to another. “Cob” is provincial English for ear, and I have known “a cob of corn” used in Canada for an ear of Indian corn. While writing this note “a cob of Indian corn”—meaning an ear—appears in the report of an address by a distinguished man at a recent meeting of the Royal Geographical Society. A lady tells me that she met, in the book of an English traveller, the remarkable statement that “the Americans are very fond of the young grain called cob.” These Indian-corn words have reached an accepted meaning after a competition. To “shell” corn, among the earliest settlers of Virginia, meant to take it out of the envelope, which was presumably called the shell. The analogy is with the shelling of pulse. ↩︎

===Chinese===
9. 要给印第安玉米的各个部位、以及用它做成的各样吃食起名，英语可谓费尽了周折。「tassel」（流苏）、「silk」（丝）这类词，是诗意地移给了玉米花；「stalk」（秆）、「blade」（叶）、「ear」（穗）是从别种谷类借来的；而从野蛮人那里借来的吃食，就只好叫印第安语来进贡。「hominy」（玉米糊）、「pone」（玉米饼）、「supawn」（玉米粥）、「succotash」（豆煮玉米）都来自他们。别的名词则从英国的各地方言里借。「shuck」（苞叶）便是其一。在北边一带，shucks 指坚果的外壳；在中部及南部，这个词指的是新英格兰人叫做玉米 husks 的东西。不过口语里「shuck」用得比「husk」广得多——半个美国以上的庄户人，几乎不认得把「husk」用在玉米穗外壳上的这层意思。在中部诸州，以及南方西部的某些地方，「husk」指玉米面里的麸皮，戴维·克罗克特的诗便是显例：

 「She sifted the meal, she gimme the hus';（她筛了面，给了俺麸皮；）

 She baked the bread, she gimme the crus';（她烤了饼，给了俺饼皮；）

 She b'iled the meat, she gimme the bone;（她煮了肉，给了俺骨头；）

 She gimme a kick and sent me home.（她踹了俺一脚，打发俺回家。）」

 战前弗吉尼亚的部分地区，「husk」或「hus'」指的却是玉米芯。「I smack you over wid a cawn-hus'」（俺拿玉米芯劈你）这样的恐吓，我常听一个黑人男孩对另一个喊。「Cob」是英国方言里「穗」的意思，我听说加拿大有人拿「a cob of corn」指一穗印第安玉米。就在写这条注的时候，「a cob of Indian corn」——意即一穗——还出现在皇家地理学会最近一次会议上一位名流演讲的报道里。有位女士告诉我，她在一本英国旅行家的书里读到过一句妙语：「美国人非常爱吃那种叫做 cob 的嫩谷物。」这些玉米词，都是经过一场混战才各得其位的。在弗吉尼亚最早的移民那里，to "shell" corn 是把玉米从苞壳里剥出来，那层壳，想必就叫 shell。类推的榜样，是剥豆。 ↩︎

===Original===
10. This word “plunder” is probably from Pennsylvania, as it is exactly equivalent to the German word *plunder*, in the sense of household effects, the original meaning of the word in German. Any kind of baggage may be called “plunder,” but the most accepted sense is household goods. It is quite seriously used. I have seen bills of lading on the Western waters certifying that A. B. had shipped “1 lot of plunder;” that is, household goods. It is here used figuratively for goods in general. ↩︎

11. “Congress land” was the old designation for land owned by the government. Under the Confederation, the Congress was the government, and the forms of speech seem to have long retained the notion that what belonged to the United States was the property of Congress. ↩︎

12. The commonest use of the word “chunk” in the old days was for the ends of the sticks of cordwood burned in the great fireplaces. As the sticks burned in two, the chunks fell down or rolled back on the wall side of the andirons. By putting the chunks together, a new fire was set a-going without fresh wood. This use of the word is illustrated in a folk-rhyme or nursery jingle of the country which has neither sense nor elegance to recommend it:

===Chinese===
10. 「plunder」（家当）这个词多半来自宾夕法尼亚，因为它同德文词 *plunder* 严丝合缝——正是「家用杂物」这个意思，也是该词在德文里的本义。什么行李都可以叫「plunder」，最通行的意思则是家具什物。这词是用得很正经的。我在西部河道上见过提单，郑重其事地写着某君发运「plunder 一批」；也就是家用杂物。正文里是转义，泛指财物。 ↩︎

11. 「Congress land」（国会地）是政府公有地的旧称。邦联时代，国会即是政府，而这类口头套子似乎长久保留着一个观念：属于合众国的东西，就是国会的财产。 ↩︎

12. 早年间「chunk」（柴头）最普通的用场，是指大壁炉里烧的劈柴烧剩的断头。柴一烧两截，柴头便落下来，或者滚回到柴架靠墙那一边。把柴头归拢到一处，不必添新柴，就能重新生起一炉火。这个词的这层用法，乡下有一首既无道理又无风雅可言的民谣、摇篮顺口溜可为佐证：

===Original===
 “Old Mother Hunk

 She got drunk

 And fell in the fire

 And kicked up a chunk.”

 ↩︎

13. “Peart” or peert is only another form of the old word pert—probably an older form. Bartlett cites an example of “peart” as far back as Sir Philip Sidney; and Halliwell finds it in various English dialects. Davies, afterward president of Princeton College, describes Dr. Lardner, in 1754, as “a little pert old gent.” I do not know that Dr. Davies pronounced his “pert” as though it were “peart,” but he uses it in the sense it has in the text, viz., bright-witted, intelligent. The general sense of “peart” is lively, either in body or mind. ↩︎

===Chinese===
 「Old Mother Hunk（洪克老太婆）

 She got drunk（喝醉了酒）

 And fell in the fire（一头栽进火里）

 And kicked up a chunk.」（蹬起来一根柴头。）」

 ↩︎

13. 「Peart」或 peert，不过是旧词 pert 的又一形态——兴许还是更老的形态。巴特利特引到的「peart」一例，早至菲利普·锡德尼爵士；哈利韦尔则在英国多处方言里都找到了它。后来当上普林斯顿学院院长的戴维斯，1754 年形容拉德纳博士是「a little pert old gent」（一个伶俐的小老头儿）。戴维斯博士念他的「pert」是不是「peart」的音，我不知道，但他用的正是正文里这层意思，即脑子快、聪明。「peart」的大意是活泛：身子活泛也好，心思活泛也好。 ↩︎

===Original===
14. Mr. Lowell suggested to me in 1869 that this word “ ’low” has no kinship with “allow,” but is an independent word for which he gave a Low Latin original of similar sound. I have not been able to trace any such word, but Mr. Lowell had so much linguistic knowledge of the out-of-the-way sort that it may be worth while to record his impression. Bartlett is wrong in defining this word, as he is usually in his attempts to explain dialect outside of New England. It does not mean “to declare, assert, maintain,” etc. It is nearly the equivalent of “guess” in the Northern and Middle States, and of “reckon” in the South. It agrees precisely with the New England “calk’late.” Like all the rest of these words it may have a strong sense by irony. When a man says, “I ’low that is a purty peart sort of a hoss,” he understates for the sake of emphasis. It is rarely or never “allow,” but simply “ ’low.” In common with “calk’late,” it has sometimes a sense of purpose or expectation, as when a man says, “I ’low to go to town to-morry.” ↩︎

15. No phrase of the Hoosier and Southwestern dialect is such a stumbling-block to the outsider as “right smart.” The writer from the North or East will generally use it wrongly. Mrs. Stowe says, “I sold right smart of eggs,” but the Hoosier woman as I knew her would have said “a right smart lot of eggs” or “a right smart of eggs,” using the article and understanding the noun. A farmer omitting the preposition boasts of having “raised right smart corn” this year. No expression could have a more vague sense than this. In the early settlement of Minnesota it was a custom of the land officers to require a residence of about ten days on “a claim” in order to the establishment of a preemption right. One of the receivers at a land office under Buchanan’s administration was a German of much intelligence who was very sensitive regarding his knowledge of English. “How long has the claimant lived on his claim?” he demanded of a Hoosier witness. “Oh, a right smart while,” was the reply. The receiver had not the faintest notion of the meaning of the answer, but fearing to betray his ignorance of English he allowed the land to be entered, though the claimant had spent but about two hours in residing on his quarter-section. ↩︎

===Chinese===
14. 1869 年，洛厄尔先生向我提出，这个「’low」（寻思）同「allow」并无亲缘，而是一个独立的词，并替它拟了一个读音相近的中古拉丁源头。这样的词我没追到，但洛厄尔先生冷僻一路的语言学知识那样渊博，他这个印象值得记下一笔。巴特利特给这个词的释义是错的——他解释新英格兰以外的方言时，十有八九是错的。它并不是「to declare, assert, maintain」（宣称、断言、主张）等等。它在北部和中部诸州差不多等于「guess」（猜），在南部等于「reckon」（估摸），同新英格兰的「calk'late」（掐算）恰好相当。同这些词里的其余几个一样，它靠反话也能出重义。一个人说「I 'low that is a purty peart sort of a hoss」（俺寻思那可是匹怪伶俐的马），他是欲扬先抑。这词很少或从不作「allow」，就是光秃秃一个「’low」。同「calk'late」一样，它有时带打算或预期的意思，譬如说「I 'low to go to town to-morry」（俺打算明儿个进城）。 ↩︎

15. 胡希尔和西南方言里，再没有哪个短语像「right smart」（老大一截）这样叫外人绊跟头。北方或东部的作家用起来，十有八九要出错。斯托夫人写「I sold right smart of eggs」，可照我认识的胡希尔大娘，该说「a right smart lot of eggs」或「a right smart of eggs」——冠词要用上，名词心里要有。庄户人省掉介词，夸口今年「raised right smart corn」（收了老大一茬玉米）。再没有哪个说法，比这更没个准谱了。明尼苏达初辟的时候，土地官有个惯例：要立下优先购买权，须在一块「claim」（占垦地）上住满十天上下。布坎南任内某土地局的一位接收员，是个很有才智的德国人，对自己的英文极其要脸面。「申请人在这块地上住了多久？」他问一位胡希尔证人。「哦，a right smart while（老大一阵子）。」证人答道。接收员对这句回答的意思一星半点也不摸门，可又怕露了英文的怯，便准了那块地放领——其实申请人统共只在他那四分之一见方的地段上「住」了约莫两个钟头。 ↩︎

===Original===
16. Written in 1871. ↩︎

17. The original from which this character was drawn is here described accurately. The author now knows that such people are not to be put into books. They are not realistic enough. ↩︎

18. Absurd as this speech seems, it is a literal transcript of words spoken in the author’s presence by a woman who, like Miss Hawkins, was born in Massachusetts. ↩︎

19. When the first edition of this book appeared, the critic who analyzed the dialect in *The Nation* confessed that he did not know what to “crate” meant. It was a custom in the days of early Indiana barbarism for the youngsters of a village, on spying a sleeping drunkard, to hunt up a “queensware crate”—one of the cages of round withes in which crockery was shipped. This was turned upside down over the inebriate, and loaded with logs or any other heavy articles that would make escape difficult when the poor wretch should come to himself. It was a sort of rude punishment for inebriety, and it afforded a frog-killing delight to those who executed justice. ↩︎

20. Even the Anti-means Baptists have suffered from the dire spirit of the age. They are today a very respectable body of people calling themselves “Primitive Baptists.” Perhaps the description in the text never applied to the whole denomination, but only to the Hardshells of certain localities. Some of these intensely conservative churches, I have reason to believe, were always composed of reputable people. But what is said above is not in the least exaggerated as a description of many of the churches in Indiana and Illinois. Their opposition to the temperance reformation was both theoretical and practical. A rather able minister of the denomination whom I knew as a boy used to lie in besotted drunkenness by the roadside. I am sorry to confess that he once represented the county in the State legislature. The piece of a sermon given in this chapter was heard near Cairo, Illinois, in the days before the war. Most of the preachers were illiterate farmers. I have heard one of them hold forth two hours at a stretch. But even in that day there were men among the Hardshells whose ability and character commanded respect. This was true, especially in Kentucky, where able men like the two Dudleys held to the Antinomian wing of their denomination. But the Hardshells are perceptibly less hard than they were. You may march at the rear of the column among Hunkers and Hardshells if you will, but you are obliged to march. Those who will not go voluntarily, the time-spirit, walking behind, prods onward with a goad. ↩︎

===Chinese===
16. 写于 1871 年。 ↩︎

17. 这个人物所本的真人，这里写得照实。如今作者明白了：这样的人是不能写进书里的。他们不够写实。 ↩︎

18. 这番话看着荒唐，却是一位妇人当着作者的面所说原话的逐字记录——她同霍金斯小姐一样，生在马萨诸塞。 ↩︎

19. 本书初版问世时，*The Nation* 上分析方言的那位批评家承认，他不知道 to 「crate」（扣笼）是什么意思。印第安纳早期蒙昧年代有个风俗：村里的少年一见醉汉睡着，就去寻一只「queensware crate」——装运陶瓷的那种圆枝条编的笼子——倒扣在醉汉身上，再压上木头或随便什么重物，好叫那可怜虫醒过来时难以脱身。这是对酗酒的一种粗鲁惩罚，而执行司法的少年们，从中得到的是掏青蛙式的快乐。 ↩︎

20. 连反手段洗礼会也吃了这个时代凶神的苦头。他们如今是一群很可敬的人，自称「原始洗礼会」。也许正文里的描画从没有适用于整个教派，只适用于某些地方的硬壳派。我有理由相信，这些极端保守的教会中，有一些从来都由体面人组成。但上文所说，作为对印第安纳和伊利诺伊许多教会的描写，没有一丝一毫的夸张。他们对戒酒运动的反对，既是理论上的，也是行动上的。我小时候认得他们教派一位颇有才干的牧师，常烂醉如泥地躺在路边。我很抱歉地承认，这人还一度代表本县出任州议员。本章里那段布道词的片段，是战前在伊利诺伊开罗附近听到的。传道人多半是不识字的庄户人。我听过其中一个一连讲上两个钟头。但即使在那个年代，硬壳派里也有才具与人品都令人起敬的人。肯塔基尤其如此，像达德利兄弟那样有才干的人，就守着他们教派的反律法主义一翼。不过硬壳派终究比从前明显地软和了些。你尽可以吊在队伍的尾巴上，混在顽固派和硬壳派中间走，可你非走不可。那些不肯自动往前走的，时代精神就跟在后头，拿刺棒赶着他们上前。 ↩︎

===Original===
21. The elaborate etymological treatment of this word in its various forms in our best dictionary is a fine illustration of the fact that something more than scholarship is needed for penetrating the mysteries of current folk-speech. “Brash”—often “bresh”—in the sense of refuse, boughs of trees, is only another form of “brush”; the two are used as one word by the people. “Brash” in the sense of brittle has no conscious connection with the noun in popular usage, but it is accounted by the people the same word as “brash” in the sense of rash or impetuous. The suggestion in the *Century Dictionary* that the words spelled “brash” are of modern formation violates the soundest canon of antiquarian research, which is that a word phrase or custom widely diffused among plain or rustic people is of necessity of ancient origin. Now “brash,” the adjective, exists in both senses in two or three of the most widely separated dialects of the United States, and hence must have come from England. Indeed, it appears in Wright’s *Dictionary of Provincial English* in precisely the sense it has in the text. ↩︎

22. The total absence of the word “pail” not only from the dialect, but even from cultivated speech in the Southern and Border States until very recently, is a fact I leave to be explained on further investigation. The word is an old one and a good one, but I fancy that its use in England could not have been generally diffused in the seventeenth century. So a Hoosier or a Kentuckian never *pared* an apple, but *peeled* it. Much light might be thrown on the origin and history of our dialects by investigating their deficiencies. ↩︎

===Chinese===
21. 我们最好的词典里对这个词各种形态的详备词源考订，恰好说明一个道理：要参透当下民间口语的奥秘，光有学问还不够。「Brash」——常作「bresh」——作残枝败叶讲的那个，不过是「brush」的又一体；老百姓把两者当一个词用。作脆讲的「brash」，在民间用法里同那名词并无自觉的干系，可老百姓把它同鲁莽、性急讲的「brash」认作一个词。《世纪词典》说拼作「brash」的这些词是晚近造出来的，这一说违反了古俗研究最牢靠的一条准则：一个词、一个短语或一种习俗，凡在平民百姓间传布得广，必然来历古老。如今「brash」这个形容词的两种意义，在美国相隔最远的两三处方言里都存在，可见必是从英国来的。果然，赖特的《英吉利省方言词典》里就有它，意义恰与正文相同。 ↩︎

22. 「pail」（提桶）一词，直到晚近以前，不但不见于南方和边界诸州的方言，连那里有教养的口语里也没有——这个事实，我留给日后再细究。这是个老词、好词，但我料想它在英格兰的使用，到十七世纪也还没有传开。所以胡希尔人、肯塔基人从来不 *pare*（削）苹果，只 *peel*（剥）它。查一查咱们方言里的种种「没有」，大可以照亮这些方言的来历与身世。 ↩︎

===Original===
23. Some time after this book appeared Dr. Brown-Séquard announced his theory of the dual brain. A writer in an English magazine called attention to the fact that the discovery had been anticipated by an imaginative writer, and cited the passage in the text as proving that the author of *The Hoosier Schoolmaster* had outrun Dr. Brown-Séquard in perceiving the duality of the brain. It is a matter for surprise that an author, even an “imaginative” one, should have made so great a discovery without suspecting its meaning until it was explained by someone else. ↩︎

24. The reader may be interested to know that “Phil” was drawn from the life, as was old Mowley and in part “General Jackson” also. Between 1867 and 1870, I visited many jails and poorhouses with philanthropic purpose, publishing the results of my examination in some cases in *The Chicago Tribune*. Some of the abuses pointed out were reformed, others linger till this day, I believe. ↩︎

===Chinese===
23. 本书问世后不久，布朗-塞卡尔博士发表了他的大脑二元论。一家英文杂志的作者提请注意：这一发现早被一位想象派作家抢了先，并引正文那段为证，说《胡希尔校长》的作者在大脑二元的见识上，跑到了布朗-塞卡尔博士前头。令人称奇的是：一位作家，哪怕是「想象派」的，竟做出了这样大的发现，却直到旁人点破，才猜到它的意思。 ↩︎

24. 读者也许有兴趣知道：「菲尔」取自真人，老莫利也是，「杰克逊将军」也有一部分是。1867 至 1870 年间，我抱着慈善的目的走访了许多监狱和济贫院，有几次把考察结果发表在《芝加哥论坛报》上。所指出的弊端，有的已经改正，其余的，我相信苟延到了今天。 ↩︎

===Original===
25. I have already mentioned the absence of “pail” and “pare” from the ancient Hoosier folk-speech. “Brook” is likewise absent. The illiterate Indiana countryman before the Civil War, let us say, had no pails, pared no apples, husked no corn, crossed no brooks. The same is true, I believe, of the South generally. As the first settlers on the Southern coast entered the land by the rivers, each smaller stream was regarded as a branch of the larger one. A small stream was therefore called a “branch.” The word brook was probably lost in the first generation. But a small stream is often called a “run” in the Middle and Southern belt. Halliwell gives “rundel” as used with the same signification in England, and he gives “ryn” in the same sense from an old manuscript. ↩︎

26. “Juberous” is in none of the vocabularies that I have seen. I once treated this word in print as an undoubted corruption of “dubious,” and when used subjectively it apparently feels the influence of dubious, as where one says: “I feel mighty juberous about it.” But it is much oftener applied as in the text to the object of fear, as “The bridge looks kind o’ juberous.” Halliwell gives the verb “juberd” and defines it as “to jeopard or endanger.” It is clearly a dialect form of “jeopard,” and I make no doubt that “juberous” is a dialect variation of “jeopardous,” occasionally used as a form of “dubious.” ↩︎

===Chinese===
25. 我已提过古胡希尔口语里没有「pail」和「pare」。「brook」（小溪）同样没有。譬如说，内战前印第安纳不识字的乡下人：没有提桶，不削苹果，不剥玉米，也不过「brook」。我相信南方大体也是如此。南方沿海头一批移民是顺着河进的内陆，每条小些的水道都被看成大水的支流，小水道因此都叫「branch」（支溪）。「brook」这个词，多半在头一代移民手里就丢了。但在中部和南部地带，小水道常叫「run」。哈利韦尔收了「rundel」，说英格兰用它表同义，又从一部古抄本里引了同义的「ryn」。 ↩︎

26. 「Juberous」（邪乎）不在我见过的任何词汇表里。我有回在印出的文字里把它当作「dubious」的确定讹变来讨论；作主观用法时，它显然也受着 dubious 的熏染，譬如说：「I feel mighty juberous about it」（俺心里对这事老邪乎了）。但它更多像正文里那样，用在所怕的东西上，如「The bridge looks kind o' juberous」（那桥瞧着有点邪乎）。哈利韦尔收了动词「juberd」，释作「to jeopard or endanger」（冒险、使陷于危）。它显然是「jeopard」的方言形，我也不怀疑「juberous」是「jeopardous」的方言变体，偶尔又充「dubious」用。 ↩︎

===Original===
27. This form, “bagonet,” is not in the vocabularies, but it was spoken as I have written it. The *Century Dictionary* gives “bagnet,” and Halliwell and Wright both give “baginet” with the *g* soft apparently, though neither the one nor the other is very explicit in distinguishing transcriptions from old authors from phonetic spellings of dialect forms. I fancy that this “bagonet” is impossible as a corruption of “bayonet,” and that it points to some other derivation of that word than the doubtful one from “Bayonne.” ↩︎

28. Written in 1871. ↩︎

===Chinese===
27. 「bagonet」这个形态不见于各种词汇表，但当年确是这么说的。《世纪词典》收了「bagnet」，哈利韦尔和赖特都收了「baginet」——*g* 看来是软音，不过两人在分辨古籍转录与方言拼音这一点上，都不很清楚。我倒觉得，这个「bagonet」作为「bayonet」（刺刀）的讹变是不可能的，它倒是提示：这个词 另有渊源，未必是从「巴约讷」（Bayonne）来的那个悬而未决的说法。 ↩︎

28. 写于 1871 年。 ↩︎

## XIX Face to Face / 面对面

===Original===
In the lane, in the dark, under the shadow of the barn, Ralph met Hannah carrying her bucket of milk (they have no pails in Indiana).22 He could see only the white foam on the milk, and Hannah’s white face. Perhaps it was well that he could not see how white Hannah’s face was at that moment when a sudden trembling made her set down the heavy bucket. At first neither spoke. The recollection of all the joy of that walk together in the night came upon them both. And a great sense of loss made the night seem supernaturally dark to Ralph. Nor was it any lighter in the hopeless heart of the bound girl. The presence of Ralph did not now, as before, make the darkness of her life light.

“Hannah﻿—” said Ralph presently, and stopped. For he could not finish the sentence. With a rush there came upon him a consciousness of the suspicions that filled Hannah’s mind. And with it there came a feeling of guilt. He saw himself from her standpoint, and felt a remorse almost as keen as it could have been had he been a criminal. And this sudden and morbid sense of his guilt as it appeared to Hannah paralyzed him. But when Hannah lifted her bucket with her hand, and the world with her heavy heart, and essayed to pass him, Ralph rallied and said:

===Chinese===
在小巷里，黑暗中，谷仓的影子底下，拉尔夫迎面碰上了提着奶桶的汉娜（印第安纳那地方不兴说「提桶」）。22 他只看得见奶面上的白沫，和汉娜那张苍白的脸。她忽然一阵发颤，放下了沉重的奶桶——幸而他看不清那一刻汉娜的脸色有多白。起初两人都没作声。那一夜同路而行的种种欢愉，一齐涌上两人的心头。而一种巨大的失落感，让拉尔夫觉得这黑夜黑得超乎自然。在这位契约帮工的姑娘那颗绝望的心里，夜色也不见得亮些。拉尔夫的到来，已不像从前那样，能让她生活的黑暗透进光来。

「汉娜——」拉尔夫终于开了口，又停住了。这句话他没法说下去。汉娜心里满装的疑团，猛地涌上他的心头。随之而来的是一种负疚之感。他站到她的立场看自己，心里泛起一种几近罪人的痛悔。这种突然袭来的、病态的自我罪责感，让他说不出话来。可当汉娜一手提起奶桶、一颗沉重的心提起整个世界，想要从他身边走过去时，拉尔夫定了定神，说：

===Original===
“*You* don’t believe all these lies that are told about me.”

“I don’t believe anything, Mr. Hartsook; that is, I don’t want to believe anything against you. And I wouldn’t mind anything they say if it wasn’t for two things”﻿—here she stammered and looked down.

“If it wasn’t for what?” said Ralph with a spice of indignant denial in his voice.

Hannah hesitated, but Ralph pressed the question with eagerness.

“I saw you cross that bluegrass pasture the night﻿—the night that you walked home with me.” She would have said the night of the robbery, but her heart smote her, and she adopted the more kindly form of the sentence.

===Chinese===
「这些编派我的鬼话，*你*总不会信以为真吧。」

「我什么也不信，哈茨克先生；就是说，凡是说你坏话的，我都不愿意信。要不是有两件事，他们说什么我也不往心里去。」——说到这里她结结巴巴，垂下了眼睛。

「要不是因为什么？」拉尔夫说，话音里带着几分愤然的否认。

汉娜犹豫着，拉尔夫却急切地追问。

「那天夜里，我看见你穿过那片蓝草牧场——就是你送我回家的那天夜里。」她本想说「失窃那晚」，话到嘴边心里一软，换了个温和些的说法。

===Original===
Ralph would have explained, but how?

“I did cross the pasture,” he began, “but﻿—”

Just here it occurred to Ralph that there was no reason for his night excursion across the pasture. Hannah again took up her bucket, but he said:

“Tell me what else you have against me.”

“I haven’t anything against you. Only I am poor and friendless, and you oughtn’t to make my life any heavier. They say that you have paid attention to a great many girls. I don’t know why you should want to trifle with me.”

Ralph answered her this time. He spoke low. He spoke as though he were speaking to God. “If any man says that I ever trifled with any woman, he lies. I have never loved but one, and you know who that is. And God knows.”

===Chinese===
拉尔夫想解释，可怎么解释呢？

「我确实穿过了牧场，」他开口道，「可是——」

偏偏在这时，拉尔夫想到自己压根说不出夜里穿过牧场的理由。汉娜又提起了奶桶，他说：

「告诉我，你还有什么疑我的地方。」

「我没什么疑你的。只是我又穷又没依靠，你不该再把我的日子弄得更难。他们说你对好多姑娘都献过殷勤。我不知道你为什么要耍弄我。」

这回拉尔夫回答了她。他说得很低，就像在对上帝说话。「谁要是说我耍弄过哪个女人，他就是撒谎。我这一生只爱过一个，你知道那是谁。上帝也知道。」

===Original===
“I don’t know what to say, Mr. Hartsook.” Hannah’s voice was broken. These solemn words of love were like a river in the desert, and she was like a wanderer dying of thirst. “I don’t know, Mr. Hartsook. If I was alone, it wouldn’t matter. But I’ve got my blind mother and my poor Shocky to look after. And I don’t want to make mistakes. And the world is so full of lies I don’t know what to believe. Somehow I can’t help believing what you say. You seem to speak so true. But﻿—”

“But what?” said Ralph.

“But you know how I saw you just as kind to Martha Hawkins on Sunday as﻿—as﻿—”

“Han‑ner!” It was the melodious voice of the angry Mrs. Means, and Hannah lifted her pail and disappeared.

Standing in the shadow of his own despair, Ralph felt how dark a night could be when it had no promise of morning.

===Chinese===
「我不知道该说什么，哈茨克先生。」汉娜的声音哽咽了。这几句庄重的情话，就像沙漠里的河流，而她像一个渴得要死的旅人。「我不知道，哈茨克先生。要是我孤身一人，倒也罢了。可我还有瞎眼的母亲和可怜的肖基要照看。我不想走错一步。这世上谎言太多，我都不知道该信什么。可不知怎么，你说的话我不由得不信。你说得那么诚恳。可是——」

「可是什么？」拉尔夫说。

「可是你知道，礼拜天我瞧见你对玛莎·霍金斯那般殷勤，就跟——跟——」

「汉——娜——！」是怒气冲冲的米恩斯太太那悠扬的嗓音，汉娜提起桶，一闪身不见了。

站在自身绝望的阴影里，拉尔夫这才体会到，一个不承诺黎明的夜晚，能有多么黑。

===Original===
And Dr. Small, who had been stabling his horse just inside the barn, came out and moved quietly into the house just as though he had not listened intently to every word of the conversation.

As Ralph walked away he tried to comfort himself by calling to his aid the bulldog in his character. But somehow it did not do him any good. For what is a bulldog but a stoic philosopher? Stoicism has its value, but Ralph had come to a place where stoicism was of no account. The memory of the Helper, of his sorrow, his brave and victorious endurance, came when stoicism failed. Happiness might go out of life, but in the light of Christ’s life happiness seemed but a small element anyhow. The love of woman might be denied him, but there still remained what was infinitely more precious and holy, the love of God. There still remained the possibility of heroic living. Working, suffering, and enduring still remained. And he who can work for God and endure for God, surely has yet the best of life left. And, like the knights who could find the Holy Grail only in losing themselves, Hartsook, in throwing his happiness out of the count, found the purest happiness, a sense of the victory of the soul over the tribulations of life. The man who knows this victory scarcely needs the encouragement of the hope of future happiness. There is a real heaven in bravely lifting the load of one’s own sorrow and work.

And it was a good thing for Ralph that the danger hanging over Shocky made immediate action necessary.

===Chinese===
而斯莫尔医生——他方才正在谷仓里安顿他的马——走了出来，悄悄进了屋，那副模样，就好像没有竖着耳朵把这场谈话一字不漏地听进去似的。

拉尔夫走开时，试着唤起自己性格里那条斗牛犬来宽慰自己。可不知怎么，这回不管用了。斗牛犬不就是一位坚忍的哲人么？坚忍自有它的价值，可拉尔夫已经走到了坚忍无济于事的境地。坚忍失效之时，那位救助者的音容——他的忧患、他勇敢而得胜的坚忍——浮上心头。幸福尽可以从生活里消失，但在基督生命之光的照耀下，幸福本来也不过是件小东西。女人的爱也许与他无缘，可还有远为珍贵、远为圣洁的东西留了下来：上帝之爱。英雄式地活着，仍然可能。做工、受苦、坚忍，都还在。能为上帝做工、为上帝坚忍的人，无疑仍握着人生最好的那一份。就像那些骑士，唯有忘掉自己才寻得圣杯；哈茨克把幸福从账上勾销，反倒寻得了至纯的幸福——一种灵魂战胜人生忧患的胜利之感。懂得这种胜利的人，几乎用不着靠将来幸福的指望来给自己打气。勇敢地扛起自己的忧愁与劳作，这本身就是实实在在的天堂。

而悬在肖基头上的危险逼得人不得不立刻行动，这对拉尔夫倒是一件好事。

## XX God Remembers Shocky / 上帝记着肖基

===Original===
At four o’clock the next morning, in the midst of a driving snow, Ralph went timidly up the lane toward the homely castle of the Meanses. He went timidly, for he was afraid of Bull. But he found Bud waiting for him, with the roan colt bridled and saddled. The roan colt was really a large three-year-old, full of the finest sort of animal life, and having, as Bud declared, “a mighty sight of hoss sense fer his age.” He seemed to understand at once that there was something extraordinary on hand when he was brought out of his comfortable quarters at four in the morning in the midst of a snowstorm. Bud was sure that the roan colt felt his responsibility.

In the days that followed, Ralph often had occasion to remember this interview with Bud, who had risked much in bringing his fractured arm out into the cold, damp air. Jonathan never clave to David more earnestly than did Bud this December morning to Ralph.

===Chinese===
第二天凌晨四点，风雪扑面，拉尔夫怯生生地顺着小巷，朝米恩斯家那座土里土气的城堡走去。他之所以怯生生，是因为怕布尔。可他发现巴德已在等他，还牵出了鞍辔齐备的那匹沙毛马驹。这匹沙毛马驹其实是匹高大的三岁马，浑身上下满是顶精神的牲口劲儿，而且正如巴德所说，「就它这岁数，懂的马事儿可多着哩」。清早四点、风雪正紧的当口被从暖和的棚里牵出来，它似乎立刻明白眼下有大事要办。巴德一口咬定，这匹沙毛马驹是知道自己的责任分量的。

在随后的日子里，拉尔夫常常会想起这次与巴德的会面——为了这一趟，巴德硬是把自己那条断胳膊带到了又冷又潮的户外。当年约拿单（Jonathan）依恋大卫（David）再诚笃，也比不上这个十二月的早晨巴德依恋拉尔夫。

===Original===
“You see, Mr. Hartsook,” said Bud, “I wish I was well myself. It’s hard to set still. But it’s a-doing me a heap of good. I’m like a boy at school. And I’m a-findin’ out that doing one’s best licks fer others ain’t all they is of it, though it’s a good part. I feel like as if I must git Him, you know, to do lots for me. They’s always some sums too hard fer a feller, and he has to ax the master to do ’em, you know. But see, the roan’s a-stomping round. He wants to be off. Do you know I think that hoss knows something’s up? I think he puts in his best licks fer me a good deal better than I do fer Him.”

Ralph pressed Bud’s right hand. Bud rubbed his face against the colt’s nose and said: “Put in your best licks, old fellow.” And the colt whinnied. How a horse must want to speak! For Bud was right. Men are gods to horses, and they serve their deities with a faithfulness that shames us.

Then Ralph sprang into the saddle, and the roan, as if wishing to show Bud his willingness, broke into a swinging gallop, and was soon lost to the sight of his master in the darkness and the snow. When Bud could no more hear the sound of the roan’s footsteps he returned to the house, to lie awake picturing to himself the journey of Ralph with Shocky and the roan colt. It was a great comfort to Bud that the roan, which was almost a part of himself, represented him in this ride. And he knew the roan well enough to feel sure that he would do credit to his master. “He’ll put in his best licks,” Bud whispered to himself many a time before daybreak.

===Chinese===
「你瞧，哈茨克先生，」巴德说，「俺倒是盼着自个儿好好的。坐着不动窝，实在难受。可这对俺大有好处。俺就像个上学的娃娃。俺琢磨出来了：光给旁人卖死力气还不够——虽说那也是顶要紧的一份。俺觉着俺还得求他老人家替俺办好些事，你懂的。总有好些算术题难得很，娃娃自个儿算不上来，就得求老师给算，你说是吧。可你瞧，沙毛马在那儿刨蹄子哩。它想动身了。你说怪不怪，俺瞅着这马知道要出事哩。俺瞅着它替俺卖力气，倒比俺替他老人家卖力气还尽心。」

拉尔夫握了握巴德的右手。巴德把脸贴在马驹的鼻子上蹭了蹭，说：「老伙计，好好给俺卖力气。」马驹嘶鸣了一声。马儿该多想开口说话啊！因为巴德说得没错。人在马眼里就是神明，而马侍奉它们的神明，那份忠心叫我们人类羞惭。

于是拉尔夫翻身上鞍。那匹沙毛马仿佛要让巴德看看自己有多情愿，撒开步子跑出一串悠扬的大步，不多时便消失在黑暗和风雪里，主人再看不见它。等巴德再也听不见沙毛马的蹄声，他才回屋去躺着，睁着眼睛，一路在心里描画拉尔夫带着肖基和沙毛马驹的这趟行程。这趟行程由这匹几乎算得上他自身一部分的沙毛马来代表他，对巴德是天大的安慰。他太了解这匹马了，确信它不会给主人丢脸。「它会卖尽力气的。」天亮之前，巴德一遍又一遍地这样喃喃自语。

===Original===
The ground was but little frozen, and the snow made the roads more slippery than ever. But the roughshod roan handled his feet dexterously and with a playful and somewhat self-righteous air, as though he said: “Didn’t I do it handsomely that time?” Down slippery hills, through deep mud-holes covered with a slender film of ice he trod with perfect assurance. And then up over the rough stones of Rocky Hollow, where there was no road at all, he picked his way through the darkness and snow. Ralph could not tell where he was at last, but gave the reins to the roan, who did his duty bravely, and not without a little flourish, to show that he had yet plenty of spare power.

A feeble candle-ray, making the dense snowfall visible, marked for Ralph the site of the basket-maker’s cabin. Miss Martha had been admitted to the secret, and had joined in the conspiracy heartily, without being able to recall anything of the kind having occurred at the East, and not remembering having seen or heard of anything of the sort the time she was to Bosting. She had Shocky all ready, having used some of her own capes and shawls to make him warm.

Miss Martha came out to meet Ralph when she heard the feet of the roan before the door.

===Chinese===
地面只结了薄薄一层冻，雪把路面弄得比先前更滑。可这匹钉了防滑蹄铁的沙毛马，四只蹄子拾掇得干净利落，还带着几分顽皮又自负的神气，仿佛在说：「刚才那一下，俺走得是不是漂亮？」滑溜溜的坡道往下走，覆着一层薄冰的深泥坑里趟过去，它都稳稳当当。随后上到乱石谷（Rocky Hollow）那乱石累累的坡上——那里压根没有路——它在黑暗和风雪里择路而行。到后来拉尔夫已辨不清自己身在何处，索性把缰绳交给沙毛马。这马勇敢地尽了本分，还不忘略略卖弄一下，表示自己尚有余力。

一豆微弱的烛光，映得漫天密雪可见，为拉尔夫标出了编筐匠小屋的所在。玛莎小姐已被告知了这个秘密，并且热切地加入了这场密谋——虽说她在东部想不起有过这等事，也不记得她去波士顿（Bosting，她管 Boston 就这么叫）那阵子见过或听说过类似的事。她已把肖基收拾停当，还把自己的几件披肩和围脖给他裹上取暖。

玛莎小姐听见门前沙毛马的蹄声，便出来迎接拉尔夫。

===Original===
“O Mr. Hartsook! is that you? What a storm. This is jest the way it snows at the East. Shocky’s all ready. He didn’t know a thing about it tell I waked him this morning. Ever since that he’s been saying that God hasn’t forgot, after all. It’s made me cry more’n once.” And Shocky kissed Mrs. Pearson, and told her that when he got away from Flat Creek he’d tell God all about it, and God would bring Mr. Pearson back again. And then Martha Hawkins lifted the frail little form, bundled in shawls, in her arms, and brought him out into the storm; and before she handed him up he embraced her, and said: “O Miss Hawkins! God ha’n’t forgot me, after all. Tell Hanner that He ha’n’t forgot. I’m going to ask him to git her away from Means’s and mother out of the poorhouse. I’ll ask him just as soon as I get to Lewisburg.”

===Chinese===
「哎哟，哈茨克先生！真是你呀？好大的雪。东部下雪就是这样的。肖基都收拾好啦。今儿早上我叫醒他，他才知道这事儿。打那会儿起，他就一直念叨，说上帝到底没有忘掉。听得我都不知哭了几回。」肖基亲了亲皮尔逊太太（Mrs. Pearson），对她说，等他离开了弗拉特溪（Flat Creek），他要把这一切都告诉上帝，上帝会把皮尔逊先生带回来的。随后玛莎·霍金斯抱起裹在披肩里那瘦弱的小身子，把他抱进风雪里；交上马背之前，他搂住她，说：「哦，霍金斯小姐！上帝到底没有忘掉我。你告诉汉娜，他没有忘掉。我要去求他把汉娜从米恩斯家弄出来，把妈妈救出贫民院。我一到刘易斯堡（Lewisburg）就求他。」

===Original===
Ralph lifted the trembling form into his arms, and the little fellow only looked up in the face of the master and said: “You see, Mr. Hartsook, I thought God had forgot. But he ha’n’t.”

And the words of the little boy comforted the master also. God had not forgotten him, either!

From the moment that Ralph took Shocky into his arms, the conduct of the roan colt underwent an entire revolution. Before that he had gone over a bad place with a rush, as though he were ambitious of distinguishing himself by his brilliant execution. Now he trod none the less surely, but he trod tenderly. The neck was no longer arched. He set himself to his work as steadily as though he were twenty years old. For miles he traveled on in a long, swinging walk, putting his feet down carefully and firmly. And Ralph found the spirit of the colt entering into himself. He cut the snowstorm with his face, and felt a sense of triumph over all his difficulties. The bulldog’s jaws had been his teacher, and now the steady, strong, and conscientious legs of the roan inspired him.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫把那颤抖的小身子接进怀里。小家伙只是仰起脸望着老师，说：「你瞧，哈茨克先生，我原以为上帝忘掉了。可他没有。」

小男孩的这句话，也安慰了老师。上帝也没有忘掉他！

打从拉尔夫把肖基接进怀里的那一刻起，沙毛马驹的做派来了个彻底的改变。先前遇到难走的路段，它总要冲过去，仿佛存心以一手漂亮的脚下功夫露露脸。如今它依旧走得稳，却走得轻。脖颈不再高拱。它扎扎实实做起活来，稳当得像匹二十岁的老马。一连数里，它迈着悠长摇摆的步子，落脚又仔细又坚定。拉尔夫觉得这马驹的精神也进了自己的身体。他用脸劈开风雪，心里生出一种战胜一切艰难的豪情。从前开导他的是斗牛犬的牙口，如今激励他的是沙毛马这四条沉稳、有力、尽本分的腿。

===Original===
Shocky had not spoken. He lay listening to the pattering music of the horse’s feet, doubtless framing the footsteps of the roan colt into an anthem of praise to the God who had not forgot. But as the dawn came on, making the snow whiter, he raised himself and said half-aloud, as he watched the flakes chasing one another in whirling eddies, that the snow seemed to be having a good time of it. Then he leaned down again on the master’s bosom, full of a still joy, and only roused himself from his happy reverie to ask what that big, ugly-looking house was.

“See, Mr. Hartsook, how big it is, and how little and ugly the windows is! And the boards is peeling off all over it, and the hogs is right in the front yard. It don’t look just like a house. It looks dreadful. What is it?”

Ralph had dreaded this question. He did not answer it, but asked Shocky to change his position a little, and then he quickened the pace of the horse. But Shocky was a poet, and a poet understands silence more quickly than he does speech. The little fellow shivered as the truth came to him.

===Chinese===
肖基一直没有作声。他躺着，听马蹄嗒嗒的音乐，多半是在心里把这沙毛马驹的蹄声，编成一首赞美那没有忘掉人的上帝的颂歌。天色渐亮，雪显得更白了，他直起身子，望着雪花在打旋的风里你追我赶，半出声地说，这些雪花瞧着玩得真开心。随后他又伏在老师怀里，满心是静静的喜悦，只从那快活的遐想里抬起头来问了一句：那边那座又大又丑的房子是什么地方。

「你瞧，哈茨克先生，它多大呀，窗户又小又难看！墙板一块块都翘起来了，猪就蹲在前院里。它不像座正经房子。它瞧着吓人。那是什么呀？」

拉尔夫就怕这一问。他没有回答，只请肖基稍微挪一挪身子，随后催快了马。可肖基是个诗人，而诗人对沉默的领会比对话语更快。当真相来到他心里时，小家伙打了个寒噤。

===Original===
“Is that the poorhouse?” he said, catching his breath. “Is my mother in that place? *Won’t* you take me in there, so as I can just kiss her once? ’Cause she can’t see much, you know. And one kiss from me will make her feel so good. And I’ll tell her that God ha’n’t forgot.” He had raised up and caught hold of Ralph’s coat.

Ralph had great difficulty in quieting him. He told him that if he went in there Bill Jones might claim that he was a runaway and belonged there. And poor Shocky only shivered and said he was cold. A minute later, Ralph found that he was shaking with a chill, and a horrible dread came over him. What if Shocky should die? It was only a minute’s work to get down, take the warm horse-blanket from under the saddle, and wrap it about the boy, then to strip off his own overcoat and add that to it. It was now daylight, and finding, after he had mounted, that Shocky continued to shiver, he put the roan to his best speed for the rest of the way, trotting up and down the slippery hills, and galloping away on the level ground. How bravely the roan laid himself to his work, making the fence-corners fly past in a long procession! But poor little Shocky was too cold to notice them, and Ralph shuddered lest Shocky should never be warm again, and spoke to the roan, and the roan stretched out his head, and dropped one ear back to hear the first word of command, and stretched the other forward to listen for danger, and then flew with a splendid speed down the road, past the patches of blackberry briars, past the elderberry bushes, past the familiar red-haw tree in the fence-corner, over the bridge without regard to the threat of a five-dollar fine, and at last up the long lane into the village, where the smoke from the chimneys was caught and whirled round with the snow.

===Chinese===
「那是贫民院吗？」他屏着气说。「我妈妈就在那里面？你*千万*不能带我进去一趟，让我就亲她一下吗？你知道的，她眼神不济。我亲她一下，她心里就会好受多了。我要告诉她，上帝没有忘掉。」他已经直起身子，一把抓住了拉尔夫的外衣。

拉尔夫费了好大的劲才让他安静下来。他告诉肖基，要是进了那里面，比尔·琼斯（Bill Jones）说不定会一口咬定他是个逃出来的、本来就该归那儿管的孩子。可怜的肖基只是打颤，说他冷。一分钟后，拉尔夫发现他冷得浑身发抖，一阵可怕的惊惧攫住了自己。万一肖基死掉怎么办？下马、抽出鞍下那床暖和的马毯裹在孩子身上、再脱下自己的大衣加上去，前后不过一分钟的工夫。天已大亮。上马之后见肖基还在发抖，他便让沙毛马撒开全速跑完剩下的路：滑溜溜的坡道上上下下地小跑，平地上便放开四蹄飞奔。沙毛马多么勇猛地扑在自己的活计上，一长串篱笆拐角从身边飞掠而过！可可怜的小肖基冷得顾不上看这些。拉尔夫心里发怵，生怕肖基再也暖和不过来，便吆喝了一声；沙毛马把头一伸，一只耳朵向后收着，听主人的第一声号令，另一只向前竖着，防备危险，随即以一种漂亮的极速沿路飞驰——掠过一丛丛黑莓刺棵，掠过一蓬蓬接骨木，掠过篱笆拐角那棵熟悉的红山楂树，冲过那座全不管五块钱罚金威吓的桥，最后跑上长长的巷道进了村子。村里烟囱里冒出的烟，正被风卷进雪里，和雪片一同打着旋。

## IX Has God Forgotten Shocky? / 上帝忘掉肖基了吗？

===Original===
“Pap wants to know ef you would spend to-morry and Sunday at our house?” said one of Squire Hawkins’s girls, on the very next evening, which was Friday. The old Squire was thoughtful enough to remember that Ralph would not find it very pleasant “boarding out” all the time he was entitled to spend at Pete Jones’s. For in view of the fact that Mr. Pete Jones sent seven children to the school, the “master” in Flat Creek district was bound to spend two weeks in that comfortable place, sleeping in a preoccupied bed, in the “furdest corner,” with insufficient cover, under an insufficient roof, and eating floating islands of salt pork fished out of oceans of hot lard. Ralph was not slow to accept the relief offered by the hospitable justice of the peace, whose principal business seemed to be the adjustment of the pieces of which he was composed. And as Shocky traveled the same road, Ralph took advantage of the opportunity to talk with him. The master could not dismiss Hannah wholly from his mind. He would at least read the mystery of her life, if Shocky could be prevailed on to furnish the clue.

===Chinese===
「俺爹想问问你，明儿个和礼拜天，肯不肯上俺家去过？」第二天傍晚——那是礼拜五——霍金斯乡绅（Squire Hawkins）家的一位姑娘这么问。老乡绅心思周到，想到拉尔夫在皮特·琼斯（Pete Jones）家该搭伙的那段日子若全靠「外宿」打发，怕不会好过。因为皮特·琼斯先生送了七个孩子上学，弗拉特溪学区的「老师」按规定就得在那户舒坦人家住上两星期：睡一张被人先睡过的床，睡在「顶里头的角落」，盖的不够厚，房顶也不严实，吃的是从滚烫猪油的大洋里捞出来的咸肉浮岛。这位好客的治安法官递来解围的好意，拉尔夫一点也没有迟疑就接下了——这位法官的主要营生，似乎就是把他浑身上下的零件逐一调摆妥帖。而肖基走的是同一条路，拉尔夫便趁这个机会跟他说话。老师的心里放不下汉娜。哪怕肖基肯提供线索，让他至少读懂她生命里的谜也好。

===Original===
