But while I have not ventured to discuss the provincialisms of the Indiana backwoods, I have been careful to preserve the true *usus loquendi* of each locution, and I trust my little story may afford material for someone better qualified than I to criticise the dialect.

===Chinese===
有几位对「校长」和他的朋友们说过好话的，提出这部小说是一部自传。但它不是——除非按每一件艺术品都是自传的那个意思来讲：它出自作者的经验与观察。因此请读者记住：拉尔夫也好，巴德也好，「所多玛弟兄」也好，斯莫尔大夫也好，都不代表作者本人；我也没有像塔列朗说斯塔尔夫人那样，「化装成一个女人」，附在汉娜或米兰迪身上。有些情节取自生活；但据我所信，没有一桩取自我的生活。不过，「最卖力气的教会」的会籍，我倒乐意领一份。

我原也想附上一些关于方言的议论——语言学的，还有别的——可洛厄尔教授为《比格罗诗稿》（Biglow Papers）所作的那篇精妙而博学的序言，注定叫一切有意写美式英语的人绝望。洛厄尔先生的荣衔在于：在我们最杰出的作家中间、也在我们最渊博的学者中间，唯独他一个人认真留意过美国的方言。我不敢妄议印第安纳老林子里的土话，却小心保存了每一处说法的真正*惯用法*（usus loquendi），并且指望我这个小故事能给哪位比我更有资格的人提供批评这份方言的材料。

===Original===
I wish to dedicate this book to Rev. Williamson Terrell, D.D. of Columbus, Indiana, the Hoosier that I know best, and the best Hoosier that I know. This is not the place to express the reverence and filial affection I feel for him, but I am glad of the opportunity of saying that there is no one to whom Southern Indiana owes a larger debt. Perhaps my dedication to so orthdox a man may atone for any heresies in the book.

Brooklyn, December, 1871.

===Chinese===
我愿把这本书献给印第安纳州哥伦布城的威廉森·特雷尔牧师（Rev. Williamson Terrell, D.D.，神学博士）——他是我最熟识的胡希尔人，也是我所识的最好的胡希尔人。我对他怀着的敬爱与孺慕之情，不是在这里抒发的场合，但我乐于借这个机会说一句：南印第安纳受惠于他的人，再没有第二个。把书献给这样一位正统人物，或许可以抵赎书里的种种异端。

布鲁克林，1871年12月。

## IV Spelling Down the Master / 四 拼倒先生

===Original===
“I ’low,” said Mrs. Means, as she stuffed the tobacco into her cob pipe after supper on that eventful Wednesday evening: “I ’low they’ll app’int the Squire to gin out the words tonight. They mos’ always do, you see, kase he’s the peartest13 *ole* man in this deestrick; and I ’low some of the young fellers would have to git up and dust ef they would keep up to him. And he uses sech remarkable smart words. He speaks so polite, too. But laws! don’t I remember when he was poarer nor Job’s turkey? Twenty year ago, when he come to these ’ere diggin’s, that air Squire Hawkins was a poar Yankee schoolmaster, that said ‘pail’ instid of bucket, and that called a cow a ‘caow,’ and that couldn’t tell to save his gizzard what we meant by *’low*14 and by *right smart*.15 But he’s larnt our ways now, an’ he’s jest as civilized as the rest of us. You would-n know he’d ever been a Yankee. He didn’t stay poar long. Not he. He jest married a right rich girl! He! he!” And the old woman grinned at Ralph, and then at Mirandy, and then at the rest, until Ralph shuddered. Nothing was so frightful to him as to be fawned on by this grinning ogre, whose few lonesome, blackish teeth seemed ready to devour him. “He didn’t stay poar, you bet a hoss!” and with this the coal was deposited on the pipe, and the lips began to crack like parchment as each puff of smoke escaped. “He married rich, you see,” and here another significant look at the young master, and another fond look at Mirandy, as she puffed away reflectively. “His wife hadn’t no book-larnin’. She’d been through the spellin’-book wunst, and had got as fur as ‘asperity’ on it a second time. But she couldn’t read a word when she was married, and never could. She warn’t overly smart. She hadn’t hardly got the sense the law allows. But schools was skase in them air days, and, besides, book-larnin’ don’t do no good to a woman. Makes her stuck up. I never knowed but one gal in my life as had ciphered into fractions, and she was so dog-on stuck up that she turned up her nose one night at a apple-peelin’ bekase I tuck a sheet off the bed to splice out the tablecloth, which was rather short. And the sheet was mos’ clean too. Had-n been slep on more’n wunst or twicet. But I was goin’ fer to say that when Squire Hawkins married Virginny Gray he got a heap o’ money, or, what’s the same thing mostly, a heap o’ good land. And that’s better’n book-larnin’, says I. Ef a gal had gone clean through all eddication, and got to the rule of three itself, that would-n buy a featherbed. Squire Hawkins jest put eddication agin the gal’s farm, and traded even, an’ ef ary one of ’em got swindled, I never heerd no complaints.”

And here she looked at Ralph in triumph, her hard face splintering into the hideous semblance of a smile. And Mirandy cast a blushing, gushing, all-imploring, and all-confiding look on the young master.

“I say, ole woman,” broke in old Jack, “I say, wot is all this ’ere spoutin’ about the Square fer?” and old Jack, having bit off an ounce of “pigtail,” returned the plug to his pocket.

As for Ralph, he fell into a sort of terror. He had a guilty feeling that this speech of the old lady’s had somehow committed him beyond recall to Mirandy. He did not see visions of breach-of-promise suits. But he trembled at the thought of an avenging big brother.

===Chinese===
「俺估摸着，」米恩斯太太说。那个要紧的星期三晚上，吃过晚饭，她正往玉米芯烟斗里塞烟丝。「俺估摸着，今儿晚上他们准会指派治安官来发词。他们多半回回都派他，你知道，因为他是这学区里13最*精神*的老头儿；俺看有些年轻后生想跟上他，得撒开腿丫子赶才行。他用的词儿聪明得不得了。他说话还那么客气。可是老天！俺不记得他当年比约伯的火鸡还穷么？二十年前，他刚到这地界来的时候，这位霍金斯治安官是个穷北方佬教书匠，开口是『pail』，不兴说『bucket』，管母牛叫『caow』，你就是要了他的命，他也闹不明白俺们说的*『估摸』*14和*『老大一截』*15是啥意思。可他如今学会俺们的规矩了，跟俺们大伙儿一样文明。你瞧不出他当过北方佬。他没穷多久。他才没有呢。他干脆娶了个顶有钱的姑娘！嘿！嘿！」老太婆冲着拉尔夫咧嘴笑，又冲米兰迪咧嘴笑，再冲着其余的人咧嘴笑，笑得拉尔夫直打哆嗦。在这咧着嘴的妖婆跟前被讨好，天底下没有比这更吓人的事了：她那几颗孤零零的发黑牙齿，看着随时要把他吞下去。「他可没穷多久，赌一匹马都成！」说着，她把火炭按在烟斗上，一口烟冒出来，两片嘴唇便像羊皮纸似的干裂作响。「他娶了钱，你瞧，」说着又意味深长地瞟了年轻先生一眼，又疼爱地瞟了米兰迪一眼，一边若有所思地喷着烟。「他婆娘没啥书本学问。拼字课本她从头到尾过了一遍，第二回啃到了『asperity（刻薄）』那儿。可她到结婚的时候一个字也不认得，往后也一直没认得。她不算灵光。她脑子里的见识，比法律许给的还缺那么一点儿。可那些年头学校稀罕，再说，书本学问对女人没啥好处，倒叫她翘尾巴。俺这辈子就认得一个闺女，算术学进了分数，她翘尾巴翘得那个厉害，一晚上削苹果，就因为俺扯了条床单来接长桌布——那桌布短了点儿——她就拿鼻子哼人。那床单还差不多是干净的，统共没睡过一两回。可俺方才要说的是：霍金斯治安官娶弗吉妮·格雷的时候，得了一大笔钱，或者说——多半是一码事——一大片好地。依俺说，这可比书本学问强。一个闺女就是把全天下的学问念穿了，念到三率法本尊，也换不来一床鸭绒被。霍金斯治安官干脆拿学问抵那姑娘的田产，公平交易，哪一边吃了亏，俺可没听着谁抱怨过。」

说完，她得意洋洋地望着拉尔夫，那张硬脸碎成一副笑的可怕模样。米兰迪则朝年轻先生投去一瞥：红着脸，热乎乎的，全是恳求，也全是信托。

「俺说，老婆子，」老杰克插嘴道，「俺说，冲着治安官这么喷唾沫星子干啥？」说着，老杰克咬下约莫一盎司「猪尾嚼烟」，把剩下的烟块儿揣回兜里。

至于拉尔夫，他陷入了一种恐慌。他有种心虚的感觉：老太太这一番话，不知怎么已经把他跟米兰迪拴死，无法反悔了。他倒没有看见什么毁约官司的幻象，可一想到一位替妹妹出气的大哥，他就发抖。
===Original===
“Hanner, you kin come along, too, ef you’re a mind, when you git the dishes washed,” said Mrs. Means to the bound girl, as she shut and latched the back door. The Means family had built a new house in front of the old one, as a sort of advertisement of bettered circumstances, an eruption of shoddy feeling; but when the new building was completed, they found themselves unable to occupy it for anything else than a lumber room, and so, except a parlor which Mirandy had made an effort to furnish a little (in hope of the blissful time when somebody should “set up” with her of evenings), the new building was almost unoccupied, and the family went in and out through the back door, which, indeed, was the front door also, for, according to a curious custom, the “front” of the house was placed toward the south, though the “big road” (Hoosier for *highway*) ran along the northwest side, or, rather, past the northwest corner of it.

When the old woman had spoken thus to Hannah and had latched the door, she muttered, “That gal don’t never show no gratitude fer favors;” to which Bud rejoined that he didn’t think she had no great sight to be pertickler thankful fer. To which Mrs. Means made no reply, thinking it best, perhaps, not to wake up her dutiful son on so interesting a theme as her treatment of Hannah. Ralph felt glad that he was this evening to go to another boarding place. He should not hear the rest of the controversy.

Ralph walked to the schoolhouse with Bill. They were friends again. For when Hank Banta’s ducking and his dogged obstinacy in sitting in his wet clothes had brought on a serious fever, Ralph had called together the big boys, and had said: “We must take care of one another, boys. Who will volunteer to take turns sitting up with Henry?” He put his own name down, and all the rest followed.

“William Means and myself will sit up tonight,” said Ralph. And poor Bill had been from that moment the teacher’s friend. He was chosen to be Ralph’s companion. He was Puppy Means no longer! Hank could not be conquered by kindness, and the teacher was made to feel the bitterness of his resentment long after. But Bill Means was for the time entirely placated, and he and Ralph went to spelling-school together.

===Chinese===
「汉娜，你把碗洗完了，要是乐意，也一块儿来。」米恩斯太太对那个抵债姑娘说着，随手关上后门、落了闩。米恩斯家在老屋前头盖了座新房，算是家境见好的广告，是一股子暴发户脾气长出来的疹子。可新屋落成之后，他们发觉它除了当堆破烂的杂物房，派不上别的用场。于是，除了一间米兰迪着力置办过一下的客厅（指望着有福的日子，晚上有谁来陪她「坐坐」），新房差不多空着，一家人照旧从后门进出。这后门，其实也是前门——因为照一种古怪的风俗，房子的「正面」朝南，尽管「大路」（印第安纳话，就是*公路*）打西北边过，或者说，擦着房子的西北角过去。

老太婆对汉娜交代完、闩了门，嘴里咕哝：「这丫头得了好处，从来不知个感恩。」巴德接话说，他寻思她也没啥大不了的可感恩的。这话米恩斯太太没接茬——兴许是觉得，在她怎么待汉娜这个有趣题目上，还是别唤醒她那孝顺儿子为妙。拉尔夫暗自庆幸，今晚要去另一家搭伙了，这场争论的下文他听不着了。

拉尔夫同比尔一道走去学校。他们又成了朋友。汉克·班塔那次落水，加上他死心眼儿穿着湿衣裳坐着不换，害了一场大病，拉尔夫把大孩子们召到一起，说：「咱们得互相照应，伙计们。谁愿意报名，轮流陪亨利守夜？」他先写下了自己的名字，其余的人跟着都写了。

「今晚威廉·米恩斯跟我守。」拉尔夫说。可怜的比尔打那一刻起就成了这位教师的朋友，还被选作拉尔夫的同伴。他再也不是「小牛头犬米恩斯」了！汉克是好心感化不了的，教师往后还久久尝着他那份怨恨的苦味。可比尔·米恩斯这阵子全然消了气，跟拉尔夫一道上拼字学校去了。

===Original===
Every family furnished a candle. There were yellow dips and white dips, burning, smoking, and flaring. There was laughing, and talking, and giggling, and simpering, and ogling, and flirting, and courting. What a full-dress party is to Fifth Avenue, a spelling-school is to Hoopole County. It is an occasion which is metaphorically inscribed with this legend: “Choose your partners.” Spelling is only a blind in Hoopole County, as is dancing on Fifth Avenue. But as there are some in society who love dancing for its own sake, so in Flat Creek district there were those who loved spelling for its own sake, and who, smelling the battle from afar, had come to try their skill in this tournament, hoping to freshen the laurels they had won in their schooldays.

“I ’low,” said Mr. Means, speaking as the principal school trustee, “I ’low our friend the Square is jest the man to boss this ’ere consarn tonight. Ef nobody objects, I’ll app’int him. Come, Square, don’t be bashful. Walk up to the trough, fodder or no fodder, as the man said to his donkey.”

There was a general giggle at this, and many of the young swains took occasion to nudge the girls alongside them, ostensibly for the purpose of making them see the joke, but really for the pure pleasure of nudging. The Greeks figured Cupid as naked, probably because he wears so many disguises that they could not select a costume for him.

===Chinese===
家家都出一支蜡烛。有黄的蘸烛，有白的蘸烛，烧着，冒着烟，爆着火苗。有笑的，有说的，有咯咯傻笑的，有抿嘴假笑的，有挤眉弄眼的，有眉来眼去的，有相好的。拼字学校之于胡波尔县，正如盛装舞会之于第五大道。这样的场合，打个比方，门口挂着一行字：「挑你的舞伴吧。」在胡波尔县，拼字只是个幌子，正如第五大道上的跳舞。可上流社会里也自有为跳舞而跳舞的人，弗拉特溪学区也自有为拼字而拼字的人：他们远远闻到了战阵的气味，赶来这场比试里显一显身手，指望把学生时代赢得的桂叶翻翻新。

「俺估摸着，」米恩斯先生以学区主要学董的身价发言道，「俺估摸着，俺们的朋友治安官，正是今儿晚上主持这个场面的不二人选。要是没人反对，俺就指派他了。来吧，治安官，别害臊。饲料不饲料的，先到槽边来，就像那人对他家驴子说的。」

这话惹起一片咯咯的笑，好些年轻后生趁机动胳膊肘捣身边的姑娘，名义上是叫她们领会这个笑话，实际上纯粹为捣一下胳膊肘的快活。希腊人把丘比特画成光着身子的，多半因为他化装得太勤，他们实在挑不出一件固定衣裳给他。
===Original===
The Squire came to the front. Ralph made an inventory of the agglomeration which bore the name of Squire Hawkins, as follows:

1. A swallowtail coat of indefinite age, worn only on state occasions, when its owner was called to figure in his public capacity. Either the Squire had grown too large or the coat too small.

2. A pair of black gloves, the most phenomenal, abnormal, and unexpected apparition conceivable in Flat Creek district, where the preachers wore no coats in the summer, and where a black glove was never seen except on the hands of the Squire.

3. A wig of that dirty, waxen color so common to wigs. This one showed a continual inclination to slip off the owner’s smooth, bald pate, and the Squire had frequently to adjust it. As his hair had been red, the wig did not accord with his face, and the hair ungrayed was doubly discordant with a countenance shrivelled by age.

4. A semicircular row of whiskers hedging the edge of the jaw and chin. These were dyed a frightful dead-black, such a color as belonged to no natural hair or beard that ever existed. At the roots there was a quarter of an inch of white, giving the whiskers the appearance of having been stuck on.

5. A pair of spectacles “with tortoiseshell rim.” Wont to slip off.

6. A glass eye, purchased of a peddler, and differing in color from its natural mate, perpetually getting out of focus by turning in or out.

7. A set of false teeth, badly fitted, and given to bobbing up and down.

8. The Squire proper, to whom these patches were loosely attached.

===Chinese===
治安官走上前来。拉尔夫给这堆顶着「霍金斯治安官」名号的家什开了一张清单，如下：

1. 一件燕尾服，年代无可考，只有盛典场合才上身——那时节主人家得以公职身份露面。要么是治安官长过了头，要么是衣裳缩过了头。

2. 一副黑手套。这是弗拉特溪学区所能想见的最高奇、最反常、最出人意料的物件——本地的传道人夏天连上衣都不穿，黑手套更是除了治安官手上，别处见不着。

3. 一顶假发，正是假发们惯常的那种发乌的蜡黄色。这一顶还总想从主人那光滑的秃脑瓜顶上出溜，治安官得不时扶一扶。他原先的头发是红的，这假发跟他的脸对不上；没花白的头发衬着一张老出皱纹的脸，就更是双倍地不协调。

4. 半圈连鬓胡子，沿着下颌和下巴的边儿围了一道。染成一种死黑死黑、吓人的颜色，天底下无论谁的天生头发胡子都不长那个色。根上留着四分之一英寸的白茬，弄得这圈胡子活像是后粘上去的。

5. 一副「玳瑁框」眼镜。也爱出溜。

6. 一只玻璃眼睛，从小贩手里买的，颜色跟旁边那只天生的对不上，还老往外斜或往里斗，焦距永远对不准。

7. 一口假牙，安得不合槽，动不动上下磕碰。

8. 治安官本尊。上头那些零碎，都松松地拴在他身上。

===Original===
It is an old story that a boy wrote home to his father begging him to come West, because “mighty mean men get into office out here.” But Ralph concluded that some Yankees had taught school in Hoopole County who would not have held a high place in the educational institutions of Massachusetts. Hawkins had some New England idioms, but they were well overlaid by a Western pronunciation.

“Ladies and gentlemen,” he began, shoving up his spectacles, and sucking his lips over his white teeth to keep them in place, “ladies and gentlemen, young men and maidens, raley I’m obleeged to Mr. Means fer this honor,” and the Squire took both hands and turned the top of his head round half an inch. Then he adjusted his spectacles. Whether he was obliged to Mr. Means for the honor of being compared to a donkey was not clear. “I feel in the inmost compartments of my animal spirits a most happifying sense of the success and futility of all my endeavors to sarve the people of Flat Creek deestrick, and the people of Tomkins township, in my weak way and manner.” This burst of eloquence was delivered with a constrained air and an apparent sense of a danger that he, Squire Hawkins, might fall to pieces in his weak way and manner, and of the success and futility of all attempts at reconstruction. For by this time the ghastly pupil of the left eye, which was black, was looking away round to the left, while the little blue one on the right twinkled cheerfully toward the front. The front teeth would drop down so that the Squire’s mouth was kept nearly closed, and his words whistled through.

“I feel as if I could be grandiloquent on this interesting occasion,” twisting his scalp round, “but raley I must forego any such exertions. It is spelling you want. Spelling is the cornerstone, the grand, underlying subterfuge, of a good eddication. I put the spellin’-book prepared by the great Daniel Webster alongside the Bible. I do, raley. I think I may put it ahead of the Bible. For if it wurn’t fer spellin’-books and sich occasions as these, where would the Bible be? I should like to know. The man who got up, who compounded this work of inextricable valoo was a benufactor to the whole human race or any other.” Here the spectacles fell off. The Squire replaced them in some confusion, gave the top of his head another twist, and felt of his glass eye, while poor Shocky stared in wonder, and Betsey Short rolled from side to side in the effort to suppress her giggle. Mrs. Means and the other old ladies looked the applause they could not speak.

===Chinese===
有个男孩写信回家，求他爹上西部来，因为「这儿顶孬的人也能当上官」——这是老话了。可拉尔夫断定，也有些北方佬在胡波尔县教过书，搁马萨诸塞的教育机构里，他们坐不上高座。霍金斯还留着几句新英格兰的土话，可早让西部的口音盖严实了。

「女士们，先生们，」他开腔了，一面把眼镜往上推，一面咂着嘴唇吸住那口白牙，「女士们，先生们，年轻人们，姑娘们，俺实在受米恩斯先生此番抬举，感激莫名。」说着，治安官抬起双手，把头顶转了半英寸，又扶了扶眼镜。他感激的是米恩斯先生的抬举呢，还是感激被比作一头驴子，这层说不清楚。「俺在俺这动物性体液的最深的隔舱里，感受到一种至为欣幸的意识：俺为弗拉特溪学区的人民、为汤金斯乡的人民效力的一切努力，其成功与徒劳，皆系于俺这点微弱的方式与路数。」这一番雄辩，讲得姿势拘谨，还显然带着一层忧惧：生怕他霍金斯治安官当众「以他自己那点微弱的方式与路数」散了架，也生怕一切复原的尝试其成功与徒劳。因为到这时，他左眼那只黑色的、死气瞳仁已经往左瞧到老远，右眼那只小小的蓝眼珠却欢欢喜喜地直冲前头眨。门牙老往下掉，治安官只好把嘴抿得近乎合上，字是从缝里哨出来的。

「俺觉得，值此有趣的场合，俺本可以雄辩一场，」他拧着头皮说，「可俺实在不得不省了这份力气。你们要的是拼字。拼字是一切良好教育的基石，是宏大而根本的托词。俺把伟大的丹尼尔·韦伯斯特编的拼字课本跟圣经并排放。俺真这么放。俺寻思俺还可以把它排在圣经前头。因为要不是有拼字课本和眼下这样的场合，圣经还往哪儿搁？俺倒要问问。那个编出、配出这部价值不可估量之著作的人，是全人类乃至随便哪一族人的恩人。」说到这儿，眼镜掉下来了。治安官有点儿慌乱地把它们按了回去，又把头顶拧了一把，还摸了摸玻璃眼睛。可怜的肖基看得直发愣，贝琪·肖特为了憋住笑，身子两边直晃。米恩斯太太和其余的老太太们，把说不出口的喝彩都摆在了脸上。

===Original===
“I app’int Larkin Lanham and Jeems Buchanan fer captings,” said the Squire. And the two young men thus named took a stick and tossed it from hand to hand to decide which should have the “first choice.” One tossed the stick to the other, who held it fast just where he happened to catch it. Then the first placed his hand above the second, and so the hands were alternately changed to the top. The one who held the stick last without room for the other to take hold had gained the lot. This was tried three times. As Larkin held the stick twice out of three times, he had the choice. He hesitated a moment. Everybody looked toward tall Jim Phillips. But Larkin was fond of a venture on unknown seas, and so he said, “I take the master,” while a buzz of surprise ran round the room, and the captain of the other side, as if afraid his opponent would withdraw the choice, retorted quickly, and with a little smack of exultation and defiance in his voice, “And *I* take Jeems Phillips.”

And soon all present, except a few of the old folks, found themselves ranged in opposing hosts, the poor spellers lagging in, with what grace they could, at the foot of the two divisions. The Squire opened his spelling-book and began to give out the words to the two captains, who stood up and spelled against each other. It was not long until Larkin spelled “really” with one *l*, and had to sit down in confusion, while a murmur of satisfaction ran through the ranks of the opposing forces. His own side bit their lips. The slender figure of the young teacher took the place of the fallen leader, and the excitement made the house very quiet. Ralph dreaded the loss of prestige he would suffer if he should be easily spelled down. And at the moment of rising he saw in the darkest corner the figure of a well-dressed young man sitting in the shadow. Why should his evil genius haunt him? But by a strong effort he turned his attention away from Dr. Small, and listened carefully to the words which the Squire did not pronounce very distinctly, spelling them with extreme deliberation. This gave him an air of hesitation which disappointed those on his own side. They wanted him to spell with a dashing assurance. But he did not begin a word until he had mentally felt his way through it. After ten minutes of spelling hard words Jeems Buchanan, the captain on the other side, spelled “atrocious” with an *s* instead of a *c*, and subsided, his first choice, Jeems Phillips, coming up against the teacher. This brought the excitement to fever-heat. For though Ralph was chosen first, it was entirely on trust, and most of the company were disappointed. The champion who now stood up against the schoolmaster was a famous speller.

===Chinese===
「俺指派拉金·兰勒姆和吉姆斯·布坎南当队长。」治安官说。两个被点到的年轻人便拿起一根棍子，你扔我接，好定谁享「先挑权」。一个把棍子抛给另一个，那一个就攥住自己恰好抓着的地方。第一个再把手扣在第二个的手上头，两只手就这样轮流往顶上挪。最后一个攥住棍子、叫对方再没处下手的人，便赢了阄。这样试了三回。拉金三回里攥住两回，得了先挑权。他迟疑了片刻。人人都朝高个子吉姆·菲利普斯张望。可拉金偏爱在没海图的海上冒一把险，于是他说：「俺挑先生。」屋里顿时嗡的一声，满场惊讶。对面的队长生怕对手反悔，飞快回了一句，嗓音里带着点儿得胜与叫板的味道：「那*俺*就挑吉姆斯·菲利普斯。」

很快，在场的人，除了几位老人家，都分列成对阵的两军，那些不擅拼字的，各自能有多体面就多体面地缀在两队的队尾。治安官翻开拼字课本，开始给两位队长发词，两人站起来对拼。没过多久，拉金把「really」拼成了单 *l*，只好臊眉耷眼地坐下，对面阵里响起一阵称心的低语。他自己这边的人直咬嘴唇。年轻教师那瘦长的身影接替了落马的队长，激动得满屋子鸦雀无声。拉尔夫生怕自己轻易被拼倒，丢了威信。起立的那一刻，他瞥见最黑的角落里，坐着一个衣冠楚楚的年轻人的身影。他的凶神为什么偏偏缠着他？可他狠了狠心，把注意力从斯莫尔大夫身上挪开，仔细听治安官发词——那些词治安官咬得并不清楚——再一个一个，拼得极慢。这叫他显出一副迟疑的做派，叫他自己这边的人大失所望。他们巴不得他拼得虎虎生风。可他不在心里把一个词从头到尾摸透，绝不开口。拼了十分钟难词之后，对面的队长吉姆斯·布坎南把「atrocious」的 *c* 拼成了 *s*，蔫头蔫脑地坐下了，由他的首选吉姆斯·菲利普斯上来对阵教师。这一下把激动推到了发高烧的地步。拉尔夫虽然是头一个被挑的，可全凭信用，多数人本就失望。如今站起来跟校长对垒的这位冠军，是有名的拼字好手。

===Original===
Jim Phillips was a tall, lank, stoop-shouldered fellow who had never distinguished himself in any other pursuit than spelling. Except in this one art of spelling he was of no account. He could not catch well or bat well in ball. He could not throw well enough to make his mark in that famous Western game of bullpen. He did not succeed well in any study but that of *Webster’s Elementary*. But in that he was—to use the usual Flat Creek locution—in that he was “a boss.” This genius for spelling is in some people a sixth sense, a matter of intuition. Some spellers are born, and not made, and their facility reminds one of the mathematical prodigies that crop out every now and then to bewilder the world. Bud Means, foreseeing that Ralph would be pitted against Jim Phillips, had warned his friend that Jim could “spell like thunder and lightning,” and that it “took a powerful smart speller” to beat him, for he knew “a heap of spelling-book.” To have “spelled down the master” is next thing to having whipped the biggest bully in Hoopole County, and Jim had “spelled down” the last three masters. He divided the hero-worship of the district with Bud Means.

For half an hour the Squire gave out hard words. What a blessed thing our crooked orthography is! Without it there could be no spelling-schools. As Ralph discovered his opponent’s mettle he became more and more cautious. He was now satisfied that Jim would eventually beat him. The fellow evidently knew more about the spelling-book than old Noah Webster himself. As he stood there, with his dull face and long sharp nose, his hands behind his back, and his voice spelling infallibly, it seemed to Hartsook that his superiority must lie in his nose. Ralph’s cautiousness answered a double purpose; it enabled him to tread surely, and it was mistaken by Jim for weakness. Phillips was now confident that he should carry off the scalp of the fourth schoolmaster before the evening was over. He spelled eagerly, confidently, brilliantly. Stoop-shouldered as he was, he began to straighten up. In the minds of all the company the odds were in his favor. He saw this, and became ambitious to distinguish himself by spelling without giving the matter any thought.

Ralph always believed that he would have been speedily defeated by Phillips had it not been for two thoughts which braced him. The sinister shadow of young Dr. Small sitting in the dark corner by the water-bucket nerved him. A victory over Phillips was a defeat to one who wished only ill to the young schoolmaster. The other thought that kept his pluck alive was the recollection of Bull. He approached a word as Bull approached the raccoon. He did not take hold until he was sure of his game. When he took hold, it was with a quiet assurance of success. As Ralph spelled in this dogged way for half an hour the hardest words the Squire could find, the excitement steadily rose in all parts of the house, and Ralph’s friends even ventured to whisper that “maybe Jim had cotched his match, after all!”

===Chinese===
吉姆·菲利普斯是个高瘦的、驼肩膀的家伙，除了拼字，干什么都没露过脸。单在拼字这一门上，他是个角色，别的门里一概不算数。打球，他接不好也击不好；扔掷差得远，够不上西部那门出名的「牛栏」游戏里露一手；什么功课都不成，独独《韦氏初级拼字课本》例外。而在这门上，他——照弗拉特溪的惯用说法——是「一把好手」。这种拼字的天才，在有些人身上是第六感，是直觉的事。有些拼字家是天生的，不是练出来的，他们的娴熟叫人想起那些隔三差五冒出来迷惑世界的算术神童。巴德·米恩斯早料到拉尔夫早晚要对上吉姆·菲利普斯，提醒过他的朋友：吉姆「拼字拼得跟打雷打闪似的」，要赢他「得一个顶顶聪明的拼字家」才行，因为他肚里「装着老大一摊拼字课本」。 「把先生拼倒」，在胡波尔县是仅次于揍趴全县头号恶霸的一桩功名，而吉姆已经把前三任先生全「拼倒」了。全学区的英雄崇拜，由他和巴德·米恩斯二人平分。

治安官发了半个钟头的难词。我们这门七扭八歪的拼写法，真是天大的恩物！没有它，就不会有拼字学校。拉尔夫摸清了对手的斤两，越拼越谨慎。他如今拿定了：吉姆终究要赢他。这家伙对拼字课本的熟络，分明赛过老诺亚·韦伯斯特本人。他站在那儿，一张呆脸，一只又长又尖的鼻子，双手背在身后，嗓音不出错地一个字母一个字母往外拼——哈特索克觉得，他的优势八成就长在那只鼻子上。拉尔夫的谨慎一箭双雕：既叫他步步踩得稳，又叫吉姆错当他怯阵。菲利普斯这会儿笃定了：不等天黑，第四任校长的头皮就要让他揭走。他拼得起劲、笃定、漂亮。驼着的肩膀，也渐渐直起来了。满场人心目中，赌注都压在他这边。他瞧出来了，便起了雄心，要露一手绝的：不假思索地拼。

拉尔夫后来一直相信，若不是有两桩念头撑着，他早叫菲利普斯利利索索拼倒了。年轻的斯莫尔大夫坐在水桶旁黑角落里那道阴险的影子，给他打了气：赢菲利普斯一场，就是对那个只盼年轻校长倒霉的人的一场败仗。另一桩撑住他胆气的念头，是想起「公牛」。他凑近一个词，就像公牛凑近那只浣熊：不摸准猎物，绝不下口；一下口，便带着一种不动声色的稳操胜券。拉尔夫就这样跟头倔脑地拼了半个钟头，治安官翻箱倒柜找出来的难词全让他接住了。满屋的激动稳稳地往上涨，拉尔夫这边的哥们儿都敢咬耳朵了：「兴许吉姆这回总算碰上对手了！」

===Original===
But Phillips never doubted of his success.

“Theodolite,” said the Squire.

“*T*-*h*-*e*, the *o*-*d*, od, theod, *o*, theodo, *l*-*y*-*t*-*e*, theodolite,” spelled the champion.

“Next,” said the Squire, nearly losing his teeth in his excitement. Ralph spelled the word slowly and correctly, and the conquered champion sat down in confusion. The excitement was so great for some minutes that the spelling was suspended. Everybody in the house had shown sympathy with one or the other of the combatants, except the silent shadow in the corner. It had not moved during the contest, and did not show any interest now in the result.

===Chinese===
可菲利普斯对自己获胜这件事，从没有过一丝怀疑。

「Theodolite（经纬仪），」治安官说。

「*T*-*h*-*e*，the，*o*-*d*，od，theod，*o*，theodo，*l*-*y*-*t*-*e*，theodolite。」冠军拼道。

「下一个。」治安官说，激动得差点把假牙丢了。拉尔夫把这个词慢慢地、一字不差地拼了出来，落败的冠军臊眉耷眼地坐下了。有好几分钟，满屋的激动大得拼字都停了摆。屋里人人都对交手的这一位或那一位露过同情，唯独角落里那道不吭声的影子例外。比赛从头到尾它没挪动过，如今对结果也不见一点兴致。

===Original===
“Gewhilliky crickets! Thunder and lightning! Licked him all to smash!” said Bud, rubbing his hands on his knees, “That beats my time all holler!”

And Betsey Short giggled until her tuck-comb fell out, though she was on the defeated side.

Shocky got up and danced with pleasure.

But one suffocating look from the aqueous eyes of Mirandy destroyed the last spark of Ralph’s pleasure in his triumph, and sent that awful below-zero feeling all through him.

“He’s powerful smart, is the master,” said old Jack to Mr. Pete Jones. “He’ll beat the whole kit and tuck of ’em afore he’s through. I know’d he was smart. That’s the reason I tuck him,” proceeded Mr. Means.

“Yaas, but he don’t lick enough. Not nigh,” answered Pete Jones. “No lickin’, no larnin’, says I.”

===Chinese===
「乖乖的蟋蟀！打雷又打闪！把他揍了个稀巴烂！」巴德一面说，一面把手在膝盖上搓来搓去，「这可比俺当年强出一大截子！」

贝琪·肖特咯咯地笑，笑得头上那把插梳都掉了下来，虽说她站在打败了的那一边。

肖基乐得站起来直蹦跶。

可米兰迪那双水汪汪的眼睛送来叫他透不过气的一瞥，把拉尔夫胜利里最后一星欢喜也给扑灭了，还叫那种零下几十度的可怕滋味流遍他全身。

「这位先生，灵光得吓人。」老杰克对皮特·琼斯先生说，「不等他收场，他能把他们一整窝连锅端了。俺早晓得他灵光。所以才挑了他。」米恩斯先生接着说。

「是呐，可他揍得不勤。差得远呢，」皮特·琼斯答道，「俺说嘛，不揍不长学问。」

===Original===
It was now not so hard. The other spellers on the opposite side went down quickly under the hard words which the Squire gave out. The master had mowed down all but a few, his opponents had given up the battle, and all had lost their keen interest in a contest to which there could be but one conclusion, for there were only the poor spellers left. But Ralph Hartsook ran against a stump where he was least expecting it. It was the Squire’s custom, when one of the smaller scholars or poorer spellers rose to spell against the master, to give out eight or ten easy words, that they might have some breathing-spell before being slaughtered, and then to give a poser or two which soon settled them. He let them run a little, as a cat does a doomed mouse. There was now but one person left on the opposite side, and, as she rose in her blue calico dress, Ralph recognized Hannah, the bound girl at old Jack Means’s. She had not attended school in the district, and had never spelled in spelling-school before, and was chosen last as an uncertain quantity. The Squire began with easy words of two syllables, from that page of Webster, so well known to all who ever thumbed it, as “baker,” from the word that stands at the top of the page. She spelled these words in an absent and uninterested manner. As everybody knew that she would have to go down as soon as this preliminary skirmishing was over, everybody began to get ready to go home, and already there was the buzz of preparation. Young men were timidly asking girls if “they could see them safe home,” which was the approved formula, and were trembling in mortal fear of “the mitten.” Presently the Squire, thinking it time to close the contest, pulled his scalp forward, adjusted his glass eye, which had been examining his nose long enough, and turned over the leaves of the book to the great words at the place known to spellers as “incomprehensibility,” and began to give out those “words of eight syllables with the accent on the sixth.” Listless scholars now turned round, and ceased to whisper, in order to be in at the master’s final triumph. But to their surprise “ole Miss Meanses’ white nigger,” as some of them called her in allusion to her slavish life, spelled these great words with as perfect ease as the master. Still not doubting the result, the Squire turned from place to place and selected all the hard words he could find. The school became utterly quiet, the excitement was too great for the ordinary buzz. Would “Meanses’ Hanner” beat the master? beat the master that had laid out Jim Phillips? Everybody’s sympathy was now turned to Hannah. Ralph noticed that even Shocky had deserted him, and that his face grew brilliant every time Hannah spelled a word. In fact, Ralph deserted himself. As he saw the fine, timid face of the girl so long oppressed flush and shine with interest; as he looked at the rather low but broad and intelligent brow and the fresh, white complexion and saw the rich, womanly nature coming to the surface under the influence of applause and sympathy—he did not want to beat. If he had not felt that a victory given would insult her, he would have missed intentionally. The bulldog, the stern, relentless setting of the will, had gone, he knew not whither. And there had come in its place, as he looked in that face, a something which he did not understand. You did not, gentle reader, the first time it came to you.

===Chinese===
眼下这场仗不难打了。对面剩下的拼字手，在治安官发出来的难词底下一个接一个很快倒下。先生把人差不多刈了个净光，对手们早已撂下不拼了，大家对这场只剩一个下场可看的比赛也淡了兴致，因为留下的净是不济的拼字手。可拉尔夫·哈茨克偏偏在他最没防备的地方撞上了一截树桩。治安官有个规矩：每逢个头小的学生或不济的拼字手起来跟先生对拼，他先发八个十个容易词，叫他们在挨宰之前喘口气，然后再发一两个刁钻词，很快打发他们下场。他先放他们跑一小会儿，就像猫放那只注定活不成的耗子。这时对面只剩下一个人了。她穿着蓝印花布裙子站起来，拉尔夫认出是汉娜，老杰克·米恩斯家的抵债姑娘。她没在学区里上过学，从前也没在拼字学校拼过字，末一个才被挑走，算是个未知数。治安官从韦氏拼字课本那一页上发些双音节的容易词——那一页凡捻过这书的人没有不熟的，按页首顶上立着的那个词，人人都管它叫「baker（面包师）」页。这些词她拼得心不在焉、意兴索然。人人都知道，这场前哨战一完，她就得倒下，于是人人张罗起回家来，收拾动身的嗡嗡声已经起来了。年轻后生怯生生地问姑娘「能不能送你平安到家」——这是公认的老套程式——一面又抖着心跳，生怕吃到那记「手套」——遭姑娘回绝的代称。不多时，治安官觉得该收场了，把头皮往前拽了拽，把那只对着他的鼻子端详了半晌的玻璃眼睛扶正，把书页翻到拼字家们称作「incomprehensibility（不可理解性）」的那一处大词，开始发那些「重音落在第六音节上的八字节的词」。方才没精打采的学生们如今都转过身来，停了咬耳朵，好赶上瞧先生的最后凯旋。可叫他们吃惊的是，「老米恩斯家的白黑鬼」——有人这么称呼她，暗指她那奴隶般的日子——拼起这些大词来，同先生一样从容妥帖。治安官依旧不疑心这个结果，把书东翻西翻，寻得着的难词全挑了出来。学校里彻底静了下来，这激动太大，寻常的嗡嗡声都盛不下了。「米恩斯家的汉娜」会把先生拼倒么？把那位撂倒了吉姆·菲利普斯的先生拼倒么？人人的同情如今都倒向了汉娜。拉尔夫注意到，连肖基也把他撇下了；汉娜每拼上一个词，肖基的脸就放一回光。说实在的，拉尔夫把自己也撇下了。他望着这个久受欺压的姑娘那张秀气、怯生生的脸，因了兴头而泛红发亮；望着那副不算高、却宽阔而有灵气的额头和水嫩白净的肤色，看见她那丰厚的女人天性在喝彩与同情里浮上了面——他不想赢了。倘若不是觉得把胜利拱手相让会羞辱了她，他情愿故意拼错。那头「公牛」，那股子严峻、不依不饶的狠劲，不知跑到哪儿去了。他望着那张脸，心里换上来的那个东西，他可闹不明白。温和的读者，它头一回落到您身上的时候，您也一样闹不明白。

===Original===
The Squire was puzzled. He had given out all the hard words in the book. He again pulled the top of his head forward. Then he wiped his spectacles and put them on. Then out of the depths of his pocket he fished up a list of words just coming into use in those days—words not in the spelling-book. He regarded the paper attentively with his blue right eye. His black left eye meanwhile fixed itself in such a stare on Mirandy Means that she shuddered and hid her eyes in her red silk handkerchief.

“Daguerreotype,” sniffed the Squire. It was Ralph’s turn.

“*D*-*a*-*u*, dau—”

“Next.”

And Hannah spelled it right.

===Chinese===
治安官犯难了。书里的难词他都发完了。他又把头顶往前拽了拽，接着擦了擦眼镜戴上。然后从口袋深处钓上来一张词单，都是那些年头刚时兴起来的词——拼字课本里没有的。他用右眼那只蓝眼珠把那张纸端详了个仔细；左眼那只黑眼珠却同时死死盯住了米兰迪·米恩斯，盯得她直打哆嗦，把眼睛埋进了红绸手绢里。

「Daguerreotype（达盖尔银版照相），」治安官嗤着鼻子说。轮到拉尔夫了。

「*D*-*a*-*u*，dau——」

「下一个。」

汉娜却把它拼对了。

===Original===
Such a buzz followed that Betsey Short’s giggle could not be heard, but Shocky shouted: “Hanner beat! my Hanner spelled down the master!” And Ralph went over and congratulated her.

And Dr. Small sat perfectly still in the corner.

And then the Squire called them to order, and said: “As our friend Hanner Thomson is the only one left on her side, she will have to spell against nearly all on t’other side. I shall therefore take the liberty of procrastinating the completion of this interesting and exacting contest until tomorrow evening. I hope our friend Hanner may again carry off the cypress crown of glory. There is nothing better for us than healthful and kindly simulation.”

Dr. Small, who knew the road to practice, escorted Mirandy, and Bud went home with somebody else. The others of the Means family hurried on, while Hannah, the champion, stayed behind a minute to speak to Shocky. Perhaps it was because Ralph saw that Hannah must go alone that he suddenly remembered having left something which was of no consequence, and resolved to go round by Mr. Means’s and get it.

===Chinese===
接着满屋嗡的一声，把贝琪·肖特的咯咯笑都淹了，只听见肖基喊：「汉娜赢了！俺的汉娜把先生拼倒了！」拉尔夫便走过去向她道贺。

斯莫尔大夫呢，在角落里坐得纹丝不动。

随后治安官叫大家守秩序，说：「既然俺们的朋友汉娜·汤姆森是她那一边剩下的独一个，她就得跟对面差不多所有人挨个儿拼。因此俺要自作主张，把这场有趣又费神的比赛的完成，往明儿晚上推延一番。俺盼着俺们的朋友汉娜能再度摘取那柏树的荣冠。再没有什么比有益而亲切的模拟对俺们更好的了。」

斯莫尔大夫深谙通往行医生意的门路，护送着米兰迪走了；巴德同别的什么人一道回家。米恩斯家其余的人匆匆赶路，冠军汉娜却留下了一小会儿，跟肖基说话。兴许正因为拉尔夫看见汉娜只好一个人走，他才忽然想起自己落下了一件顶不关紧要的东西，打定主意绕道米恩斯先生家去取它。

## XVI The Church Militant / 十六 战斗的教会

===Original===
Bud was doubly enlisted on the side of John Pearson, the basket-maker. In the first place, he knew that this persecution of the unpopular old man was only a blind to save somebody else; that they were thieves who cried, “Stop thief!” And he felt consequently that this was a chance to put his newly-formed resolutions into practice. The Old Testament religious life, which consists in fighting the Lord’s enemies, suited Bud’s temper and education. It might lead to something better. It was the best possible to him, now. But I am afraid I shall have to acknowledge that there was a second motive that moved Bud to this championship. The good heart of Martha Hawkins having espoused the cause of the basket-maker, the heart of Bud Means could not help feeling warmly on the same side. Blessed is that man in whose life the driving of duty and the drawing of love impel the same way! But why speak of the driving of duty? For already Bud was learning the better lesson of serving God for the love of God.

The old basket-maker was the most unpopular man in Flat Creek district. He had two great vices. He would go to Clifty and have a “spree” once in three months. And he would tell the truth in a most unscrupulous manner. A man given to plain speaking was quite as objectionable in Flat Creek as he would have been in France under the Empire, the Commune, or the Republic, and almost as objectionable as he would be in any refined community in America. People who live in glass houses have a horror of people who throw stones. And the old basket-maker, having no friends, was a good scapegoat. In driving him off, Pete Jones would get rid of a dangerous neighbor and divert attention from himself. The immediate crime of the basket-maker was that he had happened to see too much.

“Mr. Hartsook,” said Bud, when they got out into the road, “you’d better go straight home to the Squire’s. Bekase ef this lightnin’ strikes a second time it’ll strike awful closte to you. You hadn’t better be seen with us. Which way did you come, Shocky?”

===Chinese===
巴德加倍地站到了编篮匠约翰·皮尔逊（John Pearson）一边。首先，他知道这场对这位不得人心的老头儿的迫害，不过是掩人耳目、替旁人顶罪的把戏；嚷嚷「抓贼」的正是一伙贼。于是他觉得，这正是把新下的决心付诸实践的机会。那种以打击耶和华的仇敌为内容的《旧约》式宗教生活，正合巴德的脾性和教养。这条路日后或许能引他到更好的地步；眼下对他来说，已是再好不过。不过恐怕我得承认，促使巴德出头打这个抱不平的，还有第二个动机。玛莎·霍金斯小姐那颗好心既然站到了编篮匠一边，巴德·米恩斯的那颗心也就不由得往同一边热乎起来。一个人的一生里，责任的鞭策与爱情的牵引若是指向同一个方向，这人便有福了！可何必还说什么责任的鞭策呢？巴德已经在学更好的一课——为爱上帝的缘故事奉上帝了。

这位老编篮匠是弗拉特溪学区最不得人心的人。他有两大毛病。一是每隔三个月，总要到克利夫蒂去「放纵一回」。二是他讲真话讲得毫无顾忌。一个惯说直话的人，在弗拉特溪招人嫌的程度，不亚于在帝国、公社或共和国治下的法国，也几乎不亚于在美国任何文雅社区。住在玻璃房子里的人，最怕的就是扔石头的人。而老编篮匠无亲无故，正是个好替罪羊。把他赶走，皮特·琼斯既除掉了一个危险的邻居，又能把众人的注意从自己身上引开。编篮匠眼下的罪名是：他偏偏看见了太多不该看见的事。

「哈特索克先生，」上了大路，巴德说，「你顶好径直回治安官家去。因为要是这道闪电再劈第二回，可就要劈到你跟前了。你可别让人瞧见跟俺们在一块儿。你是打哪边来的，肖基？」

===Original===
“Why, I tried to come down the holler, but I met Jones right by the big road, and he sweared at me and said he’d kill me ef I didn’t go back and stay. And so I went back to the house and then slipped out through the graveyard. You see I was bound to come ef I got skinned. For Mr. Pearson’s, stuck to me and I mean to stick to him, you see.”

Bud led Shocky through the graveyard. But when they reached the forest path from the graveyard he thought that perhaps it was not best to “show his hand,” as he expressed it, too soon.

“Now, Shocky,” he said, “do you run ahead and tell the ole man that I want to see him right off down by the Spring-in-rock. I’ll keep closte behind you, and ef anybody offers to trouble you, do you let off a yell and I’ll be thar in no time.”

===Chinese===
「唉，我本想顺着沟下来，可刚到大路跟前就撞见了琼斯，他骂我，还说我若不回去老老实实待着，就宰了我。我就回了屋，然后再偷偷从墓地那边溜了出来。你瞧，我就是挨顿揍，也非来不可。因为皮尔逊先生护着我，我也要护着他，就是这么回事。」

巴德领着肖基穿过墓地。可走到从墓地伸出去的林间小径时，他寻思着，照他自己的说法，还是别过早「亮出底牌」为好。

「听我说，肖基，」他说，「你先跑到前头去，告诉那老头儿，俺要他马上到岩中泉（Spring-in-rock）底下见俺。俺就跟在你后头不远。要是有人想欺负你，你就放开嗓子嚎一嗓子，俺立马就到。」

===Original===
When Ralph left the schoolhouse he felt mean. There were Bud and Shocky gone on an errand of mercy, and he, the truant member of the Church of the Best Licks, was not with them. The more he thought of it the more he seemed to be a coward, and the more he despised himself; so, yielding as usual to the first brave impulse, he leaped nimbly over the fence and started briskly through the forest in a direction intersecting the path on which were Bud and Shocky. He came in sight just in time to see the first conflict of the Church in the Wilderness with her foes.

For Shocky’s little feet went more swiftly on their eager errand than Bud had anticipated. He got farther out of Bud’s reach than the latter intended he should, and he did not discover Pete Jones until Pete, with his hog-drover’s whip, was right upon him.

Shocky tried to halloo for Bud, but he was like one in a nightmare. The yell died into a whisper which could not have been heard ten feet.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫离开校舍时，心里很不是滋味。巴德和肖基行善事去了，而他这个「最卖力气的教会」的逃兵，却没跟他们在一起。他越想越觉得自己像个懦夫，越想越看不起自己；于是，一如既往地屈服于第一个勇敢的冲动，他轻捷地翻过栅栏，脚步轻快地穿过树林，朝一条能与巴德和肖基所走小径交汇的方向插了过去。他赶到望得见的地方，恰好来得及看见旷野中的教会与仇敌的第一次交锋。

原来肖基那双小脚，在这桩急切的差事上走得比巴德预想的快。他跑出了巴德本不想让他跑出那么远的地方，等发现皮特·琼斯时，皮特已提着赶猪的鞭子扑到了他跟前。

肖基想喊巴德，可他就像身陷噩梦的人。喊声到了嘴边，化成一句十英尺开外也听不见的耳语。

===Original===
I shall not repeat Mr. Jones’s words. They were frightfully profane. But he did not stop at words. He swept his whip round and gave little Shocky one terrible cut. Then the voice was released, and the piercing cry of pain brought Bud down the path flying.

“You good-for-nothing scoundrel,” growled Bud, “you’re a coward and a thief to be a-beatin’ a little creetur like him!” and with that Bud walked up on Jones, who prudently changed position in such a way as to get the upper side of the hill.

“Well, I’ll gin you the upper side, but come on,” cried Bud, “ef you a’n’t afeared to fight somebody besides a poor little sickly baby or a crippled soldier. Come on!”

===Chinese===
琼斯先生的话，我不在此复述了。那些话亵渎得吓人。可他并不止于动嘴。他抡圆了鞭子，狠狠抽了小肖基一记。这一下，嗓子可算解了禁，那声刺耳的痛呼引得巴德顺着小径飞奔而来。

「你这个不中用的恶棍，」巴德低吼道，「打这么一个小可怜儿，你既是懦夫，又是贼！」说着，巴德便朝琼斯逼近；琼斯倒乖觉，挪了挪身子，抢占了山坡的上方。

「哼，上方就让你占着，放马过来，」巴德喊道，「你要不是只敢打病歪歪的小娃娃和缺胳膊少腿的大兵，就过来呀！过来呀！」

===Original===
Pete was no insignificant antagonist. He had been a great fighter, and his well-seasoned arms were like iron. He had not the splendid set of Bud, but he had more skill and experience in the rude tournament of fists to which the backwoods is so much given. Now, being out of sight of witnesses and sure that he could lie about the fight afterward, he did not scruple to take advantages which would have disgraced him forever if he had taken them in a public fight on election day or at a muster. He took the uphill side, and he clubbed his whip-stalk, striking Bud with all his force with the heavy end, which, coward-like, he had loaded with lead. Bud threw up his strong left arm and parried the blow, which, however, was so fierce that it fractured one of the bones of the arm. Throwing away his whip Pete rushed upon Bud furiously, intending to overpower him, but Bud slipped quickly to one side and let Jones pass down the hill, and as Jones came up again Means dealt him one crushing blow that sent him full length upon the ground. Nothing but the leaves saved him from a most terrible fall. Jones sprang to his feet more angry than ever at being whipped by one whom he regarded as a boy, and drew a long dirk-knife. But he was blind with rage, and Bud dodged the knife, and this time gave Pete a blow on the nose which marred the homeliness of that feature and doubled the fellow up against a tree ten feet away.

===Chinese===
皮特可不是个不值一提的对手。他当年是打架的好手，两条久经锻炼的胳膊跟铁打的一样。他没有巴德那副漂亮的体格，但在边地盛行的粗野拳斗上，他更有技巧、更富经验。此刻四下无人见证，又笃定事后尽可以撒谎遮掩，他便毫不迟疑地使出了那些阴招——这些招数若是在选举日的公开打斗或民兵操演上使出来，会叫他一辈子抬不起头。他占了坡上，把鞭杆倒转当棍抡，用灌了铅的重头，拼足力气朝巴德砸去。巴德扬起有力的左臂一格，可那一击太猛，还是震断了他臂上的一根骨头。皮特扔了鞭子，狂怒地扑向巴德，想凭力气压倒他；巴德却敏捷地闪到一边，让琼斯从坡上冲了下去；等琼斯再爬上来，米恩斯给了他一记毁灭性的重拳，把他整个儿放翻在地。若不是一层落叶垫着，他这一跤摔得可就惨了。琼斯一跃而起，被一个自己看作毛孩子的人揍了，愈发怒不可遏，抽出一把长匕首。可他怒火攻心，眼前发花，巴德躲过刀锋，这一回照着皮特的鼻子结结实实来了一拳，把那张脸上本就不多的俊俏毁了个干净，那家伙登时折在十英尺外的一棵树上。

===Original===
Ralph came in sight in time to see the beginning of the fight, and he arrived on the ground just as Pete Jones went down under the well-dealt blow from the only remaining fist of Bud Means.

While Ralph examined Bud’s disabled left arm Pete picked himself up slowly, and, muttering that he felt “consid’able shuck up like,” crawled away like a whipped puppy. To everyone whom he met, Pete, whose intellect seemed to have weakened in sympathy with his frame, remarked feebly that he was consid’able shuck up like, and vouchsafed no other explanation. Even to his wife he only said that he felt purty consid’able shuck up like, and that the boys would have to get on tonight without him. There are some scoundrels whose very malignity is shaken out of them for the time being by a thorough drubbing.

“I’m afraid you’re going to have trouble with your arm, Bud,” said Ralph tenderly.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫赶到望得见的地方，正好看见这场恶斗的开头；待他跑到现场，皮特·琼斯也恰好倒在了巴德·米恩斯仅剩的那只拳头结结实实的一击之下。

拉尔夫察看巴德那条动不得的左臂时，皮特慢慢爬起来，咕哝着说自己「震得够呛」，像条挨了揍的小狗似的爬走了。此后每碰见一个人，智力似乎也跟着身子一块儿垮了的皮特，都有气无力地说一句自己震得够呛，别的解释一概欠奉。连对他自己的婆娘，他也只说自己觉得震得相当够呛，还说小伙子们今晚只好没他帮衬、自己对付着过了。有些恶棍，一顿结结实实的臭揍，能把他们肚子里的歹毒暂时都给打出来。

「恐怕你这条胳膊要遭罪了，巴德。」拉尔夫关切地说。

===Original===
“Never mind; I put in my best licks fer *Him* that air time, Mr. Hartsook.” Ralph shivered a little at thought of this, but if it was right to knock Jones down at all, why might not Bud do it “heartily as unto the Lord?”

Gideon did not feel any more honest pleasure in chastising the Midianites than did Bud in sending Pete Jones away purty consid’able shuck up like.

===Chinese===
「不碍事；俺那一阵子，可是替*祂*出了俺最卖力气的劲儿，哈特索克先生。」拉尔夫想到这话，微微打了个寒噤；不过，既然把琼斯打倒本来就是应该的，巴德「甘心乐意，像是给主做的」去干，又何尝不可呢？

基甸惩治米甸人时感到的那种问心无愧的快意，比起巴德送走那个震得相当够呛的皮特·琼斯时感到的，也高不到哪里去。

## XV The Church of the Best Licks / 十五 最卖力气的教会

===Original===
Just as the flame on the forestick, which Ralph had watched so intensely, flickered and burned low, and just as Ralph with a heavy but not quite hopeless heart rose to leave, the latch lifted and Bud reentered.

“I wanted to say something,” he stammered, “but you know it’s hard to say it. I ha’n’t no book-larnin to speak of, and some things is hard to say when a man ha’n’t got book-words to say ’em with. And they’s some things a man can’t hardly ever say anyhow to anybody.”

Here Bud stopped. But Ralph spoke in such a matter-of-course way in reply that he felt encouraged to go on.

===Chinese===
正当拉尔夫凝神望着的那根炉口柴的火苗摇曳着、渐渐烧低，正当他怀着沉重却还不算全然绝望的心起身要走时，门闩一抬，巴德又进来了。

「俺有句话想说，」他结结巴巴地说，「可你知道，这话难说出口。俺肚里没什么书本学问好提的，一个人没攒下书本上的词儿，有些事就是说不出口。再说，有些事一个人不管咋样，都几乎没法对任何人说。」

巴德说到这儿停住了。可拉尔夫答话的口吻那么自然而然，让他受了鼓舞，又接着说了下去。

===Original===
“You gin up Hanner kase you thought she belonged to me. That’s more’n I’d a done by a long shot. Now, arter I left here jest now, I says to myself, a man what can gin up his gal on account of sech a feeling fer the rights of a Flat Cricker like me, why, dog-on it, says I, sech a man is the man as can help me do better. I don’t know whether you’re a Hardshell or a Saftshell, or a Methodist, or a Campbellite, or a New Light, or a United Brother, or a Millerite, or whatnot. But I says, the man what can do the clean thing by a ugly feller like me, and stick to it, when I was jest ready to eat him up, is a kind of a man to tie to.”

Here Bud stopped in fright at his own volubility, for he had run his words off like a piece learned by heart, as though afraid that if he stopped he would not have courage to go on.

Ralph said that he did not belong to any church, and he was afraid he couldn’t do Bud much good. But his tone was full of sympathy, and, what is better than sympathy, a yearning for sympathy.

===Chinese===
「你把汉娜让了出来，是因为你寻思她是俺的人。这可比俺能做出来的强出老鼻子了。刚才俺打这儿走了以后，俺跟自己说：一个人能凭着对俺这么个弗拉特溪佬的名分的那点儿心意，就把自己的姑娘让出来——嘿，见鬼，俺说，这样的人才是能帮俺学好的人。俺不知道你是硬壳派（Hardshell）还是软壳派，是卫理公会的，还是坎伯尔派的、新光派的、联合弟兄会的、米勒派的，还是别的什么派。可俺说，一个人能对俺这么个丑家伙干出这么地道漂亮的事，还能咬定了不松口——那会儿俺正恨不得一口吞了他——这样的人，就是可以死心塌地拴上去跟定的人。」

说到这里，巴德被自己的滔滔不绝吓了一跳，停了下来。原来他这一番话是像背书似的冲口倾泻的，仿佛生怕一停下来，便再没有勇气说下去。

拉尔夫说，他自己不属于任何教会，而且怕帮不上巴德什么忙。可他语气里满是同情，而且——比同情更好的是——满是对同情的渴求。

===Original===
“You see,” said Bud, “I wanted to git out of this low-lived, Flat Crick way of livin’. We’re a hard set down here, Mr. Hartsook. And I’m gittin’ to be one of the hardest of ’em. But I never could git no good out of Bosaw with his whisky and meanness. And I went to the Mount Tabor church concert. I heard a man discussin’ baptism, and regeneration, and so on. That didn’t seem no cure for me, I went to a revival over at Clifty. Well, ’twarn’t no use. First night they was a man that spoke about Jesus Christ in sech a way that I wanted to foller him everywhere. But I didn’t feel fit. Next night I come back with my mind made up that I’d try Jesus Christ, and see ef he’d have me. But laws! they was a big man that night that preached hell. Not that I don’t believe they’s a hell. They’s plenty not a thousand miles away as deserves it, and I don’t know as I’m too good for it myself. But he pitched it at us, and stuck it in our faces in sech a way that I got mad. And I says, Well, ef God sends me to hell he can’t make me holler ’nough nohow. You see my dander was up. And when my dander’s up, I wouldn’t gin up fer the devil his-self. The preacher was so insultin’ with his way of doin’ it. He seemed to be kind of glad that we was to be damned, and he preached somethin’ like some folks swears. It didn’t sound a bit like the Christ the little man preached about the night afore. So what does me and a lot of fellers do but slip out and cut off the big preacher’s stirrups, and hang ’em on to the rider of the fence, and then set his hoss loose! And from that day, sometimes I did, and sometimes I didn’t, want to be better. And today it seemed to me that you must know somethin’ as would help me.”

===Chinese===
「你瞧，」巴德说，「俺想跳出这种下三滥的弗拉特溪活法。俺们这一带的人都是些硬茬儿，哈特索克先生，而俺眼看要变成硬茬儿里最硬的一个。可俺从博索和他的威士忌跟那下三滥的做派里，从来榨不出半点儿好处。俺去听过他泊山教堂的礼拜。俺听一个人讨论洗礼呀、重生呀这一套。那玩意儿治不了俺的病。俺又到克利夫蒂那边赶了一场奋兴布道会。嘿，也没用。头天晚上有个人讲耶稣基督，讲得俺恨不得跟着他走遍天涯。可俺觉得自己不配。第二天晚上俺又去了，拿定了主意要试一试耶稣基督，看祂肯不肯要俺。可是老天爷！那晚来了个大人物，讲地狱。倒不是俺不信有地狱。离俺不出一千英里远的地方，有的是该下地狱的，俺也不知道俺自己是不是就金贵得下不去。可他把那话朝俺们劈头砸过来，往俺们脸上戳，戳得俺火冒三丈。俺就说：哼，上帝要是送俺下地狱，他也休想叫俺喊一声饶。你瞧，俺的火气上来了。俺火一上来，就是魔鬼本尊来了俺也不让。那传道人干这事的手法太气人。他好像巴不得俺们下地狱似的，讲起道来跟有些人骂街一个样。听着一点儿也不像头天晚上那小个子讲的基督。于是你猜俺跟一帮伙计干了啥？俺们溜了出去，把那大个子传道人的马镫割了下来，挂到栅栏顶上那根横杆上，再把他的马撒了缰！打那天起，俺有时想学好，有时又不想了。今天俺寻思，你肚里准是有点儿什么，能帮得了俺。」

===Original===
Nothing is worse than a religious experience kept ready to be exposed to the gaze of everybody, whether the time is appropriate or not. But never was a religious experience more appropriate than the account which Ralph gave to Bud of his Struggle in the Dark. The confession of his weakness and wicked selfishness was a great comfort to Bud.

“Do you think that Jesus Christ would﻿—would﻿—well, do you think he’d help a poor, unlarnt Flat Cricker like me?”

“I think he was a sort of a Flat Creeker himself,” said Ralph, slowly and very earnestly.

“You don’t say?” said Bud, almost getting off his seat.

===Chinese===
再没有比一种不论时机合不合适、都随时准备摊开来给人看的宗教经验更糟糕的了。可也从来没有比这一次更合时宜的宗教经验——拉尔夫向巴德讲述了自己在黑暗中的挣扎。他对自己软弱和邪恶自私的坦白承认，对巴德是极大的安慰。

「你看耶稣基督会不会——会不会——呃，你看祂肯不肯帮俺这么个可怜的、没学问的弗拉特溪佬？」

「依我看，他自己就有点儿像个弗拉特溪人。」拉尔夫说得很慢，极其恳切。

「不会吧？」巴德说，差一点儿从座位上出溜下来。

===Original===
“Why, you see the town he lived in was a rough place. It was called Nazareth, which meant ‘Bush-town.’”

“You don’t say?”

“And he was called a Nazarene, which was about the same as ‘backwoodsman.’”

And Ralph read the different passages which he had studied at Sunday-school, illustrating the condescension of Jesus, the stories of the publicans, the harlots, the poor, who came to him. And he read about Nathanael, who lived only six miles away, saying, “‘Can any good thing come out of Nazareth?’”

“Jus’ what Clifty folks says about Flat Crick,” broke in Bud.

===Chinese===
「你看，他住的那座镇子本就是个粗地方，叫拿撒勒，意思就是『灌木镇』。」

「不会吧？」

「人家还管他叫拿撒勒人，这跟『边地林子里长大的人』差不多是一回事。」

拉尔夫便把他主日学里研读过的那些段落一一读来，讲的尽是耶稣如何俯就卑微：那些到他跟前来的税吏、妓女和穷苦人的故事。他还读到住在仅六英里外的拿但业说的话：「『拿撒勒还能出什么好的么？』」

「可不正是克利夫蒂人讲弗拉特溪的话么。」巴德插嘴道。

===Original===
“Do you think I could begin without being baptized?” he added presently.

“Why not? Let’s begin now to do the best we can, by his help.”

“You mean, then, that I’m to begin now to put in my best licks for Jesus Christ, and that he’ll help me?”

This shocked Ralph’s veneration a little. But it was the sincere utterance of an earnest soul. It may not have been an orthodox start, but it was the one start for Bud. And there be those who have repeated with the finest aesthetic appreciation the old English liturgies who have never known religious aspiration so sincere as that of this ignorant young Hercules, whose best confession was that he meant hereafter “to put in his best licks for Jesus Christ.” And there be those who can define repentance and faith to the turning of a hair who never made so genuine a start for the kingdom of Heaven as Bud Means did.

===Chinese===
「你看俺不受洗，能开这个头么？」过了一会儿他又问。

「为什么不能？咱们这就开始，靠祂的帮助，尽力做到最好吧。」

「那你是说，俺这就开始为耶稣基督出俺最卖力气的劲儿，祂还会帮俺？」

这话让拉尔夫的敬畏之心微微受了些震动。可它是一个恳切灵魂的真诚吐露。这或许算不得一个正统的开头，但对巴德来说，这就是那唯一的一个开头。世上有些人，能把古老的英国礼拜仪式吟诵得最具审美情趣，却从来不曾见识过像这个无知的年轻大力士这般真诚的宗教渴望——他最好的忏悔，就是今后打算「为耶稣基督出自己最卖力气的劲儿」。世上也有些人，能把悔改与信心定义到毫厘不爽，却从来不曾像巴德·米恩斯这样，为天国做过如此真切的一个开头。

===Original===
Ralph said yes, that he thought that was just it. At least, he guessed if there was something more, the man that was putting in his best licks would be sure to find it out.

“Do you think he’d help a feller? Seems to me it would be number one to have God help you. Not to help you fight other folks, but to help you when it comes to fighting the devil inside. But you see I don’t belong to no church.”

“Well, let’s you and me have one right off. Two people that help one another to serve God make a church.”

I am afraid this ecclesiastical theory will not be considered orthodox. It was Ralph’s, and I write it down at the risk of bringing him into condemnation.

But other people before the days of Bud and Ralph have discussed church organization when they should have been doing Christian work. For both of them had forgotten the danger that hung over the old basket-maker, until Shocky burst into the schoolhouse, weeping. Indeed, the poor, nervous little frame was ready to go into convulsions.

“Miss Hawkins﻿—”

Bud started at mention of the name.

“Miss Hawkins has just been over to say that a crowd is going to tar and feather Mr. Pearson tonight. And﻿—” here Shocky wept again. “And he won’t run, but he’s took up the old flintlock, and he’ll die in his tracks.”

===Chinese===
拉尔夫说是的，他想正是这么回事。至少，他估摸着，若是还有什么更进一步的东西，那个正在出最卖力气劲儿的人，准保自己就能找出来。

「你看祂肯帮一个伙计么？在俺想来，有上帝帮着你，那可是头一等的好事。不是帮你去打别人，是等你跟心里头的魔鬼开打的时候帮你。可你瞧，俺什么教会也不属于。」

「那好，咱们俩这就在这儿立一个。两个互相帮着事奉上帝的人，就是一个教会。」

恐怕这个教会理论不会被人视为正统。它是拉尔夫的理论，我把它记下来，甘冒把他也牵连受责的风险。

不过在巴德和拉尔德之前的年代，也早有人在应该做基督徒工夫的时候，去高谈教会组织。因为他们俩都把悬在老编篮匠头上的危险忘了个干净，直到肖基哭着冲进校舍。确实，那可怜的、神经质的小身板儿，眼看就要抽搐起来了。

「霍金斯小姐——」

一听到这个名字，巴德浑身一激灵。

「霍金斯小姐刚到这边来说，有一伙人今晚要给皮尔逊先生涂柏油、粘羽毛。还有——」说到这儿，肖基又哭了。「还有他不肯跑，他抄起了那杆老燧发枪，说要死就死在自己的地界上。」

## X The Devil of Silence / 十 沉默的魔鬼

===Original===
Ralph had reason to fear Small, who was a native of the same village of Lewisburg, and some five years the elder. Some facts in the doctor’s life had come into Ralph’s possession in such a way as to confirm lifelong suspicion without giving him power to expose Small, who was firmly intrenched in the good graces of the people of the county-seat village of Lewisburg, where he had grown up, and of the little crossroads village of Clifty, where his “shingle” now hung.

Small was no ordinary villain. He was a genius. Your ordinary hypocrite talks cant. Small talked nothing. He was the coolest, the steadiest, the most silent, the most promising boy ever born in Lewisburg. He made no pretensions. He set up no claims. He uttered no professions. He went right on and lived a life above reproach. Your vulgar hypocrite makes long prayers in prayer-meeting. Small did nothing of the sort. He sat still in prayer-meeting, and listened to the elders as a modest young man should. Your commonplace hypocrite boasts. Small never alluded to himself, and thus a consummate egotist got credit for modesty. It is but an indifferent trick for a hypocrite to make temperance speeches. Dr. Small did not even belong to a temperance society. But he could never be persuaded to drink even so much as a cup of tea. There was something sublime in the quiet voice with which he would say, “Cold water, if you please,” to a lady tempting him with smoking coffee on a cold morning. There was no exultation, no sense of merit in the act. Everything was done in a modest and matter-of-course way beautiful to behold. And his face was a neutral tint. Neither face nor voice expressed anything. Only a keen reader of character might have asked whether all there was in that eye could live contented with this cool, austere, self-contained life; whether there would not be somewhere a volcanic eruption. But if there was any sea of molten lava beneath, the world did not discover it. Wild boys were sick of having Small held up to them as the most immaculate of men.17

===Chinese===
拉尔夫有理由怕斯莫尔（Small）。此人与他同是刘易斯堡村人，比他年长五岁上下。这位大夫生平的几件底细，曾落到拉尔夫手里，恰足以坐实他积年的疑心，却给不了他揭穿斯莫尔的凭据。斯莫尔根基牢靠：他在县城刘易斯堡长大，深得镇上人心；如今他那块行医招牌又挂在小岔路口村子克利夫蒂，两边都吃得开。

斯莫尔不是寻常的恶棍。他是个天才。平庸的伪君子满嘴假道学，斯莫尔什么也不说。他是刘易斯堡有史以来最冷静、最沉稳、最沉默、最有出息的后生。他不装模作样，不自标榜，不发宏论，只管我行我素，过一种无可指摘的日子。俗气的伪君子在祷告会上作冗长的祷告，斯莫尔从不来这一套。他在祷告会上安安静静坐着，像个谦逊的年轻人那样听长老们说话。凡俗的伪君子爱吹嘘，斯莫尔从不提自己，于是这位登峰造极的自我中心者反倒博得了谦逊的美名。伪君子做戒酒演说，不过是三流把戏，斯莫尔大夫连戒酒会都不曾参加。可你就是劝不动他，连一杯茶他也决计不沾。大冷早晨，有太太端着热气腾腾的咖啡诱惑他，他只用平平淡淡的声音说一句「请给我白水」，那里面自有一股庄严。没有得意，没有自矜，一切都做得谦虚而自然，煞是好看。他的脸是一副中性色，脸上、声里都读不出任何东西。只有精于相人的人才会问一句：那只眼睛里装着的一切，当真能安于这样冷静、严苛、自闭的日子么？会不会在哪里来上一场火山爆发？可纵使底下真有一海熔岩，世人也没能发现。野小子们可烦透了：人们总把斯莫尔举出来，当作天下头一号的完人。17

===Original===
Ralph had failed to get two schools for which he had applied, and had attributed both failures to certain shrugs of Dr. Small. And now, when he found Small at the house of Granny Sanders, the center of intelligence as well as of ignorance for the neighborhood, he trembled. Not that Small would say anything. He never said anything. He damned people by a silence worse than words.

Granny Sanders was not a little flattered by the visit.

“Why, doctor, howdy, howdy! Come in, take a cheer. I am glad to see you. I ’lowed you’d come. Old Dr. Flounder used to say he larnt lots o’ things of me. But most of the doctors sence hez been kinder stuck up, you know. But I know’d you fer a man of intelligence.”

Meantime, Small, by his grave silence and attention, had almost smothered the old hag with flattery. “Many’s the case I’ve cured with yarbs and things. Nigh upon twenty year ago they was a man lived over on Wild Cat Run as had a breakin’-out on his side. ’Twas the left side, jes below the waist. Doctor couldn’t do nothin’. ’Twas Doctor Peacham. He never would have nothin’ to do with ‘ole woman’s cures.’ Well, the man was goin’ to die. Everybody seed that. And they come a-drivin’ away over here all the way from the Wild Cat. Think of that air! I never was so flustered. But as soon as I laid eyes on that air man, I says, says I, that air man, says I, has got the shingles, says I. I know’d the minute I seed it. And if they’d gone clean around, nothing could a saved him. I says, says I, git me a black cat. So I jist killed a black cat, and let the blood run all over the swellin’. I tell you, doctor, they’s nothin’ like it. That man was well in a month.”

===Chinese===
拉尔夫谋过两个教职，都没谋着，他把两回失败都归到斯莫尔大夫的某几次耸肩上。如今他竟在桑德斯婆婆家里撞见了斯莫尔——这婆婆是本一带聪明与愚昧的双料中枢——他心里直打颤。倒不是怕斯莫尔会说什么。他从来什么也不说。他用一种比言语更毒的沉默给人定罪。

这一趟来访，把桑德斯婆婆奉承得心花怒放。

「咋，大夫，你好啊，好啊！快进来，坐个凳儿。见着你真高兴。俺就估摸着你会来。老弗劳恩德大夫常念叨，说他跟俺学了好些本事。可打他往后的大夫们，一个个都有点儿端架子，你是知道的。可俺一眼就相出你是个有学问的人。」

这工夫，斯莫尔凭着他那一本正经的沉默与专注，差不多把这老妖婆用奉承闷了个透。「俺用草药跟这些那些治好的病症，多着呢。快二十年前吧，野猫溪那边住着一个人，肋腰上生了一圈疮。是左边，腰眼儿底下一点。大夫一点辙也没有。那是皮彻姆大夫。他从来不沾『老婆子的土方子』。嘿，那人眼看要死了，谁都瞧出来了。他们就套上车，从野猫溪一路赶了过来。你想想！俺这辈子没这么慌过手脚。可俺一搭眼瞧见那个人，俺就说，俺说，那个人，俺说，得的是缠腰龙，俺说。俺一瞅见就知道了。那疮要是长满一整圈，那就啥也救不了他喽。俺就说，俺说，给俺逮只黑猫来。俺就杀了只黑猫，让血淌了那肿胀处一身。俺跟你说，大夫，天底下没别的能比得上。那人一个月就壮实了。」

===Original===
“Did you use the blood warm?” asked Small, with a solemnity most edifying.

These were almost the only words he had uttered since he entered the cabin.

“Laws, yes; I jest let it run right out of the cat’s tail onto the breakin’-out. And fer airesipelus, I don’t know nothin’ so good as the blood of a black hen.”

“How old?” asked the doctor.

“There you showed yer science, doctor! They’s no power in a pullet. The older the black hen the better. And you know the cure fer rheumatiz?” And here the old woman got down a bottle of grease. “That’s ile from a black dog. Ef it’s rendered right, it’ll knock the hind sights off of any rheumatiz you ever see. But it must be rendered in the dark of the moon. Else a black dog’s ile a’n’t worth no more nor a white one’s.”

===Chinese===
「血你是使温乎的么？」斯莫尔问，神情庄重，最是令人起敬。

这几乎是他进屋以来开口说的仅有几句话。

「老天爷，是温乎的；俺就让它顺着猫尾巴直接淌到疮口上。再说到丹毒，俺不知道还有啥比黑母鸡的血更好使的。」

「多老的鸡？」大夫问。

「这话可就见出你的学问了，大夫！小母鸡的血可没半点儿劲儿。黑母鸡越老越好。风湿病的方子，你知道不？」说着，老太太取下一瓶油膏。「这是黑狗油。熬得得法，多凶的风湿它也能治得服服帖帖。可一定得在月黑天里熬。不然黑狗油就不比白狗油金贵了。」

===Original===
And all this time Small was smelling of the uncorked bottle, taking a little on his finger and feeling of it, and thus feeling his way to the heart—drier than her herbs—of the old witch. And then he went round the cabin gravely, lifting each separate bunch of dried yarbs from its nail, smelling of it, and then, by making an interrogation-point of his silent face, he managed to get a lecture from her on each article in her materia medica with the most marvelous stories illustrative of their virtues. When the Granny had got her fill of his silent flattery, he was ready to carry forward his main purpose.

There was something weird about this silent man’s ability to turn the conversation as he chose to have it go. Sitting by the Granny’s tea-table, nibbling cornbread while he drank his glass of water, having declined even her sassafras, he ceased to stimulate her medical talk and opened the vein of gossip. Once started, Granny Sanders was sure to allude to the robbery. And once on the robbery the doctor’s course was clear.

===Chinese===
这半天里，斯莫尔闻着那只拔了塞的瓶子，又用指头蘸一点儿捻搓着，就这样一步步摸进了这老巫婆的心——那颗心比她的草药还干。随后他神色庄重地在小屋里转了一圈，把一串串干药草从钉子上一个个摘下来闻一闻，再把那张不吭声的脸摆成一个问号，硬是让她就着自己药匣子里的每一样药材讲了一课，还附带着讲它们的神效，故事一个比一个玄。待老太太受足了他这份无声的奉承，他也就准备停当，要推进他的正事了。

这个沉默的人能把话头随心所欲地拨来拨去，这本事里带着一股邪性。他坐在老太太的茶桌旁，喝着一杯白水，啃着玉米饼——连她的黄樟茶都谢绝了——这时他不再招引她谈医道，而是把闲话这条脉给切开了。话匣子一开，桑德斯婆婆准会提到那桩盗窃案。而一提盗窃，大夫的路数就清楚了。

===Original===
“I ’low somebody not fur away is in this ’ere business!”

Not by a word, nor even by a nod, but by some motion of the eyelids, perhaps, Small indicated that he agreed with her.

“Who d’ye s’pose ’tis?”

But Dr. Small was not in the habit of supposing. He moved his head in a quiet way, just the least perceptible bit, but so that the old creature understood that he could give light if he wanted to.

“I dunno anybody that’s been ’bout here long as could be suspected.”

Another motion of the eyelids indicated Small’s agreement with this remark.

===Chinese===
「俺估摸着这档子事儿，离这儿不远就有人掺和！」

斯莫尔没有用一个字，甚至连点头都没有，只约莫是眼皮那么一动，表示他同意她的话。

「你猜是谁？」

可斯莫尔大夫没有猜人的习惯。他把头轻轻一晃，幅度小得刚刚能觉出来，却足以让这老东西明白：他要是愿意，是能点破的。

「俺不知道在这一带晃悠过这么久的，有谁能让人生疑。」

眼皮又动了一动，表示斯莫尔赞成这话。

===Original===
“They a’n’t nobody come in here lately ’ceppin’ the master.”

Small looked vacantly at the wall.

“But I ’low he’s allers bore a tip-top character.”

The doctor was too busy looking at his cornbread to answer this remark even by a look.

“But I think these oversmart young men’ll bear looking arter, *I* do.”

Dr. Small raised his eyes and let them *shine* an assent. That was all.

===Chinese===
「近来除了教书先生，没有旁人上这儿来过。」

斯莫尔空洞地望着墙壁。

「可俺估摸，他向来名声顶呱呱。」

大夫正忙着看他的玉米饼，连用眼神答这句话的工夫都没有。

「可俺觉得，这些太精灵的年轻人哪，得盯着点儿才好，*俺*就是这么想的。」

斯莫尔大夫抬起眼来，让眼中*闪*出一道赞许。如此而已。

===Original===
“Shouldn’t wonder ef our master was overly fond of gals.”

Doctor looks down at his plate.

“Had plenty of sweethearts afore he walked home with Hanner Thomson t’other night, I’ll bet.”

Did Dr. Small shrug his shoulder? Granny thought she detected a faint motion of the sort, but she could not be sure.

“And I think as how that a feller what trifles with gals’ hearts and then runs off ten miles, maybe a’n’t no better’n he had orter be. That’s what I says, says I.”

===Chinese===
「俺们这位教书先生要是见一个姑娘爱一个，俺一点儿也不奇怪。」

大夫低头看盘子。

「那天夜里他送汉娜·汤姆森回家以前，相好管保多得是，俺敢打赌。」

斯莫尔大夫耸肩了么？婆婆觉得她捕捉到了那么一丝丝动静，可又拿不准。

「俺还想呢，一个小伙子要是玩弄姑娘家的心，回头一跑十英里，只怕也不是什么好货。俺就是这么说的，俺说。」

===Original===
To this general remark Dr. Small assented in his invisible—shall I say *intangible*?—way.

“I allers think, maybe, that some folks has found it best to leave home and go away. You can’t never tell. But when people is a-bein’ robbed it’s well to lookout. Hey?”

“I think so,” said Small quietly, and, having taken his hat and bowed a solemn and respectful adieu, he departed.

He had not spoken twenty words, but he had satisfied the newsmonger of Flat Creek that Ralph was a bad character at home and worthy of suspicion of burglary.

===Chinese===
对这句总论，斯莫尔大夫以他那看不见的——我该说是*摸不着*的？——方式表示了同意。

「俺老是想啊，有些人兴许是觉得，还是离了家门、远走他乡的好。谁也说不准。可如今人家接二连三失窃，那可就得留点儿神了。是不？」

「我也这么想。」斯莫尔轻声说，随即拿起帽子，庄严而有礼地告了别，走了。

他统共没说上二十个字，却让弗拉特溪这位包打听的婆婆信定了：拉尔夫在家乡就是个坏种，眼下这桩盗窃案，也犯得着疑他。

## XXVIII The Flight / 二十八 逃亡

===Original===
About ten days after Ralph’s return to Flat Creek things came to a crisis.

The master was rather relieved at first to have the crisis come. He had been holding juvenile Flat Creek under his feet by sheer force of will. And such an exercise of “psychic power” is very exhausting. In racing on the Ohio the engineer sometimes sends the largest of the firemen to hold the safety valve down, and this he does by hanging himself to the lever by his hands. Ralph felt that he had been holding the safety-valve down, and that he was so weary of the operation that an explosion would be a real relief. He was a little tired of having everybody look on him as a thief. It was a little irksome to know that new bolts were put on the doors of the houses in which he had stayed. And now that Shocky was gone, and Bud had turned against him, and Aunt Matilda suspected him, and even poor, weak, exquisite Walter Johnson would not associate with him, he felt himself an outlaw indeed. He would have gone away to Texas or the new gold fields in California had it not been for one thing. That letter on blue foolscap paper kept a little warmth in his heart.

His course from school on the evening that something happened lay through the sugar-camp. Among the dark trunks of the maples, solemn and lofty pillars, he debated the case. To stay, or to flee? The worn nerves could not keep their present tension much longer.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫回弗拉特溪大约十天之后，事情到了摊牌的关头。

危机真来的时候，这位教书先生倒先松了口气。他一直凭着一股意志力，把弗拉特溪的娃娃们踩在脚底下。这种「心力」的消耗最磨人。在俄亥俄河上赛船，轮机员有时派块头最大的司炉去压住安全阀，那汉子便双手吊在杠杆上死死坠着。拉尔夫觉得自己正是这么吊着安全阀的人，而且坠得筋疲力尽，索性炸一下反倒痛快。人人都把他当贼看，他有点儿腻了。想到他住过的那些人家门上又添了新门闩，心里也不痛快。如今肖基走了，巴德跟他翻了脸，玛蒂尔达大婶疑他，连那个可怜、软弱、精细过人的沃尔特·约翰逊都不肯跟他来往，他真觉得自己成了法外之徒。要不是还有一件事，他早远走得克萨斯，或是上加利福尼亚的新金矿去了。那封写在蓝色大页纸上的信，叫他心里存着一点暖和气。

出事那天傍晚，他从学校回来走的路要穿过熬糖林。在糖枫黑黢黢的树干之间——一根根庄严肃穆的高柱——他把这桩案子掂量来掂量去。留下，还是逃走？疲惫的神经再也绷不住眼下这根弦了。

===Original===
It was just by the brook, or, as they say in Indiana, the “branch,”25 that something happened which brought him to a sudden decision. Ralph never afterward could forget that brook. It was a swift-running little stream, that did not babble blatantly over the stones. It ran through a thicket of willows, through the sugar-camp, and out into Means’s pasture. Ralph had just passed through the thicket, had just crossed the brook on the half-decayed log that spanned it, when, as he emerged from the water-willows on the other side, he started with a sudden shock. For there was Hannah, with a white, white face, holding out a little note folded like an old-fashioned thumb-paper.

“Go quick!” she stammered as she slipped it into Ralph’s hand, inadvertently touching his fingers with her own—a touch that went tingling through the schoolmaster’s nerves. But she had hardly said the words until she was gone down the brookside path and over into the pasture. A few minutes afterward she drove the cows up into the lot and meekly took her scolding from Mrs. Means for being gone sech an awful long time, like a lazy, good-fer-nothin piece of goods that she was.

Ralph opened the thumb-paper note, written on a page torn from an old copybook, in Bud’s “hand-write” and running:

> “i Put in my best licks, taint no use. Run fer yore life. A plans on foot to tar an fether or wuss tonight. Go rite off. Things is awful juberous.26
> “Bud.”

===Chinese===
恰恰是在那条小溪边——印第安纳人管它叫「溪沟」25——发生了一件事，让他当机立断。拉尔夫后来始终忘不了那条小溪。那是一条湍急的小水流，不在石头上哗哗地喧嚷。它穿过一片柳丛，穿过熬糖林，流出米恩斯家的牧场。拉尔夫刚穿过柳丛，刚从那根横跨溪面的半朽圆木上过来，正从水边的柳树里钻出去，猛地吃了一惊。原来汉娜就站在那儿，脸白得吓人，递出一张叠成老式指纸样儿的小字条。

「快走！」她把字条塞进拉尔夫手里，结结巴巴地说，指尖无意间碰着他的手指——这一碰，麻酥酥地直透过校长的神经。可话音未落，她人已经顺着溪边小径走远，翻进了牧场。几分钟之后，她把奶牛赶进圈里，低眉顺眼地挨了米恩斯太太一顿数落，骂她去了这么老半天，真是个又懒又没用的废物丫头。

拉尔夫打开那张指纸字条，是从旧抄本上撕下的一页，是巴德的「手笔」，上面写道：

> 「俺出了俺最卖力气的劲儿，白搭。快逃命吧。有人张罗着今黑儿给你涂柏油粘羽毛，兴许还更糟。赶紧走。事情悬乎得很。26
> 「巴德。」

===Original===
The first question with Ralph was whether he could depend on Bud. But he soon made up his mind that treachery of any sort was not one of his traits. He had mourned over the destruction of Bud’s good resolutions by Martha Hawkins’ refusal, and being a disinterested party he could have comforted Bud by explaining Martha’s “mitten.” But he felt sure that Bud was not treacherous. It was a relief, then, as he stood there to know that the false truce was over, and worst had come to worst.

His first impulse was to stay and fight. But his nerves were not strong enough to execute so foolhardy a resolution. He seemed to see a man behind every maple-trunk. Darkness was fast coming on, and he knew that his absence from supper at his boarding-place could not fail to excite suspicion. There was no time to be lost. So he started.

Once run from a danger, and panic is apt to ensue. The forest, the stalk-fields, the dark hollows through which he passed, seemed to be peopled with terrors. He knew Small and Jones well enough to know that every avenue of escape would be carefully picketed. So there was nothing to do but to take the shortest path to the old trysting place, the Spring-in-rock.

===Chinese===
拉尔夫的第一个疑问是：巴德靠不靠得住？可他很快拿定了主意：背叛二字，不在那小子的品性里头。他曾为巴德的好决心让玛莎·霍金斯的回绝毁掉而难过；他是个局外人，本可以给巴德解释解释玛莎那「发还的手套」，安慰安慰他。可他确信巴德不是奸诈之人。于是，站在那儿，他知道那虚假的停战已经到头、最坏的已经临头，反倒松了一口气。

他的第一个冲动是留下打一架。可他的神经撑不起这么莽撞的决心。他仿佛看见每根枫树干后头都站着一个人。夜色来得飞快，他知道，晚饭时不在寄宿的人家露面，免不了要招疑。一刻也耽搁不得。他动身了。

从危险跟前跑开一遭，恐慌便会接踵而至。他一路穿过的树林、割过茬的田地、黑黢黢的山沟，仿佛都塞满了骇人的东西。斯莫尔和琼斯这两个人的底细，他太清楚了：每一条逃跑的路，都会被人仔细把住口子。如此一来别无他法，只有抄最近的路，奔那老约会地去——岩中泉。

===Original===
Here he sat and shook with terror. Angry with himself, he inly denounced himself for a coward. But the effect was really a physical one. The chill and panic now were the reaction from the previous strain.

For when the sound of his pursuers’ voices broke upon his ears early in the evening, Ralph shook no more; the warm blood set back again toward the extremities, and his self-control returned when he needed it. He gathered some stones about him, as the only weapons of defense at hand. The mob was on the cliff above. But he thought that he heard footsteps in the bed of the creek below. If this were so, there could be no doubt that his hiding-place was suspected.

“O Hank!” shouted Bud from the top of the cliff to someone in the creek below, “be sure to look at the Spring-in-rock—I think he’s there.”

This hint was not lost on Ralph, who speedily changed his quarters by climbing up to a secluded, shelf-like ledge above the spring. He was none too soon, for Pete Jones and Hank Banta were soon looking all around the spring for him, while he held a twenty-pound stone over their heads ready to drop upon them in case they should think of looking on the ledge above.

===Chinese===
他在这里坐下来，吓得直抖。他恨自己，在心里骂自己是懦夫。可这反应其实是生理上的。此刻的寒噤与慌乱，是前一阵紧绷过后的反弹。

因为，当天刚擦黑、追兵的人声传进他耳朵时，拉尔夫反倒不抖了；暖血流回到四肢，自控力也在正需要的时候回来了。他在身边拢了一些石头，那是手边仅有的兵器。暴徒们在崖上。可他觉得，崖下沟床里有脚步声。若是真的，他那藏身处准是被人猜着了。

「喂，汉克！」巴德在崖顶朝沟下什么人喊道，「可别忘了瞧瞧岩中泉——我瞅着他就在那儿。」

这个暗号拉尔夫听进去了，赶紧挪窝，攀上泉水上方一处背静的、架子似的岩台。他爬得正是时候，皮特·琼斯和汉克·班塔随即就在泉水四下里搜寻起来，而他拎着一块二十磅重的石头悬在他们头顶，只等他们起意往岩台上瞧，就撒手砸下去。

===Original===
When the crowd were gone Ralph knew that one road was open to him. He could follow down the creek to Clifty, and thence he might escape. But, traveling down to Clifty, he debated whether it was best to escape. To flee was to confess his guilt, to make himself an outlaw, to put an insurmountable barrier between himself and Hannah, whose terror-stricken and anxious face as she stood by the brook-willows haunted him now, and was an involuntary witness to her love.

Long before he reached Clifty his mind was made up not to flee another mile. He knocked at the door of Squire Underwood. But Squire Underwood was also a doctor, and had been called away. He knocked at the door of Squire Doolittle. But Squire Doolittle had gone to Lewisburg. He was about to give up all hope of being able to surrender himself to the law when he met Squire Hawkins, who had come over to Clifty to avoid responsibility for the ill-deeds of his neighbors which he was powerless to prevent.

“Is that you, Mr. Hartsook?”

“Yes, and I want you to arrest me and try me here in Clifty.”

===Chinese===
人群散尽后，拉尔夫明白自己还有一条路。他可以沿溪而下到克利夫蒂，从那儿脱身。可往克利夫蒂去的路上，他又掂量起逃与不逃来。一逃就是认罪，就是自绝于法，就是在自己与汉娜之间砌一堵翻不过去的墙——她立在溪边柳丛旁时那惊惶忧切的面容，此刻正缠着他，不由自己做主地见证着她的爱。

离克利夫蒂还远，他已拿定主意，再逃一步也不逃了。他去敲安德伍德治安官的门。可安德伍德治安官兼着行医，出诊去了。他去敲杜利特尔治安官的门。可杜利特尔治安官去了刘易斯堡。他眼看要放弃向官府自首的指望时，迎面遇上了霍金斯治安官——他是躲到克利夫蒂来的，免得为邻居们那些他又无力阻止的坏事担干系。

「是哈特索克先生吗？」

「是我。我要你逮捕我，就在克利夫蒂这儿审我。」

## XXIV The Good Samaritan / 二十四 好撒玛利亚人

===Original===
The Methodist church to which Mrs. Matilda White and Miss Nancy Sawyer belonged was the leading one in Lewisburg, as it was in most county-seat villages in Indiana. If I may be permitted to express my candid and charitable opinion of the difference between the two women, I shall have to use the old Quaker locution, and say that Miss Sawyer was a Methodist and likewise a Christian; Mrs. White was a Methodist, but I fear she was not likewise.

As to the first part of this assertion, there was no room to doubt Miss Nancy’s piety. She could get happy in class-meeting (for who had a better right?), and could witness a good experience in the quarterly love-feast. But it is not upon these grounds that I base my opinion of Miss Nancy. Do not even the Pharisees the same? She never dreamed that she had any right to speak of “Christian Perfection” (which, as Mrs. Partington said of total depravity, is an excellent doctrine if it is lived up to); but when a woman’s heart is full of devout affections and good purposes, when her head devises liberal and Christlike things, when her hands are always open to the poor and always busy with acts of love and self-denial, and when her feet are ever eager to run upon errands of mercy, why, if there be anything worthy of being called Christian Perfection in this world of imperfection, I do not know why such an one does not possess it. What need of analyzing her experiences in vacuo to find out the state of her soul?

===Chinese===
玛蒂尔达·怀特太太和南希·索耶小姐所属的卫理公会，是刘易斯堡的头号教会，在印第安纳大多数县城也莫不如此。倘若容我坦率而厚道地说一说这两位妇人的分别，我只得借用贵格会的老话：索耶小姐是卫理公会徒，同时也是基督徒；怀特太太是卫理公会徒，只怕就未必同时是了。

至于这话的前半截，南希小姐的虔敬毋庸置疑。她能在班会上喜乐盈溢（谁比她更有资格呢？），也能在季度的爱宴上作美好的见证。但我对南希小姐的看法，并不建立在这些上面。法利赛人不也一样么？她做梦也不曾想过自己有资格谈「基督徒的完全」（照帕廷顿太太论全然败坏的话说，这门教义顶好，只要你行得出）；可当一个妇人的心满贮着虔诚的深情与良善的意愿，当她的头脑谋划着慷慨而像基督的事，当她的双手向穷人永远敞开、永远忙着爱与舍己的工夫，当她的双脚总是欢欢喜喜地奔走于慈悲的差事——那么，倘若这不完满的世上还有什么配称「基督徒的完全」，我不知道这样的人凭什么不算拥有它。又何必把她的经验搁到真空里去剖析，好查验她灵魂的光景呢？

===Original===
How Miss Nancy managed to live on her slender income and be so generous was a perpetual source of perplexity to the gossips of Lewisburg. And now that she declared that Mrs. Thomson and Shocky should not return to the poorhouse there was a general outcry from the whole Committee of Intermeddlers that she would bring herself to the poorhouse before she died. But Nancy Sawyer was the richest woman in Lewisburg, though nobody knew it, and though she herself did not once suspect it.

How Miss Nancy and the preacher conspired together, and how they managed to bring Mrs. Thomson’s case up at the time of the “Sacramental Service” in the afternoon of that Sunday in Lewisburg, and how the preacher made a touching statement of it just before the regular “Collection for the Poor” was taken, and how the warmhearted Methodists put in dollars instead of dimes while the Presiding Elder read those passages about Zaccheus and other liberal people, and how the congregation sang

> “He dies, the Friend of sinners dies”

more lustily than ever, after having performed this Christian act—how all this happened I can not take up the reader’s time to tell. But I can assure him that the nearly blind English woman did not room with blasphemous old Mowley any more, and that the blue-drilling pauper frock gave way to something better, and that grave little Shocky even danced with delight, and declared that God hadn’t forgot, though he’d thought that He had. And Mrs. Matilda White remarked that it was a shame that the collection for the poor at a Methodist sacramental service should be given to a woman who was a member of the Church of England, and like as not never soundly converted!

And Shocky slept in his mother’s arms and prayed God not to forget Hannah, while Shocky’s mother knit stockings for the store day and night, and day and night she prayed and hoped.

===Chinese===
南希小姐靠那点微薄收入怎么过活、还怎么这样慷慨，是刘易斯堡长舌妇们永远解不开的难题。如今她放话说，汤姆森太太和肖基再也不许回济贫院，整个「多管闲事委员会」一片哗然，都说她不把自己先送进济贫院才怪。可南希·索耶是刘易斯堡最有钱的女人，虽然谁也不知道，连她自己也一回都不曾疑心过。

南希小姐和传道人是怎么合计的，怎么赶在那个礼拜天下午刘易斯堡「圣餐礼拜」的当口，把汤姆森太太的事提了上去，传道人又怎么在例行的「周济穷人奉献」刚要收之前，把这情形动人地说了一遍，热心肠的卫理公会教友们又怎么趁教区长老读着撒该等一干慷慨人物那些经文的当儿，把一角的银币换成了整块的银元，会众又怎么在行完这桩基督徒善举之后，把那首

> 「祂死了，罪人的朋友死了」

唱得比什么时候都起劲——这一切都是怎么办成的，我不敢再耗费读者的光阴细讲。但我可以向读者担保：那位眼睛快瞎尽的英国妇人，再也不用和渎神的老莫利同住一屋了；那身蓝粗布的贫民褂子，也换成了体面些的衣裳；连严肃的小肖基都高兴得手舞足蹈，一口咬定上帝没有忘记，虽然他原以为祂忘了。而玛蒂尔达·怀特太太则议论道：卫理公会圣餐礼拜上周济穷人的奉献，竟给了一个英国国教会的妇人，八成还压根儿没有好好地悔改归正，真是岂有此理！

肖基睡在母亲怀里，祷告上帝别忘了汉娜；肖基的母亲没日没夜地给铺子织袜子，也没日没夜地祷告着、盼望着。

## XII The Hardshell Preacher / 十二 硬壳派传道人

===Original===
“They’s preachin’ down to Bethel Meetin’-house today,” said the Squire at breakfast. Twenty years in the West could not cure Squire Hawkins of saying “to” for “at.” “I rather guess as how the old man Bosaw will give pertickeler fits to our folks today.” For Squire Hawkins, having been expelled from the “Hardshell” church of which Mr. Bosaw was pastor, for the grave offense of joining a temperance society, had become a member of the “Reformers,” the very respectable people who now call themselves “Disciples,” but whom the profane will persist in calling “Campbellites.” They had a church in the village of Clifty, three miles away.

I know that explanations are always abominable to story readers, as they are to story writers, but as so many of my readers have never had the inestimable privilege of sitting under the gospel as it is ministered in enlightened neighborhoods like Flat Creek, I find myself under the necessity—need-cessity the Rev. Mr. Bosaw would call it—of rising to explain. Some people think the “Hardshells” a myth, and some sensitive Baptist people at the East resent all allusion to them. But the “Hardshell Baptists,” or, as they are otherwise called, the “Whisky Baptists,” and the “Forty-gallon Baptists,” exist in all the old Western and Southwestern States. They call themselves “Anti-means Baptists” from their Antinomian tenets. Their confession of faith is a caricature of Calvinism, and is expressed by their preachers about as follows: “Ef you’re elected, you’ll be saved; ef you a’n’t, you’ll be damned. God’ll take keer of his elect. It’s a sin to run Sunday-schools, or temp’rince s’cieties, or to send missionaries. You let God’s business alone. What is to be will be, and you can’t hender it.” This writer has attended a Sunday-school, the superintendent of which was solemnly arraigned and expelled from the Hardshell Church for “meddling with God’s business” by holding a Sunday-school. Of course the Hardshells are prodigiously illiterate, and often vicious. Some of their preachers are notorious drunkards. They sing their sermons out sometimes for three hours at a stretch.20

===Chinese===
「今儿个伯特利礼拜堂里有讲道。」早饭桌上，治安官说。在西部住了二十年，也没治好霍金斯治安官把「在」说成「到」的老毛病。「俺估摸着，老博索今儿个准要把俺们的人骂得狗血淋头。」原来霍金斯治安官因为加入了戒酒会，犯下弥天大罪，被博索先生当牧师的那个「硬壳派」教会开了革，转而投了「改革派」——就是如今自称「门徒会」的那批极其可敬的人，可那些不敬虔的偏要一口一个「坎伯尔派」地叫。他们在三英里外的克利夫蒂村有一座教堂。

我知道，解释这玩意儿，听故事的人烦，讲故事的人也烦。可是我的读者里，许多人怕是一辈子没享过那份无价的权利——坐在弗拉特溪这样开化地方所传讲的福音底下。于是我发现自己不得不要站起来解释一番——博索牧师先生会把这叫作「必——须」。有人以为「硬壳派」是个讹传，东部有些敏感的浸礼会人士，一听人提他们就跳脚。可「硬壳浸礼会」（Hardshell Baptists）——也有人管他们叫「威士忌浸礼会」「四十加仑浸礼会」——在西部、西南部各个老州里处处都有。他们凭着反律法主义的教义，自称「反手段浸礼会」。他们的信条是加尔文主义的一幅漫画，他们的传道人大致是这么表述的：「你要是蒙了拣选，就得救；你要是没有，就下地狱。上帝自会看顾祂的选民。办主日学啦、戒酒会啦、差遣传教士啦，都是罪。你别去掺和上帝的事。该来的总会来，你拦不住。」笔者就上过一个主日学，那主日学的负责人被一本正经地传了去，并以「插手上帝的事务」的罪名——就因为他办了个主日学——被硬壳派教会开除了。当然，硬壳派大都目不识丁到了骇人的地步，而且往往心术不正。他们有些传道人是出了名的酒鬼。他们那种唱出来的讲道，有时能一口气唱上三个钟头。20

===Original===
Ralph found that he was to ride the “clay-bank mare,” the only one of the horses that would “carry double,” and that consequently he would have to take Miss Hawkins behind him. If it had been Hannah instead, Ralph might not have objected to this “young Lochinvar” mode of riding with a lady on “the croup,” but Martha Hawkins was another affair. He had only this consolation; his keeping the company of Miss Hawkins might serve to disarm the resentment of Bud. At all events, he had no choice. What designs the Squire had in this arrangement he could not tell; but the clay-bank mare carried him to meeting on that December morning, with Martha Hawkins behind. And as Miss Hawkins was not used to this mode of locomotion, she was in a state of delightful fright every time the horse sank to the knees in the soft, yellow Flat Creek clay.

“We don’t go to church so at the East,” she said. “The mud isn’t so deep at the East. When I was to Bosting—” but Ralph never heard what happened when she was to Bosting, for just as she said Bosting the mare put her foot into a deep hole molded by the foot of the Squire’s horse, and already full of muddy water.

As the mare’s foot went twelve inches down into this track, the muddy water spurted higher than Miss Hawkins’s head, and mottled her dress with golden spots of clay. She gave a little shriek, and declared that she had never “seen it so at the East.”

The journey seemed a little long to Ralph, who found that the subjects upon which he and Miss Hawkins could converse were few; but Miss Martha was determined to keep things going, and once, when the conversation had died out entirely, she made a desperate effort to renew it by remarking, as they met a man on horseback, “That horse switches his tail just as they do at the East. When I was to Bosting I saw horses switch their tails just that way.”

===Chinese===
拉尔夫发现自己要骑那匹「黄沙母马」——马群里唯一肯「驮双人」的——因此他得让霍金斯小姐坐在身后。若换成汉娜，拉尔夫未必会反对这种「洛钦瓦尔」式的带女士骑法——女士坐在鞍后——可惜玛莎·霍金斯是另一码事。他只有这么点安慰：他陪着霍金斯小姐走，兴许能消一消巴德的怨气。再说了，他也由不得自己。治安官这么安排，安的什么心，他猜不透；反正十二月那个早晨，黄沙母马驮着他上礼拜堂去，玛莎·霍金斯坐在后头。霍金斯小姐不惯这种走法，每逢马蹄陷进弗拉特溪松软的黄泥里、一陷没到膝盖，她便一阵又惊又喜的慌张。

「我们在东部可不这么上教堂，」她说。「东部的泥也没这么深。我在波士顿那阵子——」可她在波士顿那阵子的事，拉尔夫永远没能听全，因为她刚说出「波士顿」仨字，母马一脚踩进了治安官的马蹄先前踩出的一个坑里，那坑里已经灌满了泥水。

母马的蹄子往这坑里陷下一英尺深，泥水蹿得比霍金斯小姐的脑袋还高，在她裙子上溅了一片黄泥的金点子。她小小地尖叫了一声，声言她「在东部可从没见过这阵仗」。

这趟路在拉尔夫觉得有点儿长：他发现，他跟霍金斯小姐能聊的话题实在寥寥。可玛莎小姐打定了主意不让场子冷掉。有一回，话头彻底断了，她做了个绝望的努力来救活它——路上遇见一个骑马的人，她便评点道：「那匹马甩尾巴的样子，跟东部的马一模一样。我在波士顿那阵子，见过的马甩尾巴就是这么个甩法。」

===Original===
What surprised Ralph was to see that Flat Creek went to meeting. Everybody was there—the Meanses, the Joneses, the Bantas, and all the rest. Everybody on Flat Creek seemed to be there, except the old wooden-legged basket-maker.
